                                           iii
Abstract

In the field of coding theory, maximum-distance separable (MDS) codes are a class of codes which
have optimal guarantees for correcting worst-case Hamming errors. Geometrically, such codes corre-
spond to configurations of vectors in k dimensions such that no k vectors are linearly dependent. By
imposing more geometric conditions, one can define a novel family of codes known as higher order
MDS codes. These codes unify a number of concepts in coding theory – such as connecting the re-
coverability guarantees of a real-world distributed storage architecture with fundamental questions
in list-decoding. One application of this theory is that Reed-Solomon codes, as well as many other
polynomial codes, generically achieve list-decoding capacity.

                                                   xii
Chapter 1

Introduction

Since the dawn of information theory, error-correcting codes have been a vital tool for protecting
data from corruption. Error correction is vital in many aspects of the modern world, from storage
media to radio communications. With these technologies continuously being pushed to their limits,
it has become increasingly important to find optimal error-correcting codes, which optimize the
trade-off between their succinctness and error-resistance.
    In this thesis, we1 examine what is perhaps the most commonly-studied class of error-correcting
codes: those which are meant to resist Hamming errors, where symbols are corrupted in-place.2 For
instance, the word “mat” could be become “cat” if there is a Hamming error in the first letter. In
our case, we’ll be examining linear error-correcting codes: our symbols will come from a finite3 field
F, and our code C on n symbols is a linear subspace of Fn .
    Perhaps the most commonly method of visualizing an error-correcting code is to directly visualize
the code as a cloud of points (see Figure 1.1). A strength of this style of illustration is that some of
the most important concepts in coding theory are easy to visualize. For instance, the (logarithmic)
density of the points is the rate of the code, and the minimum distance between any two of these
points is the distance of the code. List decoding is also easy to visual: every ball of a given radius
should only intersect a small number of points in the code.
    However, there is a significant pedagogical drawback of this visual: it does not have the power to
explain why a given code is a good list-decoder. In particular, a linear code C ⊆ Fn is represented
succinctly by its4 generator matrix G ∈ Fk×n , where k is the dimension of C. The elements of
C are all linear combinations of the rows of G. If C has poor distance, then some codeword of C
has a small Hamming weight, but it is not clear from this picture why G generated a code of small
   1 As is customary in mathematics and theoretical computer science, “we” refers to the author and the reader.
   2 Another model which has been widely studied, especially in recent years due to applications in bioinformatics, is

edit or Levenshtein errors where symbols can be completely removed or inserted.
   3 In general, the results in this thesis do not require that F is finite, outside of Chapter 5.
   4 Technically, a code can have many generator matrices, but they are all equivalent up to an invertible linear

transformation.

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                                    2

Figure 1.1: An illustration depicting a error correcting code C ⊆ {a, b, c, . . . , z}4 as a cloud of points.
The codewords are depicted in blue. With the black point corresponding to a received message. The
two codewords with arrows between them are at a distance of 2 apart. The codewords within the
circle around the received message correspond to a particular list-decoding radius.


hamming weight.
   To get around these drawbacks, the “working illustration” of this thesis will instead focus on the
columns of G. In particular, view the columns of G as n vectors v1 , . . . , vn ∈ Fk . See Figure 1.2.
Although this viewpoint may be not as immediately intuitive, we shall gain much in explanatory
power. For instance, by the Singleton bound [Kom53, Jos58, Sin64], we know that the optimal
distance a k-dimensional code with n symbols is n − k + 1, which, most famously, can be attained
by Reed-Solomon codes [RS60]. In our working illustration, codes attainting the Singleton bound,
called maximum-distance separable (MDS) codes, are those with the following property:

                         No k of the vectors v1 , . . . , vn are linearly dependent.

   More generally, if G generates a codeword of small Hamming weight, we can “blame” some subset
of the vectors for having a linear dependence. Conversely, if the vectors v1 , . . . , vn are in “general
position” then C is MDS and has optimal unique decoding guarantees.


1.1      Higher order MDS codes
So far, this story has merely retold well-understood concepts in coding theory. The primary con-
tribution of this thesis is extending this geometric picture to explain optimal error-correcting codes
via other benchmarks, such as codes which achieve list-decoding capacity. In particular, this thesis

presents a novel family of codes called higher order MDS codes which impose additional geometric
criteria on the vectors v1 , . . . , vn . In particular, there is a hierarchy5 of codes MDS(ℓ), where ℓ ≥ 1
controls the complexity of the conditions imposed.
    As an example: we visualize MDS(3) codes in the case that k = 3. The 3-dimensional vectors
v1 , . . . , vn can be visualized as points in the projective plane (Figure 1.3). The MDS condition in
the case in which no three points are collinear. However, the MDS(3) condition further asserts that
the lines through any three pairs of points should not concur (unless all three pairs share a common
point). For instance, although an MDS code only needs linear (in n) field size, an MDS(3) code
requires (in general) quadratic field size (see Chapter 5).
    We now more formally define MDS(ℓ). Given a subset A ⊆ [n], we let VA = span{vi : i ∈ A}.
We say that a code is MDS(ℓ) if for any A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] the quantity

                                              dim(VA1 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ )

is what it generically should be, a concept we coin as the generic intersection dimension. Let’s
unpack this definition a bit. This does not say that the spaces VA1 , . . . , VAℓ are in “general position”
as it often considered in the field of hyperplane arrangements (e.g., [OT92]) –except in the special
case the sets A1 , . . . , Aℓ happen to be disjoint.6 As a nontrivial example, consider k = 5 and ℓ = 3,
   5 Both MDS(1) and MDS(2) correspond to “traditional” MDS codes, while MDS(3) does not. This is due to the fact

that the inclusion-exclusion theorem applies for two vector spaces, but not for three vector spaces. See, for example,
https://mathoverflow.net/questions/17740/is-there-a-version-of-inclusion-exclusion-for-vector-spaces.
   6 Restricting to the case that A , . . . , A are disjoint is what are known as “weak” higher order MDS codes. See
                                   1           ℓ
Chapter 2 for more details.
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                                        5



with

                                               A1 = {1, 2, 3}
                                               A2 = {1, 2, 4}
                                               A3 = {3, 4, 5, 6}.

What do you think dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 ) should be? A reasonable guess is that the quantity should
be 0 because hyperplanes with codimension 2, 2, and 1 should have a dimension 0 intersection in a
5-dimensional space. However, we can observe that VA1 ∩ VA2 = span{v1 , v2 } = VA1 ∩A2 which has
codimension 3. Since VA3 has codimension 1, we have that

                            dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 ) = dim(VA1 ∩A2 ∩ VA3 ) = 1.

   Perhaps surprisingly this type of logic will always have you arrive at the right answer for the
correct dimension of a generic intersection, allowing for a compact formula for the generic intersection
dimension. Further for fixed A1 , . . . , Aℓ , this quantity can be computed in polynomial time via some
surprising connections between this problem and the field of invariant theory. All of these details
are discussed in Chapter 4.


1.2      Big picture
So far, we have explained what higher order MDS codes are and some of their interesting geometric
properties, but we have not discussed why this definition is so important to coding theory. In this
section, we highlight some of the primary areas of coding theory which the theory of higher order
MDS codes help to unify. More thorough discussions of the literature are present in their respective
chapters.

List-decoding.       Perhaps the most striking connection of higher order MDS codes is that they cor-
respond to the parity check matrices of codes with optimal list-decoding guarantees. Geometrically,
consider a code C ⊆ Fn with parity-check matrix H for which there is a message x ∈ Fn with “too
many” distinct c1 , . . . , cℓ close to x. Then, one can argue that dim(HA1 ∩ · · · ∩ HAℓ ) is larger than
what it should be, where Ai is the set of coordinates where x and ci different. Conversely, if some
dim(HA1 ∩· · ·∩HAℓ ) diverges from its generic intersection dimension, then we can directly construct
from the “excess” a list-decoding ball which has too many codewords. This simple idea, proved in
Chapter 4, unified our notion of higher order MDS codes with an alternative list-decoding themed
definition introduced by Roth [Rot21].7 A key takeaway of this is the following:
   7 Although Roth’s work is independent of ours, we introduced the notion of higher order MDS codes a few months

prior to Roth [BGM21].
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                                       6



                          Higher order MDS codes achieve list-decoding capacity.

GM-MDS Theorem and Reed-Solomon Codes. As mentioned previously, a code C has many
different generator matrices, equivalent up to a change of basis. Often, one would like C to have
a basis with as many zero as possible. These locations where the zeros reside are known as zero
patterns. In 2014, The GM-MDS conjecture [DSY14] was posed to classify all possible zero patterns
for Reed-Solomon codes, and in particular that they coincide with what is possible for a random
linear code. This conjecture was notably proved a few years later as the GM-MDS theorem [Lov18b,
YH19b].
   In another surprising connection, we show that higher order MDS codes are precisely the codes
which attain all possible zero patterns (up to a given complexity). In particular, as an important
corollary of the GM-MDS theorem, we have that generic Reed-Solomon codes (i.e., random Reed-
Solomon codes over a sufficiently large field) are higher order MDS codes, and thus also achieve
list decoding capacity! This resolves8 a long line of work initiated by [RW14] in understanding the
list decoding guarantees of random Reed-Solomon codes. It also indirectly resolves the research
program of[ST20] for showing that random Reed-Solomon codes achieve list-decoding capacity. See
Chapter 4 for a more thorough overview of the work on this problem.

MR Tensor Codes.             Another important class of codes which higher order MDS codes are con-
       9
nected to is maximally recoverable tensor codes. In distributed computing, tensor codes are ar-
rangements of servers in a (topological) grid such that each row / column is contained in some
error correcting code. Such codes have been deployed (c.f., [MLR+ 14, GHK+ 17b]). The notion of
maximally recoverable is a class of tensor codes which have optimal recovery guarantees for their
parameters (i.e., they can recover from all possible server losses which are information-theoretically
possible). We show that for a natural choice of parameters, MR Tensor Codes precisely coincide
with higher order MDS codes. See Chapter 3 for more details and further references.


1.3        Outline
In this section, we briefly overview the contents of each chapter of this thesis.

Higher Order MDS Codes. In Chapter 2, we build up the basic theory of higher order MDS
codes. Besides a truly formal definition of higher order MDS codes, we also establish some equivalent
reformulation of the definition which are easier to work with in later chapters. Another important
topic of study in the chapter is the theory of duals of higher order MDS codes. Of note, the dual
  8 In the case of exponential field size, the case of polynomial field size is still open.
   9 In fact, MR Tensor Codes were the original context in which the theory of higher order MDS codes was devel-

oped [BGM21].
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION                                                                           7



of an MDS(3) code is also MDS(3), but this ceases to be true for MDS(4) and above. In those
situations we prove a “weak” duality theorem.

MR Tensor Codes.        In Chapter 3, we prove the relationship between higher order MDS codes
and MR Tensor Codes. In the process, we also give a polynomial-time algorithm for checking if an
erasure pattern for an MR Tensor Code is recoverable in the a = 1, improving on the exponential-
time criteria of [GHK+ 17a]. We also discuss some of the challenges in extending the results in
[GHK+ 17a] to the a ≥ 2 case, including conjecturing a criteria for the a = 2 case.

Generic Intersection Dimension, GM-MDS, and List-decoding.                   In Chapter 4, we prove
the most significant results in this thesis. In particular, we prove the connections between the
GM-MDS theorem and list-decoding with higher order MDS codes. The key technical argument
in this section is proving a simple combinatorial formula for generic intersection dimension. We
also develop further connections with fields such as invariant theory, where we prove essentially
that a “non-commutative” variant of the generic intersection dimension problem has the exact same
formula, which further implies through the work of [GGOW16] and others that generic intersection
dimension can be computed in polynomial time.

Field Size of Higher Order MDS Codes.           In Chapter 5, we seek to understand what the optimal
field size is of a higher order MDS code. This includes nearly matching upper and lower bounds
on the field size of such codes, although the upper bounds come from a non-explicit randomized
construction. We also give a few explicit constructions of higher order MDS codes, including an
O(n3 ) construction of an (n, 3)-MDS(3) code.

Generalized GM-MDS Theorem. In Chapter 6, we obtain a deeper understanding of the
GM-MDS theorem. In particular, we abstract the techniques used by Lovett [Lov18a] and Yildiz-
Hassibi [YH19b] to vastly generalize the GM-MDS Theorem. In particular, the generalized theorem
is not restricted to Reed-Solomon or Gabidulin codes, but rather it encompasses nearly any generic
polynomial code. Building off of this, we also prove a Ramsey theorem about higher order MDS
codes, that any MDS codes with sufficiently many columns has a puncturing which is a higher
order MDS code. We also propose an even further generalization called the “Ultimate GM-MDS
Conjecture” which has a couple of equivalent formulations.

Chapter 2

Higher Order MDS Codes

What to find in this chapter:

   • Motivation and formal definition of higher order MDS codes and variants.

   • Elementary properties of higher order MDS codes, including equivalent characterizations.

   • What the duals of higher order MDS codes look like.

Sources:       Nearly all material in this chapter is adapted from [BGM21].


   To begin our formal exploration of higher order MDS codes, we start with some preliminary
definitions.


2.1      Preliminaries
Let F be any field. Let n > k ≥ 1 be integers. For any A ⊂ [n] and a matrix V with columns
v1 , v2 , . . . , vn ∈ Fk , we use V |A to denote the submatrix of V formed by columns {vi : i ∈ A} and
VA to denote the subspace of Fk spanned by these columns.
   A (n, k)-code C is a k-dimensional subspace of Fn . It can either be described using a generator
matrix Gk×n such that C = {GT x : x ∈ Fk } or using a parity check matrix H(n−k)×n such that
C = {y ∈ Fn : Hy = 0}. Note that G and H have full row rank and HGT = 0. The rows of G form
a basis for C and the rows of H form a basis for the dual code C ⊥ . Note that G, H are not uniquely
determined by C, but they are unique up to basis change. Nearly every property about matrices
stated in this paper is stated up to a basis change; thus a code can be identified with its generator
matrix for our purposes.




                                                   11
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                                  12



Proposition 2.1. Let C be a (n, k)-code with generator matrix Gk×n and parity check matrix
H(n−k)×n . Let E ⊂ [n] be an erasure pattern and let E = [n] \ E. The following conditions are
equivalent.

   1. E is correctable i.e. given xE for some unknown x ∈ C, we can recover x.

   2. GE has rank k.

   3. HE has full column rank.

    Therefore a maximal correctable erasure pattern has size n − k. And codes which correct all
erasure patterns of size n − k are called MDS codes.

Definition 2.2. (MDS Code) A (n, k)-code is called an MDS code if it can correct every erasure
pattern of size n − k.

    Reed-Solomon codes are explicit MDS codes and they can be constructed for all k, n over fields
of size O(n) which is tight. We now present several equivalent properties of MDS codes.

Proposition 2.3. Let C be a (n, k)-code with generator matrix Gk×n and parity check matrix
H(n−k)×n . The following conditions are equivalent.

   1. C has distance n − k + 1.

   2. Every erasure pattern of size at most n − k is correctable.

   3. Every k × k minor of G is non-zero.

   4. Every (n − k) × (n − k) minor of H is non-zero.

    This then leads to our definition of higher order MDS codes:

Definition 2.4 (Higher order MDS code (MDS(ℓ))). Let C be an (n, k)-code with generator matrix
Vk×n . For ℓ ≥ 2, we say that C is an MDS(ℓ) code if for all A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊂ [n],

                           dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ ) = dim(WA1 ∩ · · · ∩ WAℓ ),                             (2.1)

where W is a k × n generic matrix.1
   1 Genericity: A generic point X can be thought of either as a symbolic vector, or one can think of it as a point

with entries in an infinite field F which avoids any fixed low-dimensional algebraic variety. If F = R or C, then one
can think of a generic point as something which escapes any measure zero set. In particular, low-dimensional varieties
are measure zero sets. As we shall later see the characteristic of the field does not affect the value of the RHS.
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                          13



2.2      Basics
In this section, we will prove some properties of higher-order MDS codes that we will need. As a
warm-up we show that MDS(1) and MDS(2) are equivalent to MDS.

Proposition 2.5. Let C be an (n, k)-code. The following are equivalent:

 (a) C is MDS.

 (b) C is MDS(1).

 (c) C is MDS(2).

Proof. Note that dim(WA ) = min(|A|, k) for all A ⊆ [n] when W is generic. Thus, MDS(1) is
equivalent to every group of at most k rows being linearly independent. Thus, by Proposition 2.3,
MDS(1) is equivalent to MDS.
   To see that MDS(2) implies MDS, note that the MDS(1) conditions are implied by setting
A1 = A2 . For the other direction, observe that

       dim(WA1 ∩ WA2 ) = dim(WA1 ) + dim(WA2 ) − dim(WA1 + WA2 )
                          = dim(WA1 ) + dim(WA2 ) − dim(WA1 ∪A2 )
                          = min{|A1 |, k} + min{|A2 |, k} − min{|A1 ∪ A2 |, k}            (Genericity of W )
                          = dim(VA1 ) + dim(VA2 ) − dim(VA1 ∪A2 )                               (V is MDS)
                          = dim(VA1 ) + dim(VA2 ) − dim(VA1 + VA2 )
                          = dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ).

Thus, MDS implies MDS(2).

   The following proposition gives an equivalent definition of MDS(ℓ) codes which shall be easier
to work with throughout this thesis. This proposition is proved in Section 2.2.1.

Lemma 2.6. Let V ∈ Fk×n be an (n, k)-MDS code and let ℓ ≥ 2. Let W ∈ Rk×n be a generic real
matrix. Then V is MDS(ℓ) if and only if for all A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] such that |Ai | ≤ k, |A1 |+· · ·+|Aℓ | =
(ℓ − 1)k and A1 ∩ · · · ∩ Ak = ∅, we have that

                         VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ = 0 ⇐⇒ WA1 ∩ · · · ∩ WAℓ = 0,

   The following proposition shows that MDS(ℓ) property is preserved under puncturing and short-
ening of codes. If C is any (n, k) code, the punctured code at position i is an (n − 1, k) code obtained
given by projecting all the codewords of C onto the subset [n] \ {i}. The shortened code at position
i is an (n − 1, k − 1) code obtained by projecting only the codewords x ∈ C for which xi = 0 onto
[n] \ {i}. This Proposition is proved in Section 2.2.2.
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                            14



Proposition 2.7. Let C be an (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) code.

   1. If ℓ ≥ 3, then C is also an MDS(ℓ − 1) code.

   2. If ℓ ≥ 2, then the code C0 obtained by puncturing C at any position is an (n − 1, k)-MDS(ℓ)
      code.

   3. If ℓ ≥ 2, then the code C1 obtained by shortening C at any position is an (n − 1, k − 1)-MDS(ℓ)
      code.


2.2.1     Proof of Lemma 2.6
To prove this lemma, we start with some foundational claims.

Claim 2.11. Let V ∈ Fk×n and W ≤ Fk be a subspace. For any A ⊆ B ⊆ [n],

                              0 ≤ dim(W ∩ VB ) − dim(W ∩ VA ) ≤ |B \ A|.

                    = dim(VB\A ) − dim(VA ∩ VB\A )
                    ≤ |B \ A|.

   Now we prove Lemma 2.6.

Lemma 2.6. Let V ∈ Fk×n be an (n, k)-MDS code and let ℓ ≥ 2. Let W ∈ Rk×n be a generic real

2.3      Duality theory
2.3.1     Weak duality of MDS(ℓ)
A natural conjecture is that for all ℓ ≥ 2, a code C is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) iff the dual code C ⊥ is (n, n − k)-
MDS(ℓ). This is true for ℓ = 2 because MDS(2) is equivalent to the usual MDS, and MDS codes
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                              20



satisfy duality. We will later show that duality also holds for ℓ = 3. But surprisingly, this fails for
ℓ ≥ 4. We exhibit a counterexample in Section 2.3.3.
    As duality of higher-order MDS codes is false in general, we instead prove a weaker form of
duality–the dual of an MDS(ℓ) code satisfies what we call “cycle-MDS(ℓ)” property which is a
weaker form of MDS(ℓ). This result will also imply that the dual of any MDS(3) code is indeed
MDS(3).

Definition 2.14 (cycle-MDS(ℓ)). Call a collection of subsets S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ ⊂ [n] a cycle family if
for all j ∈ [n], the set Tj := {i : j ∈ Si } is an interval modulo ℓ, i.e., Tj = {cj , cj + 1, . . . , dj }
mod ℓ for some cj and dj . If you visualize S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ as subsets of rows of an ℓ × n matrix, then
T1 , T2 , . . . , Tn are the subsets of columns corresponding to S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ .
    Say that an k × n matrix V is (n, k)-cycle-MDS(ℓ) if for any cycle family S1 , . . . , Sℓ ⊂ [n] such
that |Si | ≤ k for all i, |S1 |+· · ·+|Sℓ | ≤ (ℓ−1)k, and S1 ∩· · ·∩Sℓ = ∅, we have that VA1 ∩· · ·∩VAℓ = 0
if and only if it generically holds.

Lemma 2.15. Let C be an (n, k)-MDS(m) code. Then the dual code C ⊥ is an (n, n − k)-cycle-
MDS(m) code.

Proof. In the proof, we crucially use the fact that the generator matrix of any (m, m − 1)-MDS code
is equivalent to a generic (m, m − 1) matrix up to symmetries.2 In particular any (m, m − 1)-MDS
code is also MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 2. This is because there is a unique (m, 1)-MDS code up to symmetries
which is the parity check code (whose generator matrix has a single row of all ones).
    Let Vk×n be the generator matrix of C and let Q(n−k)×n be its parity check matrix. Note that
Q is also the generator matrix for the dual code C ⊥ . Let a = 1, and b = n − k. Let U(m−1)×m be a
generator matrix of an (m, m − 1)-MDS code (and thus also M DS(m)).
   Let S1 , S2 , . . . , Sm ⊆ [n] be a cycle family, each of size at most b = n − k and of total size (m − 1)b
    Tm
and i=1 Si = ∅. To show that Q is (n, b)-cycle-MDS(m), it suffices to show that QS1 ∩· · ·∩QSm = 0
whenever it holds generically.
    For all j ∈ [n], let Tj := {i ∈ [m] : j ∈ Si }. Recall that since S1 , S2 , . . . , Sm is a cycle-MDS
family, we have Tj = {cj , . . . , dj } mod m for some cj , dj or Tj = ∅. Note that |Tj | ≤ m − 1 since

    Construct B1 , B2 , . . . , Bn ⊂ [m] as follows (again indices are considered modulo m):

   A special case of a cycle family is when the sets S1 , . . . , Sℓ are all disjoint.

Definition 2.18 (weak-MDS(ℓ)). Say that an k × n matrix V is (n, k)-weak-MDS(ℓ) if for any ℓ
disjoint subsets S1 , . . . , Sℓ ⊂ [n] such that |Si | ≤ k for all i and |S1 | + · · · + |Sℓ | ≤ (ℓ − 1)k, we have
that VS1 ∩ · · · ∩ VSℓ = 0.

   This notion is the minimal assumption needed of the structure of the code for our first field size
lower bound (Lemma 5.5) to hold.

Proposition 2.19. Suppose C is a (n, k)-cycle-MDS(ℓ) code, then C is also a (n, k)-weak-MDS(ℓ)
code.

Proof. Let V be a generator matrix for C. Let S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ ⊂ [n] be a mutually disjoint family of
subsets such that |S1 | + |S2 | + · · · + |Sℓ | ≤ (ℓ − 1)k. To prove that C is weak-MDS(ℓ), it is enough
to show that VS1 ∩ · · · ∩ VSℓ = 0.
   Clearly S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ is also a cycle family; if we imagine S1 , . . . , Sℓ as subsets of rows of an
ℓ × n matrix, then the corresponding subsets Tj of columns are just singletons. Since S1 , S2 , . . . , Sℓ
are disjoint and |S1 | + |S2 | + · · · + |Sℓ | ≤ (ℓ − 1)k, for a generic k × n matrix W , we should have
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                      23



WS1 ∩ · · · ∩ WSℓ = 0. This is because WS⊥1 , . . . , WS⊥ℓ are generic subspaces (here we are using mutual
disjointness of Si ) such that

                           ℓ
                           X                    ℓ
                                                X                          n
                                                                           X
                                 dim(WS⊥i ) =         (k − |Si |) = kℓ −         |Si | ≥ k
                           i=1                  i=1                        i=1

         Pℓ    ⊥     k
and so    i=1 WSi = F . Therefore, since C is cycle-MDS(ℓ), we should have VS1 ∩ · · · ∩ VSℓ = 0.

   Thus, Lemma 2.15 and Proposition 2.19 together imply that the dual of any (n, k)-MDS(m) code
is a (n, n − k)-weak-MDS(m) code. This observation is useful in proving our field size lower bounds.


2.3.2     MDS(3) duality
It turns out that the cycle-MDS(m) condition is equivalent to the cycle-MDS(m) condition when
m = 3.

Proposition 2.20. Let V be a (n, k)-cycle-MDS(3) code. Then, V is an (n, k)-cycle-MDS(3) code.

Proof. By Lemma 2.6, to prove that V is (n, k) − MDS(3) it suffices to check that for all sets
A1 , A2 , A3 with |Ai | ≤ k, |A1 |+|A2 |+|A3 | = 2k, and A1 ∩A2 ∩A3 = ∅, we have that VA1 ∩VA2 ∩VA3 =
0 if and only if it should happen generically. Since every subset of {1, 2, 3} is either an empty set
or an “interval” modulo 3, we have that A1 , A2 , A3 is a cycle family. Thus, the required condition
holds because V is (n, k)-cycle-MDS(3).

Corollary 2.21. The dual of an (n, k)-MDS(3) code is a (n, n − k)-MDS(3) code.

   As mentioned previously, the dual of an MDS(4) code is not necessarily an MDS(4) code (but it
must be an MDS(3) code).


2.3.3     Counterexample to MDS(ℓ) duality for ℓ ≥ 4
Consider the following matrices over F13 .
                                                                               !
                                           1    1      1   1   1   1   1   1
                                    V =
                                       0 1 2 3 4 5 6 7
                                                        
                                        1 0 0 0 0 0 6 6
                                                        
                                       0 1 0 0 0 0 7 5
                                                        
                                       0 0 1 0 0 0 8 4
                                    ⊥
                                   V =
                                                        
                                                         
                                       0 0 0 1 0 0 9 3
                                                        
                                        0 0 0 0 1 0 10 2
                                                        
                                                        
                                        0 0 0 0 0 1 11 1
CHAPTER 2. HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                           24



One can check that V is MDS(4) (this follows from V being MDS) but V ⊥ is not MDS. For example,
let A1 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5}, A2 = {1, 2, 3, 6, 7}, A3 = {1, 2, 4, 6, 8}, and A4 = {5, 7, 8}. If V ⊥ were a generic
matrix, then VA⊥1 ∩VA⊥2 ∩VA⊥3 ∩VA⊥4 = 0, but one can verify that (5, 4, 3, 2, 8, 0) ∈ VA⊥1 ∩VA⊥2 ∩VA⊥3 ∩VA⊥4 .
   A computer search can also find a number of other counterexamples, such as MDS(8, 4, 4) codes
whose duals are not MDS(4) (c.f., the counterexample in [Rot21]). Another way to see the failure of
duality of MDS(4) is by the lower bound of [KMG21] where they proved that (m = 4, n, a = 1, b = 2)-
MR tensor codes require fields of size Ω(n2 ). As we shall soon see in Chapter 3, constructing
(m = 4, n, a = 1, b = 2)-MR tensor codes is equivalent to constructing (n, n − 2) − MDS(4) code (see
Corollary 3.4). If MDS(4) duality were true, this would be equivalent to constructing (n, 2)−MDS(4)
codes. It is easy to see any (n, 2)-MDS code is also an MDS(4) code. Since there are MDS codes
over linear size fields (Reed-Solomon codes), this would violate the lower bound of [KMG21].

Remark 2.22. The work of Roth [Rot21] proves analogous duality results for his list-decoding variant
of higher order MDS codes. The results of Chapter 4 will show our duality results are (essentially)
equivalent.
Chapter 3

Maximally Recoverable Tensor
Codes

What to find in this chapter:

   • Definition of a maximally recoverable tensor code.

   • Formal relationship between higher order MDS codes and maximally recoverable tensor codes
      in the “a = 1” case.

   • Algorithmic characterization of maximally recoverable tensor code patterns in the a = 1 case.

   • Insights and challenges in the a ≥ 2 case.

Sources:    Nearly all material in this chapter is adapted from [BGM21]. The final section is based
on unpublished work with Sivakanth Gopi [BG22].


   In the previous chapter, we defined what a higher order MDS code is with some basic properties.
We now connect the theory of higher order MDS codes to the study of maximally recoverable tensor
codes, which are useful in the field of distribution storage.


3.1     Introduction
In distributed storage, data is stored in individual servers each with a few terabytes of capacity.
A large datacenter can have millions of such servers holding a few exabytes (millions terabytes)
of data. Costs for building and running such datacenters run into billions of dollars. In such a
large system, hard disks crash every minute. Servers can also become temporarily unavailable due
to system updates or network bottlenecks. To avoid data loss and to serve user requests with low

                                                  25
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                        26



latency, some form of redundancy is necessary. Replicating data multiple times is too wasteful,
doubling or tripling the costs. Erasure coding has been used to improve the storage efficiency while
maintaining data reliability. For example a (k + h, k)-Reed-Solomon code can be used to add h
redundant servers to every k data servers. This allows us to correct any h erasures (node failures).
To improve storage efficiency, one is forced to choose large values of k. But this creates problems
with latency, to recover a single erased node, one needs to read data from k other servers. When k
is large, this is prohibitively slow. To balance this tension between storage efficiency and latency,
erasure codes with locality were introduced in [GHSY12, SAP+ 13]. These codes allow fast recovery
of an erased symbol by reading a small number of other unerased coordinates, this ability is referred
to as locality. This requires that each coordinate of the code participates in a parity check equation
involving a few other symbols. In addition to these local parity checks, the code also satisfies a small
number of global parity checks which give it resilience to tolerate a large number of erasures in the
worst case. Such codes with different architectures were deployed in practice to reduce the storage
overhead while maintaining low latency and high durability [HSX+ 12, MLR+ 14].
   The key notion of maximal recoverability for such local codes was introduced in the works [CHL07,
GHSY12]. Maximal recoverability refers to the optimality of a code with respect to its ability to
correct every erasure pattern that is information theoretically possible to correct given the code
architecture (or topology). Therefore maximally recoverable (MR) codes have the best durability
among all the codes with that particular architecture. In a seminal paper, Gopalan et al. [GHK+ 17a]
generalized and brought under a common framework various code topologies used in erasure coding,
by introducing MR codes with grid-like topologies. Here the topology is specified by Tm×n (a, b, h).
This means that the codewords are m × n matrices where each column satisfies a parity check
equations and each row satisfies b parity check equations. In addition there are h global parity
check equations that all the mn symbols satisfy. It is easy to show the existence of MR codes
for any given topology over exponentially large fields by using randomization and Schwartz-Zippel
lemma [GHK+ 17a]. There are two main questions about these codes which are still wide open:

Question 3.1. What are the erasure patterns that are correctable by an MR code with topology
Tm×n (a, b, h)?

Question 3.2. What is the minimum field size required to construct an MR code with topology
Tm×n (a, b, h)? In particular, can we get explicit constructions over small fields?

   Both questions are really important. Knowing which patterns are correctable allows to design
the topology which gives the desired durability while minimizing the storage costs. And explicit
constructions over small fields are important for them to be useful in practice. The amount of
computation needed for encoding and recovering from erasures is very sensitive to the field size over
which the code is defined. Typically, field sizes of 28 to 216 are used in practice. Fields which are
much bigger incur a large computational overhead and therefore infeasible to use in practice.
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                      27



   When h = 0, codes with topology Tm×n (a, b, 0) are pure tensor codes (also called product codes),
i.e., the code is a tensor product of a column code and a row code. In this paper, we will denote
a code with this topology as an (m, n, a, b)-tensor code. Let us denote the row code by denoted by
Crow , which is a (n, n − b)-code. And denote the column code by Ccol , which is a (m, m − a)-code.
Then the tensor code is C = Ccol ⊗Crow , the codewords are m×n matrices where each row belongs to
Crow and each column belongs to Ccol . The setting h = 0 is already very interesting for the following
reason. In a recent work [HPYWZ21], it was shown that the set of erasure patterns correctable by
an MR code with topology Tm×n (a, b, h) are precisely those obtained by adding h more erasures
arbitrarily to erasure patterns correctable by an MR code with topology Tm×n (a, b, 0). Therefore
answering Question 3.1 for h = 0 is enough to answering it for any h. Moreover, if one can construct
an explicit MR code with topology Tm×n (a, b, 0) over Fq , then one can get an explicit MR code
with topology Tm×n (a, b, h) over fields of size q (m−a)(n−b) [HPYWZ21], which also partially answers
Question 3.2.
   In this chapter, we will focus on (m, n, a, b)-MR tensor codes in the special case of a = 1, i.e.,
there are no global parity checks and all the columns satisfy a single parity check equation. Firstly,
this setting is very much practically relevant. The f4 storage architecture of Facebook [MLR+ 14]
(now Meta) uses an (m = 3, n = 14, a = 1, b = 4)-tensor code, though they couldn’t obtain an MR
construction in their implementation. They simply use a tensor product of (14, 10)-Reed-Solomon
code with a (3, 2)-parity check code, which need not be MR and therefore doesn’t have the optimal
durability. Moreover, as we will see, constructing MR tensor codes even in this special case of a = 1
is quite challenging and leads to some really interesting generalization of MDS (Maximum Distance
Separable) codes.


Previous work
Correctable Patterns:        In the paper where they introduce MR tensor codes, [GHK+ 17a] char-
acterize the set of correctable erasure patterns by an (m, n, a, b)-MR tensor code when a = 1 in
terms of a combinatorial condition called ‘regularity’. But they don’t give an efficient procedure to
check if an erasure pattern is regular, naively it would require checking an exponential number of
constraints. They also conjectured that regularity characterizes correctable erasure patterns when
a > 1. But this conjecture is false as shown in [HPYWZ21], we will later present a counterexam-
ple with an illuminating explanation of why regularity fails to capture correctability when a > 1.
Currently, we do not have any characterization of correctable erasure patterns by an (m, n, a, b)-MR
tensor codes when a, b > 1. A subset of correctable erasure patterns in MR tensor codes when a = 2
were obtained in [SRLS18].

Constructions:   If we instantiate the row code and column code with random codes over fields
                                  mn
                                        
of size q ≫ (an + bm − ab) · an+bm−ab     , by Schwartz-Zippel lemma and union bound, we can
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                   28



conclude that with high probability the tensor code will be MR. By doing a more careful union
bound, [KMG21] show that there exists (m, n, a = 1, b)-MR tensor codes over fields of size q =
            
Om,b nb(m−1) . In some special cases, (m = 4, n, a = 1, b = 2) and (m = 3, n, a = 1, b = 3),
[KMG21] proved the existence of MR tensor codes over fields of size q = O(n5 ). These are discussed
in more detail in Chapter 5.

Lower bounds:       Prior to our work there are no general lower bounds on the field size required
for MR tensor codes. In the special case of (m = 4, n, a = 1, b = 2)-MR tensor code, [KMG21]
prove a quadratic lower bound on the field size, i.e., q = Ω(n2 ). For codes with topology given by
                                                                              
Tn×n (a = 1, b = 1, h = 1), [GHK+ 17a] prove a lower bound of exp Ω log(n)2 . This was improved
by [KLR19], where they proved the optimal field size is q = exp(O(n)). We shall improve on some
of these lower bounds in Chapter 5.

MR Local Reconstruction Codes MR codes with topology Tm×n (a, 0, h) are called MR Local
Reconstruction Codes (MR LRCs).1 There is extensive body of work on MR LRCs. Several explicit
constructions of MR LRCs over small fields are given in [GHJY14, GYBS17, GJX20, MK19, GGY20,
Bla13, TPD16, HY16, GHK+ 17a, CK17, BPSY16, MK19, GJX20, CMST20, Mar20]. A construction
of an MR LRC with field size
                                                               
                                                     min{h,m−a}
                                    q ≤ O (max{m, n})

is given in [GG20, CMST20] using skew polynomials. See the prior work section in [GG20] for a
survey of existing results on MR LRCs. A lower bound of
                                                             
                                        q ≳h,a nmmin{a+1,h−1}

on the field size is shown in [GGY20]. The upper and lower bounds match for the setting where
n = m, and αm ≥ a ≥ h − 2 for some constant α < 1, showing that the optimal field size is Θh (nh )
in this case. Closing the gap between upper and lower bounds for general setting of parameters is a
major open problem.


3.2     Basics
3.2.1    Equivalence between MR tensor codes with a = 1 and MDS(ℓ)
The following lemma shows that MDS(ℓ) codes are intimately connected to MR Tensor codes with
a = 1 or b = 1.
  1 MR LRCs are also called Partial-MDS (Maximum Distance Separable) codes in prior works.
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                          29



Lemma 3.3. Let C = Ccol ⊗ Crow be an (m, n, a = 1, b)-Tensor Code where Ccol is a parity check
code and Crow is an (n − b, n)-MDS code. Let E ⊂ [m] × [n] be a maximal erasure pattern i.e.
|E| = mn − (m − 1)(n − b) and suppose that each row has at least b erasures. Let A1 , . . . , Am ⊂ [n]
such that ∪m
           i=1 {i} × Ai = E. Then E is correctable iff dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAm ) = 0.

Proof. The conditions on E translate to the following conditions on A1 , A2 , . . . , Am .

  1. |Ai | ≤ n − b
       P
  2.     i |Ai | = (m − 1)(n − b).

Since Ccol is a simple parity check code, each column of C sum to zero. Let Vb̄×n be a generator
matrix for Crow where b̄ = n − b. The following statements are equivalent.

  1. E is not correctable.

  2. By definition of correctability, there exists a non-zero codeword of C whose support is a subset
       of E.

  3. From the code being a tensor, there exist r1 , r2 , . . . , rm ∈ Crow , not all zero, such that

           • supp(ri ) ⊂ Ai for i ∈ [m],
             Pm
           •   i=1 ri = 0.

       Since ri = yiT V for some yi ∈ Fb̄ , we have the following equivalent statement.

  4. There exist y1 , y2 , . . . , ym ∈ Fb̄ , not all zero, such that

           • yiT VAi = 0 for i ∈ [m],
             Pm
           •    i=1 yi = 0.

       Since yi ∈ VA⊥i for each i ∈ [m], we have the following equivalent statement.
                                                              Pm
  5. There exists yi ∈ VA⊥i , not all zero, such that           i=1 yi = 0.
       Since |Ai | ≤ n − b, and V is a generator matrix of an MDS code, we have dim(VA⊥i ) =
       (n − b) − dim(VAi ) = (n − b) − |Ai |. Therefore

                    m

                                         X

                    i=1                  i=1


       So we have the following equivalent statement.

  6. VA⊥1 + VA⊥2 + · · · + VA⊥m ̸= Fb̄ .

  7. By taking the dual, VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAm ̸= 0.

This completes the proof.

Corollary 3.4. Let C = Ccol ⊗ Crow be an (m, n, a = 1, b) tensor code. Let Ccol be the parity check
code. Then C is an MR tensor code iff Crow is (n, n − b) − MDS(m).

Proof. If Crow is MDS(m), then by Lemma 3.3, C is MR. The other direction follows from Lemma 3.3
and Lemma 2.6.

   In the style of Lemma 3.3, we prove a similar but more intricate lemma which captures the case
a ≥ 2. Recall we have a ≤ m and b ≤ n. This result is used to prove the weak duality of higher-order
MDS codes.

3.3     Efficient regularity testing for MR tensor codes when
        a=1
In this section, we will assume that a = 1. Therefore WLOG, we can assume that Ccol is a parity
check code. In this case, we have a neat characterization of the generically correctable patterns in
terms of regularity.
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                        32



3.3.1     Characterizing correctable patterns: regularity
Definition 3.6 (Regular pattern, [GHK+ 17a]). An erasure pattern E ⊂ [m] × [n] is regular for an
(m, n, a, b)-Tensor Code if for every S ⊂ [m] of size at least a and T ⊂ [n] of size at least b, we have

                                      |E ∩ (S × T )| ≤ sb + ta − ab                                (3.5)

where s = |S| and t = |T |.

Remark 3.7. We can rewrite the regularity condition (3.5) as:

                                     |E ∩ (S × T )| ≥ (s − a)(t − b)                               (3.6)

   We will first prove that correctability implies regularity for any value of a, b. We will need the
following key lemma.

Lemma 3.8. If U1 , U2 are subspaces of U and V1 , V2 are subspaces of V , then

 dim(U1 ⊗ V1 + U2 ⊗ V2 ) = dim(U1 ) · dim(V1 ) + dim(U2 ) · dim(V2 ) − dim(U1 ∩ U2 ) · dim(V1 ∩ V2 ).

Proof. It is enough to show that (U1 ⊗ V1 ) ∩ (U2 ⊗ V2 ) = (U1 ∩ U2 ) ⊗ (V1 ∩ V2 ). The rest follows
from the fact that for any two spaces A, B, dim(A + B) = dim(A) + dim(B) − dim(A ∩ B) and
dim(A ⊗ B) = dim(A) · dim(B). We will show that the duals of both sides are equal. We use the
fact that for any subspaces A and B, we have that A⊥ + B ⊥ = (A ∩ B)⊥ .

                                       ⊥
              ((U1 ⊗ V1 ) ∩ (U2 ⊗ V2 )) = (U1 ⊗ V1 )⊥ + (U2 ⊗ V2 )⊥
                                           = (U1⊥ ⊗ V + U ⊗ V1⊥ ) + (U2⊥ ⊗ V + U ⊗ V2⊥ )
                                           = (U1⊥ ⊗ V + U2⊥ ⊗ V ) + (U ⊗ V1⊥ + U ⊗ V2⊥ )
                                           = (U1⊥ + U2⊥ ) ⊗ V + U ⊗ (V1⊥ + V2⊥ )
                                           = (U1 ∩ U2 )⊥ ⊗ V + U ⊗ (V1 ∩ V2 )⊥
                                           = ((U1 ∩ U2 ) ⊗ (V1 ∩ V2 ))⊥ .

Theorem 3.9 (Correctability ⇒ Regularity [GHK+ 17a]). If an erasure pattern E is correctable by
an (m, n, a, b)-tensor code, then it is regular.

Proof. By Proposition 2.1, E is correctable iff dim((U ⊗ V )E ) = (m − a)(n − b). Let S ⊂ [m] be of
size at least a and T ⊂ [n] be of size at least b. We can upper bound dim((U ⊗ V )E ) as:

                  dim((U ⊗ V )E ) ≤ dim((U ⊗ V )E∩(S×T ) ) + dim((U ⊗ V )E\(S×T ) )
                                   ≤ |E ∩ (S × T )| + dim((U ⊗ V )([m]×[n])\(S×T ) ).
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                              33




Figure 3.1: The blue tiles represent non-erasures. This pattern for (m, n, a, b) = (5, 5, 2, 2) found
by [HPYWZ21] refutes the regularity conjecture of [GHK+ 17a]. In fact, this counterexample is the
smallest one.

We now use Lemma 3.8 to calculate dim((U ⊗ V )([m]×[n])\(S×T ) ).

  dim((U ⊗ V )([m]×[n])\(S×T ) ) = dim((U ⊗ V )S×[n] + (U ⊗ V )[m]×T )
                                   = dim(US ⊗ V[n] + U[m] ⊗ VT )
                                   = dim(US ) · dim(V[n] ) + dim(U[m] ) · dim(VT ) − dim(US ) · dim(VT )
                                   = (m − s)(n − b) + (m − a)(n − t) − (m − s)(n − t).

Combining the above, we get |E ∩ (S × T )| ≥ (s − a)(t − b).

   The following theorem shows that every regular pattern is correctable if a = 1 or b = 1.

Theorem 3.10 ([GHK+ 17a]). An erasure pattern E ⊂ [m] × [n] is generically correctable for an
(m, n, a = 1, b)-tensor code iff E is regular.

Remark 3.11. [GHK+ 17a] conjecture that regularity is equivalent to correctability for arbitrary a, b.
This conjecture was recently disproved by [HPYWZ21]. For their counterexample (c.f., Figure 3.1),
they consider m = n = 5, a = b = 2 and let the complement of the erasure pattern be

                    E = {(1, 1), (2, 2), (2, 3), (3, 2), (3, 3), (4, 4), (4, 5), (5, 4), (5, 5)}.

E is a regular pattern which is not correctable. Our analysis gives a short proof of noncorrectability.
By Proposition 2.1, E is correctable iff dim((U ⊗ V )E ) = (m − a)(n − b) = 9. Because dim(U{2,3} ∩
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                              34



U{4,5} ) ≥ dim(U{2,3} ) + dim(U{4,5} ) − 3 = 1 and likewise dim(V{2,3} ∩ V{4,5} ) ≥ 1, by Lemma 3.8,
we have that
                        dim(U{2,3} ⊗ V{2,3} + U{4,5} ⊗ V{4,5} ) ≤ 4 + 4 − 1 = 7,

Thus, dim((U ⊗ V )E ) ≤ 8, proving that E is regular but not correctable.


3.3.2    Efficiently checking regularity
Let E ⊂ [m] × [n] be any pattern for a (m, n, a, b) tensor code. For all i ∈ [m], let degE (i) be the
number of j ∈ [n] such that (i, j) ∈ E. Likewise, for all j ∈ [n], define degE (j) to be the number of
i ∈ [m] such that (i, j) ∈ E. Let f : [n] → Z≥0 be supply constraints and g : [m] → Z≥0 be demand
constraints. We define a (g, f )E -quasi-matching (c.f., Definition 2 of [BBJ12]) to be a subset of the
edges E ′ ⊂ E such that all supply/demand constraints are met:

  1. For all i ∈ [m], degE ′ (i) ≥ g(i).

  2. For all j ∈ [n], degE ′ (j) ≤ f (j).

We shall use the following Hall-like condition for testing if a quasi-matching exists:

Lemma 3.12 (Theorem 20 of [BBJ12]). There does not exist a (g, f )E -quasi-matching if and only
if there exists a Hall-blocker, that is a U ⊆ [m] with
                               X              X
                                     g(i) >           min(f (j), degE∩(U ×[n]) (j))
                               i∈U            j∈[n]


Definition 3.13. Fix V ⊂ [n] and let EV := E ∩ ([m] × V ). For all i ∈ [m] define the excess
e(i) = max(degE (i) − b, 0). For all j ∈ [n] define a(j) := a. Define a V -excess flow E ′ ⊆ E to be an
(e, a)EV -quasi-matching.
   We say that a E is excess-compatible if for all V ⊂ [n] of size b̄ = n − b, there exists a V -excess
flow EV′ ⊂ EV .

   See also Construction II.3 in [SRLS18] for a similar notion in the literature.

Theorem 3.14. E is regular if and only if E is excess-compatible.

Proof. We proceed by showing both directions.

Regularity implies excess-compatibility.                 First, assume E is regular but not excess-compatible.
Thus, there exists V ⊂ [n] of size b̄ such that EV := E ∩ ([m] × V ) lacks a V -excess flow. For any
U ⊆ [m], let N (U ) be the set of j ∈ [n] for which there is i ∈ U for which (i, j) ∈ E. By Lemma 3.12,
there exists a Hall-blocker U1 ⊂ [m] and neighborhood V1 := N (U1 ) ∩ V for which demand exceeds
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                                    35



supply. That is, if we let E1 = E ∩ (U1 × V1 ) then
                                   X               X
                                          e(i) >          min(a, degE1 (j)).                                  (3.7)
                                   i∈U1            j∈V1


We may assume without loss of generality that e(i) ≥ 1 for all i ∈ U1 –deleting any exceptions would
keep the LHS the same and perhaps decrease the RHS. In particular, e(i) + b = degE (i) for all
i ∈ U1 . We seek to show a contradiction by proving that n − |V | ≥ b.
   Let V2 ⊂ V1 be the vertices j ∈ V1 for which degE1 (j) < a. Let V3 = V1 ∪ ([n] \ V ). Note that
V3 \ V1 = [n] \ V. Thus, since E is regular,

          |U1 |b + |V3 \ V2 |a − ab ≥ |E ∩ (U1 × (V3 \ V2 ))|
                                  = |E ∩ (U1 × V3 )| − |E ∩ (U1 × V2 )|
                                     X              X
                                  =     degE (i) −      degE∩(U1 ×V2 ) (j)
                                      i∈U1                 j∈V2

                                                                       (no edges from U1 to V \ V1 = [n] \ V3 )
                                      X                    X
                                  =          degE (i) −           degE1 (j)
                                      i∈U1                 j∈V2

                                                (extra vertices on right side do not change right-degrees)
                                      X                   X
                                  =          (b + e(i)) −     degE1 (j)
                                      i∈U1                  j∈V2
                                                 X                             X
                                  > |U1 |b +           min(a, degE1 (j)) −            degE1 (j)          (by (3.7))
                                                j∈V1                           j∈V2

                                  = |U1 |b + |V1 \ V2 |a                                      (by definition of V2 )

Therefore since V2 ⊂ V1 , a(|V3 \ V1 | − b) = a(n − |V | − b) > 0, which contradicts that n − |V | = b.

Excess-compatibility implies regularity. Second, assume E is excess-compatible. We show by
induction on the size of U ⊆ [m] that for all S ⊆ [n], with |S| ≥ b, we have that

                                   |E ∩ (U × S)| ≤ |U |b + |S|a − ab.

The base case of U = ∅ is trivial as the LHS equals 0 for all S. For nontrivial U we may assume for
all i ∈ U that degE (i) ≥ b + 1. Otherwise, let U ′ ⊂ U be the set of all i with degE (i) ≥ b + 1 and
note that for all S ⊆ [n] of size at least b, we have by the induction hypothesis that

                            |E ∩ (U × S)| ≤ |E ∩ (U ′ × S)| + b|U \ U ′ |
                                               ≤ |U ′ |b + |S|a − ab + b|U \ U ′ |
                                               ≤ |U |b + |S|a − ab,
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                           36



as desired.
   Thus, we may now assume that degE (i) ≥ b + 1 for all i ∈ U . Observe that the condition
|E ∩ (U × S)| ≤ |U |b + |S|a − ab. is equivalent to.

                                     X
                                      (degE∩(U ×[n]) (j) − a) ≤ |U |b − ab.
                                     j∈S

   Note that the RHS is independent of S, and each term on the LHS is an independent contribution
for each j ∈ S. Therefore, the worst-case choice of S is one of the following:

  1. S is the set of all j ∈ [n] with degE∩(U ×[n]) (j) > a if this set has size at least b.

  2. Otherwise, S is the set of b vertices j ∈ [n] which are the largest with respect to degE∩(U ×[n]) (j).

   In either case, to apply the excess compatibility condition, let V ⊂ [n] be the n − b vertices
with lowest degree with respect to E ∩ (U × [n]). Let T be the set of vertices j ∈ [n] such that
degE∩(U ×[n]) (j) > a. Observe that in case (1), we have that S = T and in case (2), we have that
S = [n] \ V.
   By the contrapositive of Lemma 3.12, we have that
                               X                X
                                     e(i) ≤           min(a, degE∩(U ×V ) (j))
                               i∈U              j∈V
                                               X
                                           =          min(a, degE∩(U ×[n]) (j))
                                               j∈V
                                                X
                                           =            degE∩(U ×[n]) (j) + a|V ∩ T |.
                                               j∈V \T

                                           = |E ∩ (U × (V \ T ))| + a|V ∩ T |.

   Also note that
                                       X                X
                                               e(i) =         (degE (i) − b)
                                       i∈U              i∈U

                                                     = |E ∩ (U × [n])| − b|U |.

   Combining the two equation blocks, we have that

              |E ∩ (U × S)| = |E ∩ (U × [n])| − b|U | − |E ∩ (U × ([n] \ S))| + b|U |
                              X
                            =    e(i) − |E ∩ (U × ([n] \ S))| + b|U |
                               i∈U

                            ≤ |E ∩ (U × (V \ T ))| + a|V ∩ T | − |E ∩ (U × ([n] \ S)| + b|U |.

   We split the remaining analysis into the two cases.
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                     37



  1. In this case, S = T . Since |S| ≥ b and consists of the largest degrees, we have that |V \ T | =
     [n] \ S and |S \ V | = b. Therefore,

              |E ∩ (U × S)| ≤ |E ∩ (U × (V \ T ))| + a|V ∩ T | − |E ∩ (U × (V \ T ))| + b|U |
                            = a|V ∩ S| + b|U |
                            = a|S| + b|U | − a|S \ V |
                            = a|S| + b|U | − ab.


  2. In this case, S = [n] \ V and |S| = b. Therefore,

              |E ∩ (U × S)| ≤ |E ∩ (U × (V \ T ))| + a|V ∩ T | − |E ∩ (U × ([n] \ S)| + b|U |
                             = a|V ∩ T | − |E ∩ (U × (V ∪ T ))| + b|U |
                                X
                             =       (a − degE∩(U ×[n]) (j)) + b|U |
                               j∈V ∩T

                             ≤ b|U |
                             = b|U | + ab − ab
                             = b|U | + a|S| − ab,

     where the fourth line follows from the definition of T .

   By the regularity theorem of [GHK+ 17a] (Theorem 3.10), regularity is equivalent to correctability
of erasure patterns when a = 1. Since Theorem 3.14 shows that regularity is equivalent to excess-
compatibility, we have the equivalence between all three notions.

Corollary 3.15. If a = 1, an erasure pattern E is excess-compatible iff E is generically correctable.

   We will now prove that excess-compatibility of an erasure pattern can be reduced to a max flow
problem which can be solved in polynomial time. This implies that correctability of an erasure
pattern by an (m, n, a = 1, b)-MR tensor code can be checked in polynomial time.

Proposition 3.16. Excess-compatibility of an erasure pattern is testable in time

                                       O(min( m
                                                n            3
                                              a , b ) · (m + n) ).


Proof. We will prove that excess compatibility can be checked in the given time using a max flow
algorithm.
   Let E ⊂ [m] × [n] be the pattern we wish to test excess-compatibility. Fix a subset V ⊂ [n] of
size b̄ = n − b. Construct a directed graph on m + b̄ + 2 nodes: a source s, a sink t, ci for i ∈ [m]
and dj for j ∈ V . For each i ∈ [m], have a (directed) edge from s to ci with capacity e(i). For all

(i, j) ∈ E ∩ ([m] × V ), have an edge from ci to dj with capacity 1. For all j ∈ V , have an edge

V -excess flow E ′ ⊂ E ∩ ([m] × V ) exists if and only if the maximum flow in our constructed directed
graph saturates every edge from s to ci . This maximum flow can be computed in O(m + n)3 time
(e.g., the Relabel-To-Front algorithm in [CLRS22] runs in O((number of vertices)3 ) time). Since we
need to perform this check for all V ⊂ [n] of size b̄, the total running time is O( nb (m + n)3 ). Since

3.4     Toward understanding the a ≥ 2 case.
In this section, we discuss the primary challenges toward understanding maximally recoverable
tensor codes in the regime where a ≥ 2. As previously mentioned, [GHK+ 17a] conjectured that
their characterization of correctable patterns for a = 1 also extends to a ≥ 2, but this was later
disproved by [HPYWZ21].
   We start by observing a peculiar property of the a ≥ 2 case that maximal recoverability is a joint
property of the codes U and V we tensor together. That is, there is no “universal” higher order
MDS property P such that if both U and V have property P then U ⊗ V is maximally recoverable.
After that, we present some novel inequalities for a = 2 case that we conjecture are complete and
discuss (even if correct) why they cannot extend to a ≥ 3.
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES                                                                     39



3.4.1      Maximal recoverability does not factor when a ≥ 2
In this section, we assume that F is an algebraically closed field. Let U be an (m, m − a) symbolic
matrix over F and V be an (n, n − b) symbolic matrix over F.
    Observe that the condition that U ⊗ V is an MR tensor code can be described2 by a single
polynomial hM R ∈⊆ F[U, V ] such that hM R (U, V ) ̸= 0 if and only U ⊗ V is an MR tensor code.
    We now make the following observation.

Proposition 3.17. Assume f ∈ F[U ] and g ∈ F[V ] are polynomials such that if f (x) ̸= 0 and g(y) ̸=
0 then h(x, y) ̸= 0. Then, there exist hU ∈ F[U ] and hV ∈ F[V ] such that h(x, y) = hU (x)hV (y).

Proof. In other words h(x, y) = 0 implies f (x)g(y) = 0. Thus, By Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz (e.g.,
[CLO13]), we have that f (x)g(y) ∈ rad⟨hM R ⟩, where the RHS denotes the radical of the ideal
generate by hM R . In particular, this implies that there exists a positive integer r such that hM R
divides into f (x)r g(y)r . Since polynomial rings over algebraically closed fields are PID, we have that
h factors into an x part and a y part.

    Thus, if there is a standalone property for U and V each (e.g., they are higher order MDS codes)
for their tensor product to be MR, then the polynomial hM R must factor into a U -part and a V -part.
We now show this is not the case if a ≥ 2.

Proposition 3.18. For (m, n, a, b) = (4, 4, 2, 2), there does not exist nonzero f ∈ F[U ] and g ∈ F[V ]
such that f (U ) ̸= 0 and g(V ) ̸= 0 then U ⊗ V is an MR tensor code.

Proof. Consider the non-erasure pattern Ē = {(1, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3), (4, 4)}. The condition that this
pattern is full rank is equivalent to
                                                                                   
                                  u1,1 v1,1    u1,1 v1,2    u1,2 v1,1   u1,2 v1,2
                                                                                 
                                 u2,1 v2,1    u2,1 v2,2    u2,2 v2,1   u2,2 v2,2 
                             det                                                  ̸= 0.
                                 u v          u3,1 v3,2    u3,2 v3,1   u3,2 v3,2 
                                  3,1 3,1                                        
                                  u4,1 v4,1    u4,1 v4,2    u4,2 v4,1   u4,2 v4,2

By plugging in some of the variables, we can force the third row to be (1, 0, 0, 0) and the fourth row
to be (0, 0, 0, 1). Then, we are left with

                                    u1,1 u2,2 v1,2 v2,1 − u2,1 u1,2 v1,1 v2,2 ̸= 0.

This condition cannot factor by inspection, and thus the f and g cannot exist by Proposition 3.17

    As such, there is no “universal MDS” property for U and V such that U ⊗ V is MR.
   2 More explicitly, take the product of det(U ⊗ V | ) for all E ⊆ [m] × [n] for which |E| = mn − (m − a)(n − b) and
                                                     Ē
the determinant is generically nonzero

note that k = n − b = 6 in both cases.

   It is not difficult to generalize gid2 to capture natural inequalities which are required for a ≥ 3.
However, such inequalities cannot capture the example erasure pattern in Figure 3.4.
   To see why this pattern is dependent, look at the following spaces:

                      W1 = span{u1 , u2 } ⊗ span{v1 , v2 }
                      W2 = span{u3 , u4 } ⊗ span{v3 , v4 }
                      W3 = span{u5 , u6 , u7 } ⊗ span{v5 , v6 , v7 }
                      W4 = span{ui ⊗ vj : (i, j) ∈ {5, 6, 7} × {5, 6, 7} \ {(5, 5)}}.

   Assume W1 , W2 , W4 are independent. Note that there are nontrivial w1 ∈ W1 ∩ W1 ∩ W3 and
w2 ∈ W2 ∩ W3 . Thus, span{w1 , w2 , W4 } should be 10-dimensional if generic, but they fit in the
9-dimensional space W3 .
   This kind of logic seems rather difficult to capture in a simple dynamic program, and we leave
open even coming up with a plausible set of inequalities for cases like these.
   One may hope that Proposition 3.21 is true in the reverse direction, however there are counterex-
amples. For instance, take (m, n, a, b) = (5, 6, 2, 2) and let A1 = {1, 2, 6}, A2 = {1, 2}, A3 = {3, 4, 6},
A4 = {3, 4} and A5 = {5, 6}. In that case, one can prove that gid2 ([m]) = 1, but the best lower
CHAPTER 3. MAXIMALLY RECOVERABLE TENSOR CODES   45



bound obtained by Proposition 3.21 is 0.
Chapter 4

Generic Intersection Dimension,
GM-MDS, and List-decoding

What to find in this chapter:

   • Equivalence of MDS(ℓ) with GZP(ℓ), a family of codes inspired by the GM-MDS theorem.

   • An explicit formula for generic intersection dimension.

   • Equivalence (up to duality) of MDS(ℓ) with LD-MDS(≤ ℓ − 1).

   • Resolution of the Shangguan-Tamo conjectures [ST20] on the list-decodability of generic Reed-
      Solomon codes.

Sources:   The material in this chapter is adapted from [BGM22].


   In the previous chapter, we connected higher order MDS codes to the study of maximally recov-
erable tensor codes. We now connect the study of higher order MDS codes to theory of list decoding
and the GM-MDS theorem, which are our main results in this thesis.


4.1     Background
The singleton bound states that a (n, k)-code can have distance at most n − k + 1. Codes achieving
this bound are called MDS codes. Reed-Solomon codes [RS60] are an explicit construction of such
codes over fields of size O(n). In particular, they allow us to decode uniquely from up to half the
minimum distance. List decoding was introduced independently by [Woz58, Eli57] to decode from
beyond half the minimum distance. Naturally, we are not guaranteed to decode uniquely. But we



                                                46
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING47



can hopefully return a small list of codewords which are close to a corrupted codeword. We now
define this formally.

Definition 4.1. We say that a (n, k)-code C is (ρ, L)-list decodable if there are at most L codewords
in any Hamming ball of radius ρn.

   We call ρ the list-decoding radius and L the list size. A code with rate R cannot be list decoded
beyond radius 1 − R with polynomial list size (see [GRS12]). Therefore we must have ρ ≤ 1 − R,
this is called list-decoding capacity. A code with rate R which is (1 − R − ϵ, L)-list decodable for
L = L(ϵ) is said to achieve list-decoding capacity. Here ϵ is called the gap to capacity. It is known
that random non-linear codes can achieve list decoding capacity with list size O(1/ϵ) and alphabet
size exp(1/ϵ) (see [GRS12]). It is also known that random linear codes over large enough alphabet
achieve list-decoding capacity [ZP81]. There is also a stronger form of list decoding called average-
radius list-decoding.

Definition 4.2. We say that a (n, k)-code C is (ρ, L)-average-radius list-decodable1 if there doesn’t
exist L + 1 distinct codewords c0 , c1 , . . . , cL ∈ C and y ∈ Fn , such that2

                                                  L
                                             1 X
                                                     wt(ci − y) ≤ ρn.
                                           L + 1 i=0

   Note that if a code is (ρ, L)-average-radius list-decodable then it is also (ρ, L)-list decodable.
The capacity for average case list-decoding is also 1 − R and random linear codes achieve it [ZP81].
   A long line of work exists on constructing explicit codes which achieve list-decoding capacity.
Following an initial breakthrough by [PV05], Folded Reed-Solomon codes of [GR08] were the first
explicit codes to achieve list-decoding capacity, but with list size and alphabet size polynomial in
code length. Further works have reduced the list size to exp(Õ(1/ϵ)) where ϵ is the gap to capacity
[DL12, GX12, GX13, KRZSW18]. Some classes of structured random codes can also be shown to
achieve list decoding capacity. [MRRZ+ 20] show that LDPC codes achieve list decoding capacity.
[GM21] show that puncturings of low-bias codes have good list-decodability.


4.1.1     List-decoding Reed-Solomon codes
Reed-Solomon codes are one of the most popular codes with several applications both in theory and
practice [WB99]. We say that C is a Reed-Solomon code if it has a generator matrix which is a
  1 In some previous works, such as [Rot21] this is referred to as strongly list-decodable.
  2 Here wt(x) is the Hamming weight of x, i.e., the number of non-zero coordinates of x.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING48



Vandermonde matrix. That is, there exists distinct α1 , . . . , αn ∈ F such that
                                                                   
                                          1       1     ···     1
                                       α1        α2    ···     αn 
                                                                    
                                                                    
                                       α2        α22   ···     αn2 
                                       1                                                             (4.1)
                                       .          ..   ..        .. 
                                       .                  .
                                       .           .              . 
                                                                     

                                        α1k−1   α2k−1   ···    αnk−1

is a generator matrix of C. Reed-Solomon codes are MDS (maximum distance separable) codes, i.e.,
they achieve the maximum distance possible for a code with given rate. So naturally, there is a lot
of interest in understanding the list-decodability of Reed-Solomon codes.

Question 4.3. Can Reed-Solomon codes achieve list-decoding capacity?

   Guruswami and Sudan [Sud97, GS98] showed that rate R Reed-Solomon codes can be list-
                       √
decoded from radius 1 − R, which is also coincidentally the Johnson bound for list-decoding
[Joh62]. Whether Reed-Solomon codes can be list-decoded beyond the Johnson bound has been
a topic of intense research. [RW14] are the first to show that random Reed-Solomon codes (i.e.,
when αi are chosen randomly from a large enough field in (4.1)) can be list-decoded beyond the
Johnson bound in some parameter ranges. On the other hand, full length Reed-Solomon codes where
α1 , α2 , . . . , αn are all the field elements (with n = |F|) are not list-decodable with constant list size
                                        √
with list-decoding radius 1 − α R for sufficiently small constant α, R < 1, in fact the list size has
to be at least n2 log(1/α) [BSKR09]. Therefore it is clear that the evaluation points αi have to be
chosen carefully from a large enough finite field to even beat the Johnson bound. In this paper, we
are interested in understanding the list-decoding behavior of generic Reed-Solomon codes. That is,
the limiting behavior of a random choice of α1 , . . . , αn as F tends to infinity. In [ST20], Shangguan
and Tamo made a startling conjecture that generic Reed-Solomon codes don’t just beat the Johnson
bound, but in fact achieve list-decoding capacity! They also conjectured a precise bound on the list
size. In Section 4.4, we fully resolve their conjecture.

Theorem 4.4. Generic Reed-Solomon codes achieve list decoding capacity. If C is a generic (n, k)-
Reed-Solomon code with rate R = k/n, then C is (ρ, L)-list decodable for

                                                          1−R
                                           ρ=1−R−             .                                        (4.2)
                                                          L+1

Moreover, C is also (ρ, L)-average-radius list-decodable for the same ρ.

   Equivalently, Theorem 4.4 shows that a generic Reed-Solomon code of rate R is (1 − R − ϵ, L)-list
decodable with L = 1−R−ϵ
                     ϵ   . The bound (4.2) is the best possible even for non-linear codes [GST22,
Rot21].
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING49



   In Chapter 5, we also show how to turn Theorem 4.4 into a quantitative bound on the field size
required for random Reed-Solomon codes to achieve list-decoding capacity.
                                                                         n
                                                                                                       L+1
Theorem 4.5. Let n, k, L be positive integers and let c(n, k, L) = 2Ln2 ≤n−k                                  . A random
(n, k)-Reed-Solomon code of rate R = k/n over F is (1 − R − 1−R
                                                            L+1 , L)-average-radius list decodable
with probability at least 1 − c/|F|.

in Theorem 4.5.

                                                                                    L
                                                                                       1 − nk .
                                                                                             
MDS(L), denoted by LD-MDS(L), if C is (ρ, L)-average-radius list-decodable for ρ = L+1
In other words, for any y ∈ Fn , there doesn’t exist L + 1 distinct codewords c0 , c1 , . . . , cL ∈ C such
that
