We say5 that C is LD-MDS(≤ L) if it is LD-MDS(ℓ) for all 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ L.

   The list-decoding guarantees of LD-MDS(L) are very strong. In particular, LD-MDS(L) codes
of rate R get ϵ-close to list-decoding capacity when L ≥ 1−R−ϵ
                                                           ϵ   . Note that the usual MDS codes are
LD-MDS(1). [Rot21] showed that LD-MDS(L) property is preserved under duality only for L = 1, 2,
and also gave some explicit constructions of LD-MDS(2) codes.

   ▶ GZP(ℓ).      In many coding theory applications, it is useful to have codes with generator ma-
trices having specified supports in addition to being MDS, see [DSY14] for some such applications.
Dau et al. [DSY14] have made a remarkable conjecture that Reed-Solomon codes over fields of size
q ≥ n + k − 1 can have generator matrices with arbitrary patterns of zeros, as long as the pattern
of zeros do not obviously preclude MDS property by having a large block of zeros. This came to
  5 In general, the notion of LD-MDS(ℓ) is not monotone in ℓ.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING51



be called the GM-MDS conjecture. It was eventually proved independently by Lovett [Lov18a] and
Yildiz and Hassibi [YH19b]. Before we state the GM-MDS theorem, we will make some crucial
definitions.
    Let S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk ) where S1 , . . . , Sk ⊂ [n], we call such an S a zero pattern for k × n
matrices. We say that S has order ℓ if there are ℓ distinct non-empty sets among S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk . We
say that a matrix Gk×n attains the zero pattern S if there exists an invertible matrix Mk×k such
     e := M G has zeros in Sk {i} × Si . Note that G
that G                                                e and G generate the same code. We now
                                    i=1
define the crucial notion of a generic zero pattern.

Definition 4.8 (Generic zero pattern). Suppose S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk ) is a zero pattern for k × n
matrices. We say S is a generic zero pattern if for all I ⊂ [k],

                                                 \
                                                      Si ≤ k − |I|.                                           (4.4)
                                                i∈I


   It is not hard to see that, by Hall’s matching theorem, (4.4) is equivalent to the condition that
                                                Sk
a generic k × n matrix W which has zeros in i=1 {i} × Si (and the rest of the entries of W are
generic), has all k × k minors non-zero. This is because the condition (4.4) ensures that any k × k
submatrix of W has a matching of non-zero entries, and thus ensures non-zero determinant for this
k × k submatrix. (4.4) appeared in [DSY14], where it is called the MDS condition because it is
a necessary condition for a k × n matrix with zeros in S to be MDS. We will now define a new
generalization of MDS codes, which we call GZP(ℓ).

Definition 4.9 (GZP(ℓ)). We say that a (n, k)-code C is GZP(ℓ) if C is MDS 6 and its generator
matrix Gk×n attains all k × n generic zero patterns of order at most ℓ. 7

    Thus, a code C is GZP(ℓ) if we can choose a generator matrix of C to have any order ℓ generic
zero pattern. One can prove that GZP(1) and GZP(2) are equivalent to MDS property, therefore
this is indeed a generalization of the MDS property. We will now state the GM-MDS theorem in
terms of GZP(ℓ) property.

Theorem 4.10 (GM-MDS [DSY14, Lov18a, YH19b]). A generic Reed-Solomon code is GZP(ℓ) for
all ℓ.

    The actual GM-MDS theorem says that for any particular generic zero pattern S, there exists a
Reed-Solomon code over any field of size q ≥ n+k−1 which can attain S. This is a simple consequence
of Theorem 4.10, but we are more interested in GZP(ℓ) codes which simultaneously attain all order
ℓ generic zero patterns. Given how we defined GZP(ℓ), it is not obvious to see why generic matrices
should be GZP(ℓ). One can use the GM-MDS theorem to prove this (Proposition 4.19). An alternate
   6 We explicitly add the MDS condition to avoid degenerate cases like zero matrix being GZP(ℓ).
    7 Note that GZP(ℓ) is a property of the code C, and not a particular generator matrix G used to generate C. This

if because if G attains a generic zero pattern, then M G also attains it for any invertible k × k matrix M.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING52



proof can also be obtained using a combinatorial characterization (called regularity) of correctable
erasure patterns in maximally recoverable tensor codes where the column code is a simple parity
check code [GHK+ 17b].

   Equivalence of higher-order MDS codes. We are now ready to present the most important
theorem of our paper. We show that, surprisingly, all these notions of higher-order MDS codes are
equivalent (up to duality).

Theorem 4.11. The following are equivalent for a linear code C for all ℓ ≥ 1.

 (a) C is MDS(ℓ + 1).

 (b) C ⊥ is LD-MDS(≤ ℓ).

 (c) C is GZP(ℓ + 1).

Proof. (a) iff (b) is proved in Section 4.4 and (a) iff (c) is proved in Section 4.3.

Remark 4.12. One can show that MDS(1), MDS(2), LD-MDS(1), GZP(1), GZP(2) are all equivalent
to MDS (see [BGM21, Rot21]).

Corollary 4.13. Generic Reed-Solomon codes are GZP(ℓ), MDS(ℓ) and LD-MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ.

Proof. The dual of a generic Reed-Solomon code is also a generic Reed-Solomon code (Proposi-
tion 4.33). Therefore, Theorem 4.11 together with the GM-MDS theorem (Theorem 4.10), immedi-
ately implies that generic Reed-Solomon codes are MDS(ℓ) and LD-MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ.

   This immediately implies our main result that generic Reed-Solomon codes achieve list-decoding
capacity (Theorem 4.4).


4.1.3     Proof overview
The proof of Theorem 4.11 has a few key steps, including proving some novel properties of generic
zero patterns as well as solving a generic intersection problem.

Dimension of generic intersections           The core of the proof of Theorem 4.11 is a combinatorial
characterization of the generic intersection dimension.

Theorem 4.14 (Dimension of Generic Intersection). Given A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] of size at most k, for
a generic matrix Wk×n , we have that
                                                                                               
                                                                      X \
                dim(WA1 ∩ · · · ∩ WAℓ ) =         max                             Aj − (s − 1)k    (4.5)
                                            P1 ⊔P2 ⊔···⊔Ps =[ℓ]
                                                                      i∈[s] j∈Pi
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING53



where the maximum is over all partitions of [ℓ]. Note that the result is independent of the character-
istic of the underlying field.

   The proof of Theorem 4.14 appears in Section
                                               4.3.1. Since the RHS of (4.5) has a maximum over
exponentially many terms in ℓ, it gives an exp(Õ(ℓ))k -time algorithm for computing the generic
intersection dimension. In Section 4.7, we give a poly(k, ℓ)-time algorithm to compute the RHS of
(4.5) by reducing it non-commutative rank computation (see Theorem 4.16).
   In literature, one can find many problems that are similar or related to Theorem 4.14 in a range
of subjects like Schubert calculus, intersection theory, matroid theory, representation stability and
homological algebra to name a few. For example, it seems conceivable that there is a matroid-
theoretic description or that there is a formula for the intersection dimension coming from Schubert
calculus and intersection theory. Despite that, it seems very difficult to adapt the techniques from
any of those subjects to say anything meaningful about the problem above, but a more in-depth
analysis from the view-point of any of those subjects could lead to new insights in broader contexts.

A novel characterization of sets in order-ℓ generic zero patterns                 We also show a novel
structural result on order ℓ generic zero patterns. In particular, if S is an order ℓ generic zero pattern
containing sets A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ and say d copies of the empty set. Then by applying (4.4), one can
easily show that for all partitions P = P1 ⊔ P2 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Ps = [ℓ] we have that

                                      s
                                      X    \
                                                 Aj ≤ (s − 1)k + d.                                 (4.6)
                                      i=1 j∈Pi


Surprisingly the converse is also true (see Lemma 4.25). A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ can be used to form an
order ℓ generic zero pattern with d copies of the empty set iff (4.6) holds. The proof involves
an intricate induction, which on a high level is comparable to the induction used to prove Hall’s
matching theorem. In the proof, one identifies the partition P for which the above inequality is
tight (if no such partition exists, then one pads with elements). One can then recursively apply the
induction hypothesis to each portion of the partition, which can then be combined together to show
the result. This result is crucially used to prove that GZP(ℓ) codes are also MDS(ℓ) and to prove
Theorem 4.14.
   The proof of Theorem 4.14 proceeds as follows. Let d be the RHS of (4.5). By Lemma 4.25,
there exists a order ℓ zero pattern S with copies of A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] and d copies of the empty
set. Then, since a generic matrix W is GZP(ℓ) (Proposition 4.19), there is an invertible Mk×k
          f = M W has the zero pattern S. From this, it is straightforward to upper bound the
such that W

lower bound follows from the pigeonhole principle and dimension counting. Note that this proof also
immediately implies that GZP(ℓ) codes are MDS(ℓ) because in the proof of Theorem 4.14, we only
used the GZP(ℓ) property of generic matrices to get the correct dimension.

A generalized Hall’s theorem. One of the key results in [DSY14] is a generalized Hall’s theorem.
For any generic zero pattern S = (S1 , . . . , Sk ) there exists a generic zero pattern S ′ = (S1′ , . . . , Sk′ )
which contains S (i.e., for all i, Si′ ⊇ Si ) such that |Si′ | = k − 1 for all i. However, this theorem does
not preserve order, if S is order ℓ, the order of S ′ can be as large as k (and in fact must equal k).
   In Section 4.2.1, we further generalize the generalized Hall’s theorem from [DSY14]. In particular,
we show that if S is an order ℓ generic zero pattern, then there is an order ℓ generic zero pattern S ′
which contains S such that for the non-empty A1 , . . . , Aℓ which define S ′ , each Ai appears exactly
k − |Ai | times. Such an S ′ is called maximal.
   This new generalized Hall’s theorem is used to prove that MDS(ℓ) codes are also GZP(ℓ). Suppose
C is an MDS(ℓ) code with generator matrix G. Given an order ℓ generic zero pattern S, one
uses our generalized Hall theorem (Theorem 4.23), to find a maximal order ℓ generic zero pattern
S ′ containing S. Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ be the ℓ non-empty sets in S ′ and say S ′ has d copies of the
empty set. Using the MDS(ℓ) property of G and the fact that S ′ satisfies (4.4), we can prove that
dim(GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ ) = d via Theorem 4.14. By taking the dual of this intersection and performing
a dimension-counting argument, one can show that8 (GA1 )⊥ , . . . , (GAℓ )⊥ are linearly independent.
One can then show that bases for these spaces can be put together to build a matrix M such that
M G has the desired zero pattern. For the proof to work, we absolutely need the fact that each
Ai appears exactly k − |Ai | times in the pattern S ′ , which is guaranteed by the generalized Hall’s
theorem.

Equivalence of MDS(ℓ + 1) and LD-MDS(≤ ℓ)⊥ .                  The proof of equivalence mostly follows from
Theorem 4.14. We prove the contrapositive: that C is not MDS(ℓ + 1) iff C ⊥ is not LD-MDS(≤ ℓ).
   Let G be a generator matrix of C, note that G is a parity check matrix for C ⊥ . If C is not
MDS(ℓ + 1), there exists some choice of A1 , . . . , Aℓ+1 for which (4.5) is not satisfied. In fact, one can
assume that the RHS of (4.5) is 0. This implies there is a nontrivial z ∈ GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ+1 which is
not captured by a generic intersection. In particular, for all i ∈ [ℓ], there is ei with supp(ei ) ⊆ Ai
with z = Gei . This is almost enough to prove that C ⊥ is not LD-MDS(ℓ), but some of the ei ’s
may be equal. To get around this, we consider a partition of [ℓ] with two Ai ’s in the same part if
their ei ’s are equal. The resulting inequality arising from using this partition with (4.5) is enough
to prove that C ⊥ is not LD-MDS(ℓ′ ) for some ℓ′ ≤ ℓ.
   Now assume that C ⊥ is not LD-MDS(≤ ℓ), WLOG say C ⊥ is not LD-MDS(ℓ). In particular, this
                                                                                   Pℓ+1
implies that there are distinct e1 , . . . , eℓ+1 for which Ge1 = · · · = Geℓ+1 and i=1 wt(ei ) ≤ ℓk. One
can then let Ai = supp(ei ), and consider the intersection GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ+1 . Using the distinctness
of e1 , . . . , eℓ+1 , one can prove that the dimension of this intersection is strictly greater than the
corresponding generic intersection. This is enough to show that C ⊥ is not MDS(ℓ).
  8 The dual is of the linear space G      k
                                     A1 ⊆ F . We are not taking the dual of the original matrix G.

Generic Gabidulin codes achieve list-decoding capacity

Let α1 , α2 , . . . , αn be linearly independent over some base field Fq . A Gabidulin code has the following
generator matrix:
                                                                         
                                          α1       α2      ···     αn
                                       q
                                       α1         α2q     ···     αnq 
                                                                        
                                       2              2              2
                                                                        
                                       αq        α2q      ···     αnq 
                                       1                               .                                 (4.7)
                                       .          ..      ..       .. 
                                       .                     .
                                       .           .                . 
                                                                        
                                           k−1      k−1             k−1
                                        α1q      α2q       ···    αnq

A generic Gabidulin code is defined by choosing α1 , α2 , . . . , αn generically over a large enough
extension field of Fq . Gabidulin codes are rank metric codes which achieve the rank metric singleton
bound with applications in network coding, space-time coding and cryptography [Gab, Gab21].
GM-MDS theorem was extended to Gabidulin codes over both finite and zero characteristic in
[YH19a, YRH20]. Thus generator of matrices of generic Gabidulin codes over both finite and zero
characteristic also satisfy GZP(ℓ) for all ℓ. Since dual of a generic Gabidulin code is also a generic
Gabidulin code [Gab21], Theorem 4.11 shows that Gabidulin codes are MDS(ℓ) and LD-MDS(ℓ) for
all ℓ. This implies that generic Gabidulin codes have optimal list-decoding guarantees with respect
to the Hamming metric.

Theorem 4.15. Generic Gabidulin codes achieve list-decoding capacity (in the Hamming metric).
In particular, they are (ρ, L)-average-radius list-decodable for

                                                            1−R
                                               ρ=1−R−           .
                                                            L+1

Connections to invariant theory

Theorem 4.14 has connections to invariant theory. Although Theorem 4.14 produces a closed formula
for generic intersection dimension, there are exponentially many (in ℓ, k) partitions to consider.
This can make computing the dimension cumbersome. We give a deterministic9 polynomial-time
algorithm by reducing the intersection dimension to a computation of non-commutative rank of a
suitable linear matrix. The computation of non-commutative rank in polynomial-time is a recent
breakthrough, an analytic algorithm appears in [GGOW16] based on Gurvits operator scaling and
an algebraic algorithm in [IQS18] based on their previous work [IQS17] and the degree bounds in
[DM17]. These results are part of a larger ambitious program of Mulmuley [Mul17] that attempts to
approach central problems in complexity via orbit problems in invariant theory that has seen much
progress over the last decade, see [BFG+ 19] and references there-in. In Section 4.7, we use these
methods to prove the following result.
   9 We remark that a simple randomized polynomial-time algorithm to compute generic intersection dimension is to

randomly sample W over a large enough field and compute the intersection dimension directly by a rank computation
[BGM21].
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING56



Theorem 4.16. Given k, n and A1 . . . Aℓ ⊆ [n], we can compute the generic intersection dimension
dim(WA1 ∩ · · · ∩ WAℓ ) for generic W in poly(k, ℓ)-time.

Remark 4.17. Explicitly computing the formula found by Theorem 4.14 takes exp(Õ(ℓ))k time,
as there are exp(Õ(ℓ)) partitions of [ℓ] (e.g., [DB81]). Thus, Theorem 4.16 is superior when ℓ ≥
polylog(k).


4.2      Generic zero patterns (GZPs)
Recall that a zero pattern S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk ) is called a generic zero pattern if

                                               \
                                                    Si ≤ k − |I|.                                      (4.8)
                                              i∈I


We restate the GM-MDS theorem more explicitly here.

Theorem 4.18 (GM-MDS [Lov18a, YH19b]). Let S1 , . . . , Sk ⊂ [n]. The following are equivalent:

   1. S1 , . . . , Sk satisfy (4.8) for all nonempty I ⊂ [k].

   2. A matrix Wk×n which is generic except that Wi,j = 0 and j ∈ Si has all of its k × k minors
      non-zero.

   3. Let Vk×n be a generic Vandermonde matrix as in (4.1) where (α1 , α2 , . . . , αn ) is generic. Then
      V attains zero pattern S.

   Note that Theorem 4.18 is equivalent to saying that generic Reed Solomon codes are GZP(ℓ) for
all ℓ. We will now show that generic codes are GZP(ℓ).

Proposition 4.19. Generic codes are GZP(ℓ) for all ℓ.

Proof. Let Vk×n be a generic matrix, which is the generator matrix of a generic (n, k)-code. Let
S = (S1 , . . . , Sk ) be a generic zero pattern for k × n matrices, i.e. S satisfies (4.8). We want to
show that there exists some invertible matrix Mk×k such that M V has zeros in ∪i∈[k] {i} × Si . By
the generalized Hall’s theorem (Theorem 4.20), there exists Si′ ⊂ [n] such that Si′ ⊃ Si , |Si′ | = k − 1
and (S1′ , S2′ , . . . , Sk′ ) satisfy (4.8). Therefore, WLOG we can assume that |Si | = k − 1 for all i. Let
v1 , v2 , . . . , vn be the columns of V . Let m1 , m2 , . . . , mk be the rows of M . For M V to have zeros
in i × Si , it must be that ⟨mi , vj ⟩ = 0 for all j ∈ Si . Therefore mi = VS⊥i (up to scaling). Note
that VS⊥i is a one-dimensional space since |Si | = k − 1. Therefore M is completely determined (up
to scaling of rows) by V . Now we just need to prove that det(M ) ̸= 0 where mi = VS⊥i . From (3)
in Theorem 4.18, det(M ) ̸= 0 when we choose V to be a generic Vandermonde matrix. Therefore
det(M ) ̸= 0, when we choose V to be completely generic.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING57



4.2.1      Generalized Hall’s theorem and maximal GZPs
While formulating the GM-MDS conjecture, [DSY14] proved a variant of a Generalized Hall’s The-
orem and used it to show that any generic zero pattern can be extended to a maximal generic zero
pattern.

Theorem 4.20 (Generalized Hall’s Theorem–modern statement [DSY14, Lov18a, YH19b]). Let
S = (S1 , . . . , Sk ) be a generic zero pattern for (n, k)-codes. Then, there exists a generic zero pattern
S ′ = (S1′ , . . . , Sk′ ) such that for all i ∈ [k], |Si′ | = k − 1 and Si ⊆ Si′ .

Remark 4.21. Note that Theorem 4.20 is a generalization of the classic Hall’s theorem about
existence of a bipartite matching when k = n. In this case, if we form a bipartite graph between
[k] and [n] where i ∈ [k] has neighborhood S̄i , (4.8) becomes the Hall’s matching condition that the
neighborhood N (I) of any set I satisfies |N (I)| ≥ |I|.

    If we apply Theorem 4.20 to an order ℓ pattern, the resulting pattern S ′ will not be order ℓ
in general (in fact, it will be order k). To extend an order ℓ generic zero pattern to a maximal
order ℓ generic zero pattern, we need a further generalization of the generalized Hall’s theorem
(Theorem 4.23). First, we state an equivalence.

Proposition 4.22. Assume n ≥ k. Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] of size at most k. The following are
equivalent.

 (a) There exist δ1 , . . . , δℓ ≥ 0. such that for all nonempty I ⊆ [ℓ]
                                                                   X
                                                     |AI | ≤ k −          δi .                                       (4.9)
                                                                    i∈I


                                                                                                         Pℓ
  (b) The pattern (S1 , . . . , Sk ), with δi copies of Ai for i ∈ [ℓ] and additional k −                  i=1 δi copies of
       the empty set, is a generic zero pattern order ℓ.
                                                                           P
Proof. First we prove that (a) implies (b). Let d = k −                          i∈[ℓ] δi .   By (4.9), we know that d is
nonnegative. We need to show that (4.8) holds for (S1 , . . . , Sk ). Let I ⊆ [k] be any non-empty
subset, we want to show that (4.8) holds for I. If I includes at least one i for which Si = ∅, then
(4.8) trivially holds. Further, if I ⊆ [k] includes a nonzero number of copies of Ai , we might as well
include all the δi copies, as the LHS of (4.8) is unchanged and the RHS can only decrease. If I
includes 0 or δi copies of each Ai , then (4.8) is exactly (4.9) for the relevant set of Ai ’s.
    To prove that (b) implies (a), for every I ⊆ [ℓ] for (4.9), look at the subset I ′ ⊆ [k] which includes
δi copies of Ai for each i ∈ I. The truth of (4.9) is then implied by applying (4.8) to I ′ .

    We now state our generalized Hall’s theorem which is a further generalization of Theorem 4.20.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING58



Theorem 4.23 (Generalized Hall’s Theorem (new)). Assume n ≥ k.Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ be subsets of
[n] of size at most k. Assume there exist δ1 , . . . , δℓ ≥ 0 such that for all nonempty I ⊆ [ℓ], (4.9)
holds. Then, there exists A′i ⊇ Ai such that (4.9) holds and for all i ∈ [ℓ], |A′i | = k − δi .

Proof. It suffices to show that if for some i ∈ [ℓ], we have that |Ai | < k −δi , then we can find A′i ⊃ Ai
for which |A′i | = k − δi and replacing A′i with Ai still satisfies (4.9). WLOG, by permutation of the
Ai ’s, we may assume that |A1 | < k − δ1 . Note that if δ1 = 0, we may extend A1 to an arbitrary
superset of size k, Since (4.9) holds for all I with 1 ̸∈ I, adding 1 to I can only decrease the LHS
but not change the RHS. Thus, this is a valid extension.
    We now assume δ1 ≥ 1. By Proposition 4.22, we have that S1 , . . . , Sk , with δi copies of Ai for
each i ∈ [ℓ] and the rest the empty set is a generic zero pattern. For all i ∈ [ℓ], let Ji ⊆ [k] (possibly
empty) be the indices for j for which Sj = Ai . Let T be an arbitrary subset of [n] of size k for which
A1 ⊆ T . Define Ti := Si ∩ T for all i ∈ [k]. Note that (T1 , . . . , Tk ) is a generic zero pattern, as for
each (4.8) the LHS could only decrease going from Si to Ti .
    Note that the “T -complements” T̄i := T \ Ti satisfy the matching conditions of classical Hall’s
theorem (e.g., [VW01]). That is, for any nonempty I ⊆ [k],

                                   [                    \
                                         T̄i = |T | −         Ti ≥ k − (k − |I|) = |I|.

    By recursively applying the argument, we may deduce that each Ai can be extended to a set of
size k − δi .

    If a generic pattern (S1 , . . . , Sk ) contains δi copies of a set Ai , then (4.8) implies that |Ai | ≤ k−δi .
As an immediate corollary of Theorem 4.23 and Proposition 4.22, we can now show that any generic
zero pattern of order ℓ can be extended to a “maximal” generic zero pattern of order ℓ.

Corollary 4.24. Assume n ≥ k. Let (S1 , . . . , Sk ) be a generic zero pattern order ℓ containing δi
copies of nonempty Ai for all i ∈ [ℓ]. Then, there exists A′i ⊇ Ai with |A′i | = k − δi for all i, such
that the zero pattern (S1′ , . . . , Sk′ ) with δi copies of A′i for all i (and the same number of copies of
the empty set) is a generic zero pattern of order ℓ.


4.2.2      A characterization of sets in order-ℓ generic zero patterns
Given subsets A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ ⊂ [n], we are interested in knowing if there exists an order-ℓ generic
zero pattern S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sk ) for k × n matrices which is formed by copies of A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ and
d copies of the empty set. We now give a combinatorial characterization of sets A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ for
which this can be done, based on partitions of the ℓ sets. This characterization is essential for relating
generic zero patterns with the generic intersection problem (see Section 4.3 for its application).

Lemma 4.25. Assume n ≥ k and d ≥ 0. Let A1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] of size at most k. The following
are equivalent.

4.3     Equivalence of GZP(ℓ) and MDS(ℓ)
In this section, we prove both Theorem 4.11 on computing the dimension of a generic intersection
and that MDS(ℓ) is equivalent to GZP(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 1 (and thus that (a) and (c) are equivalent in
Theorem 4.11).


4.3.1    GZP(ℓ) implies MDS(ℓ)
The key result toward proving that GZP(ℓ) implies MDS(ℓ) is the following:
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING63



Lemma 4.26. Let C be an (n, k)-code whose generator matrix Gk×n is GZP(ℓ). Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n]
be sets of size at most k. Then,
                                                                                                                   
                                                                                X \
                    dim(GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ ) =               max                               Aj − (s − 1)k                    (4.15)

where the maximum is over all partitions of [ℓ].

   By Proposition 4.19, a generic matrix is GZP(ℓ) for all ℓ and thus Theorem 4.14 is an immediate
corollary of Lemma 4.26. As a result, replacing the RHS of (4.15) with the LHS of (4.5), we have
that any GZP(ℓ) code is also MDS(ℓ).

Proof of Lemma 4.26. We prove (4.15) as two inequalities. First we show that ≥ direction of (4.15).
                                   T

By Proposition 4.22, the pattern S := (S1 , . . . , Sk ) with δi copies of Ai for each i ∈ [ℓ] and d copies
of the empty set (assume Sk−d+1 = · · · = Sk = ∅) is a generic zero pattern of order ℓ. Thus, since
C is a GZP(ℓ) code, there exists an invertible matrix Mk×k such that M G has the zero pattern S.
   Note that dim((M G)A1 ∩ · · · ∩ (M G)Aℓ ) = dim(GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ ). Thus, in order to show that
dim(GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ ) ≤ d, it suffices to show that for any z ∈ (M G)A1 ∩ · · · ∩ (M G)Aℓ , only the last
d coordinates can be nonzero.
   For each i ∈ [ℓ], let Ii be the indices j for which Sj = Ai . Since z ∈ (M G)Ai , and M G has the
zero pattern S, we have that z|Ii = 0. Thus,

                                                         z|Si∈[ℓ] Ii = 0.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING64



Thus, z is only nonzero on the coordinates corresponding to empty Si ’s in the partition. That is,
only the last d coordinates of z can be nonzero, proving the dimension upper bound.


4.3.2      MDS(ℓ) implies GZP(ℓ)
The “(a) implies (c)” part of Theorem 4.11 follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 4.27. Let C be a (n, k)-code which is MDS(ℓ). Let Gk×n be its generator matrix. Let
S = (S1 , . . . , Sk ) be a generic zero pattern of order ℓ. Then, G attains S.

Proof. By Corollary 4.24, there exists a maximal order-ℓ generic zero pattern S ′ which is a superset
of S. That is, there are A1 , . . . , Aℓ and δ1 , . . . , δℓ , such that S ′ is the union for all i ∈ [ℓ] of δi copies
                     Pℓ
of Ai plus d := k − i=1 δi copies of the empty set. Further, by “maximality”, each Ai is of size
k − δi . It suffices to show that C attains S ′ .
    Because S ′ is a generic zero pattern, we have for all nonempty I ⊆ [ℓ],
                                                                 X
                                                   |AI | ≤ k −         δi .
                                                                 i∈I


Thus, by Lemma 4.25, we have that for all partitions P of [ℓ], we have that
                                            X
                                                   |AP | ≤ (|P| − 1)k + d.
                                            P ∈P


For the partition, {1} ∪ · · · ∪ {ℓ}, we have equality because each |Ai | = k − δi .
    Thus, since C is MDS(ℓ), by Theorem 4.14, we have that

                                            dim(GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ ) = d.

Let X := GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAℓ be this d-dimensional subspace of Fk . Thus, by taking the dual,

                                              X ⊥ = G⊥             ⊥

4.3.3      Characterizing the null intersection property
We recall the following definition.

Definition 4.28. Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] be sets of size at most k. We say that A1 , . . . , Aℓ have the
null intersection property if for a generic matrix, Wk×n we have that

                                              WA1 ∩ · · · ∩ WAk = 0.

    Note that Theorem 4.14 characterizes which sets have the null intersection property: they are
those for which the RHS of (4.5) is equal to 0. Notably, the MDS(ℓ) condition is equivalent to
attaining null intersection for any family of ℓ sets with the null intersection property.

Remark 4.29. Composing this result and the proof that MDS(ℓ) and GZP(ℓ) are equivalent, one can
in fact show that GZP(ℓ) is equivalent to the seemingly weaker property that the matrix G attains all
generic zero patterns with at most ℓ distinct sets including the empty set. We omit further details.


4.4       Applications to list decoding: proof of theorem 4.4
In this section, we show that the recently established notions of higher-order MDS codes in the works
of [BGM21] and [Rot21] are in fact equivalent. An equivalent way to define LD-MDS(L) codes is
using the parity check matrix H(n−k)×n matrix of C. C is LD-MDS(L) if there doesn’t exist L + 1
distinct vectors e0 , e1 , . . . , eL ∈ Fn such that

                             L
                             X
                                   wt(ei ) ≤ L(n − k) and He0 = He1 = · · · = HeL .                (4.16)
                             i=0


4.4.1      Equivalence of MDS and LD-MDS (up to duality)
We now prove that (a) iff (b) in Theorem 4.11. We will break the proof into Propositions 4.30 and
4.31.

Proposition 4.30. C is LD-MDS(≤ L) ⇒ C ⊥ is MDS(L + 1).

Proof. We will prove the contrapositive: C ⊥ is not MDS(L + 1) ⇒ C is not LD-MDS(≤ L).
    Let C be a (n, k)-code and H(n−k)×n be its parity check matrix. Note that H is the generator
matrix of C ⊥ . By Lemma 2.6, the fact that C ⊥ is not MDS(L + 1) implies that there exists subsets
J0 , J1 , . . . , JL ⊂ [n] such that

   1. HJ0 ∩ HJ1 ∩ · · · ∩ HJL ̸= 0

   2. WJ0 ∩ WJ1 ∩ · · · ∩ WJL = 0 for a generic W(n−k)×n .

4.4.2     Reed-Solomon codes
Generic Reed-Solomon codes

Our main result Theorem 4.4 follows from this next result on duals of generic Reed-Solomon codes.

Proposition 4.33. The dual of a generic Reed-Solomon code is equivalent to a generic Reed-
Solomon code (up-to scaling of columns of the generator matrix).

Proof. Let C be a generic (n, k)-Reed-Solomon code. Let (α1 , . . . , αn ) be the generators of C. Let
C ′ be the (n, n − k)-code also generated by (α1 , . . . , αn ). Let G′n−k,n be the Vandermonde matrix
with generators (α1 , . . . , αn ). Note that by definition G′ is also generic.
   For all i ∈ [n], let ∆i = j∈[n]\{i} (αi − αj ). By standard results13 on Reed-Solomon codes, C ⊥
                                 Q

is a generalized Reed-Solomon code with generator matrix H such that Hj,i = αij−1 /∆i . Since C
is generic, we have that ∆i ̸= 0 for all i ∈ [n]. Therefore, each column of H is a nonzero scalar
multiple of a column of G′ . Thus C ⊥ is equivalent to a generic Reed-Solomon code.
 13 See, for example, [MS77] or Theorem 5.1.6 in [Hal].
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING68



Proof of Theorem 4.4. From Theorem 4.10 (GM-MDS), Theorem 4.11, and Proposition 4.33, we
know that generic Reed-Solomon codes are LD-MDS(L) for all L. In other words they are (ρ, L)-
                                       L
average-radius list-decodable for ρ = L+1 (1 − R) for all L. This also implies that they are (ρ, L)-
list-decodable for the same ρ.


4.5      Resolution of Conjecture 5.7 of [ST20]
Shangguan and Tamo [ST20] made an algebraic conjecture in their paper (see Conjecture 5.7 from
[ST20]) about the non-singularity of certain symbolic matrices, which would imply that generic
Reed-Solomon codes achieve list decoding capacity. We prove this conjecture here, the proof follows
from some of the results in our paper which we use to prove Theorem 4.4. We first introduce the
necessary notation to properly state Conjecture 5.7 of [ST20].
   Let C be a (n, k)-code with generator matrix G. Let J1 , . . . , Jt ⊆ [n]. We define a block matrix
MG,(J1 ,...,Jt ) as follows. For each i, j ∈ [t] with i < j, there is a block of k columns of M corresponding
to (i, j). The rows of M are filled in as follows.

 (a) For each i, j ⊆ [t − 1] with i < j, we add k rows for which the identity matrix Ik appears in the
      (i, j) and (j, t) blocks. The matrix −Ik appears in the (i, t) block. All other blocks are zero.

 (b) For each {i, j} ⊆ [t], we add |Ji ∩ Jj | rows for which the {i, j} block equals G⊤
                                                                                      Ji ∩Jj , with all
      other blocks being zero.
                     t                    t−1
                                               P
   Thus, M has       2 k columns and       2 k +  i̸=j |Ji ∩ Jj | rows.    The following lemma of [ST20]
relates MG,(J1 ,...,Jt ) to list decoding properties of C.

Lemma 4.34 ([ST20]). Let L, τ ≥ 1. Assume there exists c0 , c1 , . . . , cL ∈ C ∩ Bτ (y). Then, there
exist J0 , . . . , JL such that |Ji | ≥ n − τ for all i and MG,(J0 ,...,JL ) does not have full column rank.

   To give intuition about M , we include an adaptation of [ST20]’s proof of this lemma for com-
pleteness.

Proof. For all i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , L}, let Ji be the set of coordinates for ci and y are equal. Since

there exists a unique fi ∈ Fk such that ci = G⊤ fi . For all i, j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , L} with i < j let
fij = fj − fi .

   Let v be a column vector of length       2 k which is the fij ’s concatenated together in the order as

   First, to see why v ̸= 0, note that

                                   G⊤ f01 = G⊤ (f1 − f0 ) = c1 − c0 ̸= 0.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING69



Thus, f01 ̸= 0, so v ̸= 0.
    Second, to see why M v = 0, we split the analysis into the type (a) rows and the type (b) rows.

fij + fjt − fit = (fj − fi ) + (ft − fj ) − (ft − fi ) = 0. For the type (b) rows, it suffices to check for
all i < j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , L} that G⊤
                                    Ji ∩Jj fij = 0. Observe that


                               G⊤             ⊤
                                Ji ∩Jj fij = GJi ∩Jj (fj − fi ) = (cj − ci )|Ji ∩Jj


Note that, by definition, for all indices a ∈ Ji ∩ Jj , we have that (cj )a = ya = (ci )a . Thus, the above
expression does indeed equal zero. Therefore, M v = 0.
    Thus, M lacks full column rank.

implies that generic Reed-Solomon codes reach list-decoding capacity.

4.6       Computing Generic Intersection Dimension in Polyno-
          mial Time
In this section, we present a polynomial-time algorithm for computing the generic intersection di-
mension of a family of sets. Let A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] be sets of size at most k. Consider the following
linear program:

                                                     Primal LP

                                          ℓ
                                          X
                      minimize: k −             δi
                                          i=1

                   subject to: ∀i ∈ [ℓ],                                  δi ≥ 0
                                                                    X
                                      ∀I ⊆ [ℓ] (I ̸= ∅),                  δi ≤ k − |AI |.
                                                                    i∈I


    By Theorem 4.14 and Lemma 4.25, we know that the optimal integral solution to this linear
program is equal to the generic intersection dimension of A1 , . . . , Aℓ . In fact, we shall prove that
the optimal “fractional” has the same objective value.

Lemma 4.36. The objective value of the Primal LP is equal to the k-dimensional generic intersec-
tion dimension of A1 , . . . , Aℓ .

    Even with this observation, it is not obvious that the Primal LP can be solved in poly(n, k, ℓ)
time, as there are roughly 2ℓ constraints. However, we shall demonstrate the Primal LP can still be
solved efficiently.

Lemma 4.37. One can solve the Primal LP in poly(n, k, ℓ) time.

    As a result of these two lemmas, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 4.38. Given sets A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] of size at most k as input, one can compute the
k-dimensional generic intersection dimension of these sets in poly(n, k, ℓ) time.

    We note that while this is the same result as that proved in Section 4.7, the proof methods are
very different and seem to highlight different structural aspects of generic intersections.
    The remainder of this appendix is devoted to proving the two lemmas.


4.6.1      Proof of Lemma 4.36
By the theory of LP duality, the objective value of the Primal LP is equal to the objective value of
the Dual LP.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING72



                                                      Dual LP

                                         X
                    maximize: k −                (k − |AI |)µI
                                         I⊆[ℓ]
                                         I̸=∅

                   subject to: ∀I ⊆ [ℓ] (I ̸= ∅),                                          µI ≥ 0
                                                                                    X
                                  ∀i ∈ [ℓ],                                                µI ≥ 1.
                                                                                   I⊆[ℓ]
                                                                                    i∈I


   By Theorem 4.14 and Lemma 4.25, we have that there exists a partition P1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Ps = [ℓ] such
that
                                           s
                                           X
                                                  |APj | = (s − 1)k + d,
                                           j=1

where d is the generic intersection dimension. Consider the following assignment to the Dual LP:
µPj = 1 for all j ∈ [s] and µS = 0 otherwise. Note that since each i ∈ [ℓ] is a member of (at least)
one of the Pj ’s, we have that this assignment is feasible for the Dual LP. The objective value is then

                                  s
                                  X                       s
                                                          X
                             k−         (k − |APj |) =           |APj | − (s − 1)k = d.
                                  j=1                     j=1


   Thus, by duality, the objective value of the Primal LP is at least the generic intersection dimension
d. Since we previously mentioned that this value d is attainable by an integral assignment (via
Lemma 4.25), we have that the objective value of the Primal LP (and the Dual LP) is exactly the
generic intersection dimension.
   One can also prove that the Primal LP and the Dual LP are both integral directly (i.e., without
using Lemma 4.25) using the theory of Total Dual Integrality [Sch03].


4.6.2        Proof of Lemma 4.37
To solve the Primal LP, it suffices to implement an efficient (i.e., in poly(n, k, ℓ) time) separation
oracle (c.f., [GLS93]). In particular, given nonnegative δ1 , . . . , δℓ ∈ Q, we need to efficiently compute
                                                                                                   P
that either (1) the δ’s satisfy the primal LP or (2) exhibit a nonempty I ⊆ [ℓ] for which i∈I δi >
k − |AI |.
   Consider the function f : 2[ℓ] → Q defined by
                                                                     X
                                           f (I) = k − |AI | −             δi .
                                                                     i∈I


with f (∅) = 0. Observe that computing the separation oracle is thus equivalent to either verifying
that f is nonnegative or exhibiting an I ⊆ [ℓ] such that f (I) < 0.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING73



   It is straightforward to verify that f is submodular, that is for all I, J ⊆ [ℓ],

                                       f (I) + f (J) ≥ f (I ∪ J) + f (I ∩ J).

Minimizing such a function can be done in poly(ℓ) queries to f (c.f., [GLS93]), and thus we can
efficiently determine whether there exist I ⊆ [ℓ] such that f (I) < 0 (note that such an I must be
nonempty). Therefore, the Primal LP can be solved efficiently.


4.7      Connections to invariant theory
The combinatorial characterization of the generic intersection dimension in Theorem 4.14 allows for
a surprising connection between M DS(ℓ) codes and invariant theory. A simple observation charac-
terizes the generic intersection dimension as an instance of the well-known Edmonds problem, i.e.,
a computation of the rank of a symbolic matrix with linear entries, see Chapter 2. The Edmonds’s
problem is not known to have a polynomial-time algorithm. Nevertheless, the non-commutative
version of the Edmonds’s problem does have a polynomial-time algorithm, but this is highly non-
trivial and rests on some deep results in invariant theory, see [GGOW16, IQS18]. The combinatorial
characterization in Theorem 1.15 allows us to prove in a curious way that the Edmonds’s problem
for the generic intersection dimension is equivalent to its non-commutative counterpart, which then
immediately yields a polynomial-time algorithm for computing generic intersection dimension.


4.7.1      Linear matrices, non-commutative rank and the blow-up regularity
           lemma
A matrix L = t1 X1 + · · · + tm Xm , where Xi are p × q matrices with entries in a ground field F and
ti are indeterminates. is called a linear matrix. There are two important notions of ranks associated
to such a linear matrix are the commutative rank and the non-commutative rank. Computing the
commutative rank of a linear matrix is the Edmonds’s problem and computing the non-commutative
rank is the non-commutative version of the Edmonds’s problem. Interpolating between these two
ranks are the ranks of blow-ups, a tool that originated in invariant theory and is crucial in un-
derstanding the non-commutative rank. These blow-ups are also important for our purposes. We
explain all of these below.

Definition 4.39. Let L = t1 X1 + · · · + tm Xm be a linear matrix.

   • The commutative rank crk(L) is defined as rk(L) viewed as a matrix with entries in the function
      field F(t1 , . . . , tm ).

   • The non-commutative rank ncrk(L) is defined as rk(L) viewed as a matrix with entries in the
      free skew-field F <
                        ( t1 , . . . , t m )
                                           >.
CHAPTER 4. GENERIC INTERSECTION DIMENSION, GM-MDS, AND LIST-DECODING74



    First, we note that most of linear algebra works over skew-fields (a.k.a. division algebras). Rank
is defined in terms of maximum number of (left)-linearly independent columns. This is sometimes
called left column rank. Similarly one can define left and right row and column ranks. Left column
rank equals the right row rank and the right column rank equals the left row rank, but the left
column rank may not equal the right column rank when working over a general division algebra. For
linear matrices interpreted as matrices with entries in the free skew-field, all ranks, i.e., left/right
row/column ranks, are all the same. We will not delve into the intricacies of the definition of the
free skew-field here as we will mostly use the characterization of non-commutative rank via ranks of
blow-ups, which we now define.

Definition 4.40. Let L = t1 X1 + · · · + tm Xm be a linear matrix of size p × q. Then, for d ∈ Z≥1 ,
we define the dth blow-up of L to be the dp × dq matrix

                                L(T1 , . . . , Tm ) := X1 ⊗ T1 + · · · + Xm ⊗ Tm ,

                                                                                          (i)
where Ti are d × d matrices whose entries are all distinct variables, say tj,k . We define the dth
blow-up rank of L by
                                         rkd (L) := crk(L(T1 , . . . , Tm )),

i.e., the latter rank is taken by viewing L(T1 , . . . , Tm ) as a matrix with entries in the function field
   (i)
F(tj,k ).
                                                                                                        P
    Let us first justify the notation L(T1 , . . . , Tm ) := X1 ⊗ T1 + · · · + Xm ⊗ Tm . To get            i Xi ⊗ Ti
                                                                                          th
from L we replace each entry with a d × d matrix as follows: if the (α, β) entry of L is of the
     P                                P
form i ci ti , then we replace it with i ci Ti . Thus, it is as if we plugged in Ti for ti . We have the
equality (see [IQS17, IQS18]):

                                              rkd (L)        rkd (L)       rkd (L)
                           ncrk(L) = lim              = supd         = max         .
                                        d→∞      d              d       d     d

    Dividing the rank of the blow-up by d is a normalization is to be expected because we blow-up
the size of the matrix by a factor of d. There are a few subtleties, for example the sequence rkdd(L)
is not always monotone. But perhaps the major subtlety the reader may have noticed is that while
ncrk(L) is an integer by definition, it is not so clear why any of the other expressions are integers.
This is a consequence of an amazing result called the blow-up regularity lemma14 that was first
proved by Ivanyos, Qiao and Subrahmanyam in [IQS17]. A more conceptual proof can be found in
[DM18].

                         ncrk(LA (W )) = lim rankt (LA (W ))/t = crk(LA (W )).
                                            t→∞


Thus, we conclude that

Chapter 5

Field Size of Higher Order MDS
Codes

What to find in this chapter:

   • Upper bounds on the field size of higher order MDS codes via randomized construction.

   • Lower bounds on the field size of higher order MDS codes.

   • Explicit constructions of higher order MDS codes.

Sources:    The material in this chapter is adapted from [BGM21, BGM22, BDG23b].


   Now that we have connected the study of higher order MDS codes to a number of different parts
of coding theory, we focus on constructing higher order MDS codes in this chapter. In particular,
we study the possible field sizes of higher order MDS codes with various parameters.


5.1      Upper bounds on field size of MDS(ℓ)
5.1.1     Parity-check upper bound
Theorem 5.1. There exists a (m, n, a, b)-MR Tensor Code with field size Oa,b,m (nb(m−a) ).

Proof. Let Crow ⊗ Ccol be the code we seek to construct. We shall exhibit a system of equations,
the sum of whose degrees is Oa,b,m (nb(m−a) ). By the Schwartz-Zippel Lemma [DL78, Zip79, Sch80],
this will imply the existence of a code over a field size of Oa,b,m (nb(m−a) ).




                                                   78
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                  79



   Let Hrow be the parity check matrix of Crow of size b × n, Hcol be the parity check matrix of
Ccol of size a × m. And let                 "                     #
                                                  Im ⊗ Hrow
                                      H=
                                                Hcol ⊗ D(n−b)×n

be the parity check matrix of Crow ⊗ Ccol . Here Im is the m × m identity matrix and D is the
(n − b) × n matrix formed by the first n − b rows of In . Note that the number of columns of H is
mn and the number of rows of H is mb + (n − b)a = mb + na − ab which is the codimension of the
tensor code.
   Call an erasure pattern E ⊂ [m] × [n] minimal if each nonempty row of E has size least b + 1
and each nonempty column of E has size at least a + 1.
   We impose the following three constraints on H,

  1. Hrow is an MDS code.

  2. Hcol is an MDS code.

  3. For every E ⊂ [m] × [n] which is minimal and correctable, we impose that the minor H|E has
     rank |E|.

   First, we show these conditions are sufficient to ensure that Crow ⊗ Ccol is an MR Tensor Code.
Let E ⊆ [m] × [n] be any correctable pattern. Since Hrow is MDS, if any row of E has at most b
entries, we can correct that row just by using Hrow . Likewise, since Hcol is MDS, if and row of E
has at most a entries, we can correct that row just by using Hcol . By removing any such rows and
columns iteratively, it suffices to correct some E ′ ⊆ E which is minimal and correctable (as E is
correctable). The correctability of E ′ follows from condition 3.
   To ensure the first condition, we sample the entries of Hrow randomly. To check MDS, we need
to ensure that each a × a minor of the [m, a] code has nonzero determinant. This is a system of
equations of total degree a m
                             
                            a = Oa,m (1).
   Likewise, for the second condition, we can ensure Hcol is MDS with a system of equations of
total degree b nb = Ob (nb ).
                 

   The last condition is a bit more tricky to analyze. First, we show that there are at most
Om,a,b (nb(m−a) ) minimal patterns, and each has Om,a,b (1) entries.
   Assume that a minimal correctable pattern E has u nonempty rows and v nonempty columns.
Because E is correctable, it is regular and so |E| ≤ ub + va − ab. Also, since E is minimal, we have
that |E| ≥ v(a + 1). Therefore,

                   ub + va − ab ≥ |E| ≥ v(a + 1) =⇒ v ≤ b(u − a) ≤ b(m − a).

Thus, E spans at most b(m−a) columns and has at most ub+va−ab ≤ mb+b(m−a)a−ab = Oa,b,m (1)
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                       80



entries. Thus, the number of such E is at most
                                                       
                           n             m · b(m − a)
                                  ·                         = Oa,b,m (nb(m−a) ).
                       ≤ b(m − a)    ≤ mb + b(m − a)a − ab

Ensuring that H|E has full rank is equivalent to some |E| × |E| minor of H|E having nonzero
determinant. If E is correctable, then one of these minors has at least one symbolically nonzero
determinant. The constraint for this determinant has degree |E| = Oa,b,m (1). Thus, we can specify
all the necessary constraints with total degree Oa,b,m (nb(m−a) ).


5.1.2     An upper bound for random Reed-Solomon codes
Here we prove Theorem 4.5 and show how one can make Theorem 4.4 more quantitative in order to
reason about the list-decoding capabilities of a random Reed-Solomon code. For a code parameters
(n, k) and a finite field F, we define a random Reed-Solomon code to be one whose generators
α1 , . . . , αn are chosen uniformly and independently from F.
                                                                                   n
                                                                                           L+1
Proposition 5.2. Let n, k, L be positive integers. The function c(n, k, L) = 2Ln2 ≤n−k            has the
                                                                            ℓ
property that a random (n, k)-Reed-Solomon code is LD-MDS(≤ L), and thus ( ℓ+1 (1 − k/n), ℓ)-list
decodable for all ℓ ≤ L, with probability at least 1 − c/|F|.

Proof. Let C be the code generated by the random α1 , . . . , αn . With probability at least 1 − n2 /|F|,
we have that αi ̸= αj for all i ̸= j ∈ [n] and thus C is MDS. From the proof of Proposition 4.33 and
Theorem 4.11, we then have that (α1 , . . . , αn ) generate a LD-MDS(≤ L) matrix if and only if the
(n, n − k)-matrix G′ with entries G′j,i = αij−1 is MDS(L + 1). In order to check that G′ is MDS(ℓ),
it suffices to show by Lemma 2.6 that for all null intersecting families A1 , . . . , AL+1 ⊆ [n], each of
size at most n − k, with total size L(n − k), we have that

                                             G′A1 ∩ · · · ∩ G′AL+1 = 0.

This condition is equivalent to the following block matrix being nonsingular:

The square matrix has size L(n − k) and each entry has degree at most n − k − 1. Therefore, the

Remark 5.3. Combining the two upper bounds (adapting the first for Reed-Solomon codes), one
can take c(n, k, L) = nO(min(k,n−k)L) in Theorem 4.5.

   Combining the two results in this section, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.4. There exists (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) codes over fields of size nO(min(k,n−k)ℓ) .


5.2      Lower bounds on field size of MDS(ℓ)
5.2.1     An nℓ−1 lower bound for weak-MDS(ℓ).
Our lower bound for MDS(m) is inspired by lower bounds for Maximally Recoverable Local Recon-
struction Codes from [GGY20] and works as follows. We will actually prove the lower bound for
weak-MDS(m) codes which implies a lower bound for MDS(m) codes. By a reduction, we can assume
that the dimension of the code is equal to m. Suppose V be the generator matrix of an (n, m)-weak-
MDS(m) code where m is a constant and suppose m divides n for simplicity. We will partition
V1 , V2 , . . . , Vn in to m parts of size n/m each, say the partition is given by [n] = P1 ⊔ P2 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Pm .
Now consider arbitrary subsets Ai ⊂ Pi of size |Ai | = m − 1 for i ∈ [m]. For V to be MDS(m),
it is necessary that VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAm = 0. To see this, note that x ∈ VAi imposes 1 generic
linear equation on x. And so x ∈ VAi for all i ∈ [m] imposes m equations which should be linearly
independent if V behaves generically. Fix any i ∈ [m] and fix a subset Ai ⊂ Pi . A random point
X of Fm
      q lies in VAi for some Ai ⊂ Pi with high probability. This is because for a fixed subset
                                                              n/m
Ai ⊂ Pi , X lies in VAi with probability 1q . Since there are m−1
                                                                  
                                                                    subsets Ai ⊂ Pi , the expected
                                           n/m
                                                 1
number of Ai ⊂ Pi such that X ∈ VAi is m−1 · q which is ≫ 1 if q ≪m nm−1 . By using pairwise
independence and carefully calculating second moments, we can conclude that if q ≪m nm−1 , with
high probability, there exists some Ai ⊂ Pi such that X ∈ VAi . By union bound over i ∈ [m],
X ∈ VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ · · · ∩ VAm for some Ai ⊂ Pi and X is non-zero with high probability which violates
the MDS(m) property. Therefore q ≫m nm−1 . We will now make this argument formal.

Lemma 5.5. Assume n ≫ k ≥ 3. Let V be an (n, k)-weak-MDS(k) code over field Fq . Then
q ≥ Ωk (nk−1 ).

C0 which is (n − k + b, b)-MDS(b) code (also trivially cycle-MDS(b)). By Lemma 5.5, we have that
the field size of C0⊥ (and thus C) is

Corollary 5.7. Let C = Ccol ⊗ Crow be an (m, n, a, b)-MR tensor code. The minimum field size of
C is at least Ωm (nmin{b−1,n−b−1,m−a} ).
                                     ′

                                                  ′
Crow at any a − 1 positions. Observe that Ccol ⊗ Crow must be an (m − a + 1, n, 1, b)-tensor code.
                                                ′
This is because the generator matrix of Ccol ⊗ Crow is U ⊗ V[n]\A where A is the set of punctured
positions which is a part of U ⊗ V , the generator matrix for Ccol ⊗ Crow . Moreover, there is a
                                                                     ′
canonical injective map from correctable erasure patterns of Ccol ⊗ Crow and those of Ccol ⊗ Crow .
By Corollary 3.4, Ccol is an (n, n − b)-MDS(m − a + 1) code. Thus, by Corollary 5.6 the field size
of Ccol is at least Ωm (nmin{b,n−b,m−a+1}−1 ), as desired.

Corollary 5.9. Let U ⊗ V be an (m, n, a, b) MR tensor code with a ≥ 1 and m − a ≥ 2. Then the
field size must be at least Ob (nb−1 ).

we have that V is an MDS(3) code. Thus, the field size lower bound of (n−b)−1    − 1 = Ωb (nb−1 )
applies.


5.2.3      Application to list decoding
For applications to list decoding, the previous lower bound when combined with Theorem 4.11
isn’t satisfactory as it only says that information-theoretically-optimal2 average-radius list decoding.
Further, the “hard” case identified is a rather extreme example, where one of the codewords has
Hamming distance n − 2 from the received codeword!
    In this section, we extend the lower bound to worst-case list decoding.

Theorem 5.10. Let n ≥ k ≥ 0 be such that n − k is divisible by 3. Let C be an (n, k)-MDS code
which is (2, 2(n−k)                                                        n−2(n−k)/3+1
                                                                                       
               3n ) worst-case list decodable. Then, C requires field size  (n−k)/3+1
                                                                                         − 1.

Remark 5.11. This lower bound does not say that error-correcting codes achieving list decoding
capacity require exponential field size (which contradicts the known list decoding capacity of random
linear codes). Instead is just says that codes over subexponential fields cannot achieve capacity with
the exactly optimal list size.
   2 Here we mean that the tradeoff between list decoding radius and list size is exactly as specified by the generalized

singleton bound.
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                         86



Remark 5.12. Also note that this lower bound only applies to MDS codes (i.e., (1, n−k
                                                                                   2n ) list
decodable–such as Reed-Solomon codes. It does not apply to non-MDS codes, as they already have a
suboptimal tradeoff for list-size 1.

   To start, we show that the MDS(3) lower bound extends to the case the sets A1 , A2 , A3 are all
the same size.

Lemma 5.13 (Extension to the (2k/3, 2k/3, 2k/3) split.). Let k be divisible by 3. Let V be an
(n, k)-MDS code such that for all A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] of size 2k/3 we have that dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 )
is what it generically should be. Then, the field size of the code is at least n−2k/3+1

Proof. Let V be an MDS(3) code such that VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 = 0 whenever |A1 | = |A2 | = |A3 | = 2k/3
and generically the intersection should be 0 (this is equivalent to |Ai ∩ Aj | ≤ k/3 for all i ̸= j).
   Pick 2k/3 − 2 columns I ⊆ [n] of V . Let k ′ = k − |I| = k/3 + 2 Pick an arbitrary projection
              ′
Π : Fk → Fk of rank k ′ such that ker(Π) = VI . Let V ′ be the code with columns Π(vi ) for i ∈ [n] \ I.

Proposition 5.14. V ′ is MDS.

     Note that |A1 | = |A2 | = |A3 | = 2k/3. Using the generic intersection formula, one can verify
that A1 , A2 , A3 have the generic intersection property over dimension k. Thus, by definition of V
we have that VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 = 0.
     Let W = Π−1 (span(x)). Note that dim(W ) = k − k ′ + 1 = 2k/3 − 1. Without much work, one
can verify that W ⊆ VA1 and further

                                        dim(W ∩ VA2 ) ≥ |I2 | + 1
                                        dim(W ∩ VA3 ) ≥ |I3 | + 1.

Thus,

        dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 ) ≥ dim((W ∩ VA2 ) ∩ (W ∩ VA3 )) ≥ |I2 | + 1 + |I3 | + 1 − |W | = 1,

a contradiction. Thus, V ′ is MDS(3) for the (2, k ′ − 1, k ′ − 1) split. Therefore we get a lower bound
of                                                           

     We are now ready to prove Theorem 5.10.

Proof of Theorem 5.10. We adapt the proof of Proposition 4.30. Let H be the parity check matrix
of C (i.e., the generator matrix of C ⊥ ). If H has the property that for all A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] of size
2
3 (n − k) we have that dim(HA1 ∩ HA2 ∩ HA3 ) is generically what it should be, then the desired field
size lower bound holds.
     Otherwise, there exists A1 , A2 , A3 of size 32 (n − k) such that dim(HA1 ∩ HA2 ∩ HA3 ) is more
than its generic intersection dimension. Thus, (by e.g., Claim 2.11) there exist A′i ⊆ Ai such that
HA′1 ∩ HA′2 ∩ HA′3 ̸= 0 when generically it should be 0. Therefore, we can find nonzero e1 , e2 , e3 ∈ Fn
such that He1 = He2 = He3 (i.e., they are in the same coset of C) and supp(ei ) ⊆ A′i for all i ∈ [3].
If e1 , e2 , e3 are all distinct, then we have violated that C is (2, 2(n−k)
                                                                        3n ) worst-case list decodable. If
e1 = e2 = e3 , then A1 ∩ A2 ∩ A3 ̸= 0 which contradicts the generic intersection is 0. Otherwise, if
say WLOG e1 = e2 ̸= e3 , then

                                | supp(e1 − e3 )| ≤ |A′1 ∩ A′2 | + |A′3 | ≤ k.

However H(e1 − e3 ) = 0, so C cannot be MDS.
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                                       88



5.3         Doubly-exponential construction of higher-order MDS

construct MDS(ℓ) codes for all ℓ ≥ 4 with field sizes comparable to Roth’s.

5.4       Constructions of MDS(3) codes
In this section, we give a number of observations concerning constructions of MDS(3) codes. Al-
though we do not give explicit constructions of (n, k) − MDS(3) codes, we do make major progress
on the important special cases of (n, 3)-MDS(3) codes which have been of much interest (c.f.,
[BGM21, Rot21, SL22]).


5.4.1     Observations about Reed-Solomon codes
First, we give an alternative characterization of the higher-order MDS conditions when considering
a Reed-Solomon code.
   Assume we have a (n, k)-RS code with evaluation points β1 , . . . , βn ∈ F. For A ⊆ [n] define
                                                          Y

                              ΠdA (x) := (ΠA (x), xΠA (x), · · · , xd−1 ΠA (x)).

   Assume that A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] such that |Ai | ≤ k for all i ∈ [ℓ]. Let δi = k − |Ai |.

   The upper-left (k − δi ) × δi submatrix is all 0’s and the lower-left δi × δi submatrix has full rank.

rank.


5.4.2     Application: An n2k−1 construction for the (2, k − 1, k − 1) split.
The goal of this section is the following theorem.
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                       91



Theorem 5.19. There exists an explicit (n, k)-MDS code V over field size O(n)2k−1 such that for
any A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] such that |A1 | = 2 and |A2 | = |A3 | = k − 1 we have that dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 )
is equal to the generic intersection dimension.

Remark 5.20. As the only nontrivial condition for (n, 3)-MDS(3) is the case in which |A1 | = |A2 | =
|A3 | = 2, this yields an O(n5 ) construction in this case, beating the previous record of O(n32 ) by
Roth [Rot21]. Soon, we shall present an O(n3 ) construction.

Proof. Let q ≥ n be a prime power with characteristic at least k. Let K be the a degree-2k − 1
extension of Fq . We let γ be a generator of this extension.
   Pick an arbitrary injective map α : [n] → Fq . We shall let our RS field evaluations be

                                                βi = γαi − αi2 .

   We let V (α, γ) be the dimension k RS code with β1 , . . . , βn (as defined above) as evaluation
points.
   Let A1 , A2 , A3 be such that |A1 | = 2 and |A2 | = |A3 | = k − 1. We seek to show that (5.4) has full
row rank (i.e., nonzero determinant) whenever A1 , A2 , A3 have the combinatorial null intersection
property. Note we may assume WLOG that A1 = {1, 2}, so it suffices to consider the determinant

                                 ΠA2 (β1 )ΠA3 (β2 ) − ΠA2 (β2 )ΠA3 (β1 ).

   If 1 ∈ A2 . Then the expression is nonzero exactly when 2 ̸∈ A2 and 1 ̸∈ A3 , which corresponds
precisely with the combinatorial null intersection conditions. Similar cases hold by the same logic,
so we may now assume that A2 and A3 are disjoint from A1 .
   Further, we may assume A2 and A3 are disjoint because we can factor out the nonzero term
ΠA2 ∩A3 (β1 )ΠA2 ∩A3 (β2 ) from the expanded determinant. In that case, we need to allow for the
(easier) case that 0 < |A2 | = |A3 | < k − 1.
   Now, observe that for some nonzero a ∈ Fq , we have that
                                             Y                         Y
                    ΠA2 (β1 )ΠA3 (β2 ) = a          (γ − (α1 + αi ))          (γ − (α2 + αj )).
                                             i∈A2                      j∈A3


Likewise for some nonzero b ∈ Fq ,
                                             Y                         Y
                    ΠA2 (β2 )ΠA3 (β1 ) = b          (γ − (α2 + αi ))          (γ − (α1 + αj )).
                                             i∈A2                      j∈A3


Assume for sake of contradiction that ΠA2 (β1 )ΠA3 (β2 ) = ΠA2 (β2 )ΠA3 (β1 ). Since γ is a degree n2k−1
extension, by comparing the lead coefficients, we know that a = b, and the polynomials in γ must
have the same roots. Thus, there exists a permutation π : {1} × A2 ∪ {2} × A3 → {2} × A2 ∪ {1} × A3
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                92



such that whenever π(i1 , i2 ) = (j1 , j2 ) we have that αi1 + αi2 = αj1 + αj2 . Since we are assuming
that A2 and A3 are disjoint, we can never map {1} × A2 to {1} × A3 nor {2} × A3 to {2} × A2 .
Thus, we can decompose π into a pair of permutations τ2 : A2 → A2 and τ3 : A3 → A3 such that
for all i ∈ A2 we have that α1 + αi = α2 + ατ2 (i) with a similar definition for τ3 . Now observe that
since Fq has characteristic at least k we have that
                                               X                          X             X
                      0 ̸= |A2 |(α2 − α1 ) =          (αi − ατ2 (i) ) =          αi −          αi = 0,
                                               i∈A2                       i∈A2          i∈A2


a contradiction.

5.4.3     Application: An n2k construction for the weak (a, k + 1 − a, k − 1)

We generalize the construction a bit in the weak-MDS case.
                                                                                                         2
Theorem 5.21. There exists an explicit (n, k)-MDS code V over field size O(n)2k such that for any
disjoint A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] such that |A1 | + |A2 | = k + 1 and k − 1 we have that dim(VA1 ∩ VA2 ∩ VA3 )
is equal to the generic intersection dimension.

Remark 5.22. The construction likely works for when A1 , A2 , A3 are not disjoint (e.g., see argu-
ments in [BGM21, Rot21]), but we omit the details.

Proof. Pick distinct α1 , . . . , αn ∈ Fq such that αi1 + αi2 = αj1 + αj2 iff {i1 , i2 } = {j1 , j2 }. It is clear
that this works for q ≈ n2 . Let Fq [x] be the univariate polynomial ring over Fq . We define our RS
evaluation polynomials to be βi (x) = αi x − αi2 for all i ∈ [n]. A key observation is that due to our
choice of αi ’s, whenever i1 ̸= i2 , j1 ̸= j2 , and {i1 , i2 } ̸= {j1 , j2 } we have that4

                                         gcd(βi1 − βi2 , βj1 − βj2 ) = 1.                                    (5.7)

   Let V (x) be the (n, k)-RS code generated by these evaluation points. We claim that for any
A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] disjoint with |A1 | + |A2 | = k + 1 and |A3 | = k − 1, we have that

                                        VA1 (x) ∩ VA2 (x) ∩ VA3 (x) = 0.                                     (5.8)

Since this condition can be written as a degree ≤ k 2 polynomial in x, by evaluating V at some
                                                                          2
degree k 2 irreducible of Fq , we then immediately get an n2k construction.

    Using (5.7) one last time, we have that 1≤i<j≤a−1 (βj −βi ) is relatively prime to ΠA2 (βa ). Thus,
each gi is divisible by ΠA2 (βa ). This violates the assumption that gcd(g1 , . . . , ga ) = 1. Therefore,
(5.8) holds and our construction is valid.


5.4.4      New explicit constructions for (n, 3) − MDS(3)
A simple O(n4 ) construction

Theorem 5.23. There exists an explicit (n, 3)-MDS(3) code with field size O(n4 ).

Proof. Assume q is an odd prime power of size at least n. Let Fq [γ] be a degree four extension. Let
α : [n] → Fq be some injective map. We claim that

                                              βi = α(i) + γα(i)2 , i ∈ [n]
CHAPTER 5. FIELD SIZE OF HIGHER ORDER MDS CODES                                                                94



form the generators of a (n, 3) − MDS(3) code. In particular, we must check that
                                                                     

for all injective α : [6] → Fq . When expanded, LHS is a degree three polynomial in γ; that is, the
symbolic determinant can be written as

                             P (γ) = p0 (α)γ 0 + p1 (α)γ 1 + p2 (α)γ 2 + p3 (α)γ 3 ,

where pi is some polynomial in Fq [x1 , . . . , x6 ] evaluated at α(1), . . . , α(6). A notable observation
that will be useful later is that

                                        p1 (α) = p0 (α)(α1 + · · · + α6 ).

Since γ is a degree four field extension, we have that P (γ) = 0 if and only if pi (α) = 0 for all
i ∈ {0, 1, 2, 3}. Let J ⊆ Fq [x1 , . . . , x6 ] be the ideal generated by p0 , p1 , p2 , p3 . It suffices to prove
that there exists h ∈ J such that h(α) ̸= 0. We show this as follows.
