An O(n3 ) construction.

With another trick, we can shave the field size by another factor of n.

Theorem 5.26. There exists an explicit (n, 3)-MDS(3) code with field size O(n3 ).

Proof. Assume q is a power of 7, then let Fq [γ] be the degree three extension such that γ 3 = 2. Let
S ⊆ Fq be such that no six sum to 0. It is clear that we can have |S| ≥ q/7.
   We claim that
                                         βα = α + γα2 , α ∈ S.

form the generators of a (n, 3) − MDS(3) code. To see why, if we naively expand the determinant
condition, we get
                              p0 (α)γ 0 + p1 (α)γ 1 + p2 (α)γ 2 + p3 (α)γ 3 .

Thus, the ideal we wish to ensure is nonzero is

                                    (p0 (α) − 2p3 (α), p1 (α), p2 (α))

                                            P                                   P
A key observation is that that p1 (α) = (       α)p0 (α). By definition of S,       α ̸= 0. Thus, the idea
generated above is equivalent to (p0 (α), p2 (α), p3 (α). Using a Groebner basis, one can verify that
Q
  i̸=j (αi − αj ) ̸= 0 is in this ideal, proving that our construction works.

   We leave closing the gap between Ω(n2 ) and O(n3 ) as an intriguing open question.
Chapter 6

Generalized GM-MDS Theorem

What to find in this chapter:

   • An extensive generalization of the GM-MDS theorem for polynomial codes and their duals.

   • An abstraction called “higher order MDS codes with a basis” for proving various GM-MDS-like
      theorems.

   • A conjectured further generalization known as the “Ultimate GM-MDS Conjecture” with some
      evidence toward its veracity.

Sources:    The material in this chapter is adapted from [BDG23a] as well as some additional un-
published work by the same authors.


   In Chapter 4, we proved that to construct a higher order MDS codes, it suffices to prove a
suitable variant of the GM-MDS theorem of Lovett [Lov18a] and Yildiz-Hassibi [YH19b]. In this
chapter, we generalize their results to a much broader family of error-correcting codes. As a result,
we also greatly expand the family of known higher order MDS codes. These results also allow the
thesis to be more self-contained. In particular, one can prove Theorem 4.4 using only basic linear
algebra over finite fields and the proofs in this thesis.


6.1      Introduction
The optimal rate distance tradeoff of an (n, k)-code (over large alphabets) is given by the Singleton
bound [Sin64] which states that the minimum distance d is at most n − k + 1. A code which achieves
this bound is called an MDS (Maximum Distance Separable) code. MDS codes are some of the most
important codes because of their optimality, for example they are extensively used in distributed
storage. Reed-Solomon codes [RS60] are the most well-known family of MDS codes and one of the

                                                   97
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                     98



first polynomial codes to be discovered. An (n, k) Reed-Solomon code is obtained by evaluating
degree < k polynomials over Fq at n distinct points from Fq . These codes achieve the Singleton
bound over any field of size q ≥ n. In this paper, we will only consider linear codes defined over
finite fields. It is not hard to show that a linear (n, k)-code C with generator matrix Gk×n is MDS
iff for every k × k minor of G is nonzero. The generator matrix of a Reed-Solomon code is of the
form                                                                     
                                               1       1     ...      1
                                          α1         α2     ...     αn 
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                          2          α22            αn2 
                                  Gk×n =  α1                ...                                      (6.1)
                                          .           ..    ..        .. 
                                          .                    .
                                          .            .               . 
                                                                          

                                             α1k−1   α2k−1   ...    αnk−1

where α1 , α2 , . . . , αn ∈ Fq are distinct. There are several natural generalizations of MDS codes
and recently many of them have been unified into a single concept called higher order MDS codes
[BGM21, BGM23]. Higher order MDS codes tie together the GM-MDS theorem, optimally list-
decodable codes and MR tensor codes. We will now briefly discuss these connections.

GM-MDS theorem.            In some coding theory applications, we need MDS codes whose generator
matrices have constrained support, i.e., with a specific pattern of zeros. See [DSY14, HHYD14, YS13,
DSDY13] for applications of such codes to multiple access networks and secure data exchange. Let
S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sn ) where S1 , . . . , Sk ⊂ [n], we call such an S a zero pattern for k × n matrices.
If there are ℓ distinct non-empty sets among S1 , . . . , Sk , we say that S is an order-ℓ zero pattern.
We say that an (n, k)-code C with some generator matrix Gk×n attains the zero pattern S if there
exists an invertible matrix Mk×k such that G̃ = M G has zeros in the positions specified by S (i.e.,
in positions ∪ki=1 {i} × Si ). Since G̃ is also a generator matrix of C, equivalently, we say C attains
the zero pattern S if there exists a generator matrix of C which has zeros in positions of S. For an
MDS code to attain a zero pattern S, a simple necessary condition is that the zero pattern should
satisfy a Hall type condition:
                                      ∀I ⊂ [k], | ∩i∈I Si | ≤ k − |I|.                                 (6.2)

It is easy to see why (6.2) is a necessary condition, if (6.2) is not satisfied, then there exists a k × k
submatrix of G which has too many zeros and hence cannot be invertible. On the other hand, if
(6.10) is satisfied, then by Hall’s matching theorem every k × k submatrix has some matching of
non-zeros entries and hence can be made invertible by a suitable (or generic) choice of the non-zero
entries [DSY14]. Therefore, over large enough alphabet, one can always find an MDS matrix with a
given zero pattern S if (6.10) is satisfied. A pattern which satisfies condition (6.2) is called a generic
zero pattern [BGM23].
   Dau et al. [DSY14] conjectured that given any generic zero pattern, there exists a Reed-Solomon
code over any field of size q ≥ n+k −1 which attains the zero pattern S. This conjecture which came
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                        99



to be known as the GM-MDS conjecture 1 was eventually proved independently by Lovett [Lov18a]
and Yildiz and Hassibi [YH19b]. The heart of the proof (using a reduction shown by [DSY14]) is
to show that generic Reed-Solomon codes can achieve the zero pattern S which itself reduces to
showing that a symbolic matrix has non-zero determinant. By observing that the degree of each
variable in the determinant polynomial is at most n + k − 2, one can immediately get an upper
bound on the field size needed to achieve the zero pattern S [DSY14]. So the GM-MDS theorem
can equivalently be stated as follows.

Theorem 6.1 (GM-MDS Theorem [DSY14, Lov18a, YH19b]). Let S = (S1 , S2 , . . . , Sn ) be any
generic zero pattern. Then for any finite field Fq , there exists some α1 , . . . , αn ∈ Fq (algebraic
closure of Fq ) such that the Reed-Solomon code with generator matrix G as in (6.1) attains the zero
pattern S.

   An MDS code which achieves all generic zero patterns of order ℓ is called a GZP(ℓ) code [BGM23].
In other words, the GM-MDS theorem states that Reed-Solomon codes are GZP(ℓ) for all ℓ. GM-
MDS type theorems have been proved for Gabidulin codes [Gab21] in [YH19a] and for linearized
Reed-Solomon codes (or skew polynomial codes) in [LWWZS23]. Are these codes somehow very
special or is there a common generalization of these results?

Question 6.2. Is there a common generalization of the GM-MDS theorem which implies the existing
results for Reed-Solomon codes, Gabidulin codes and linearized Reed-Solomon codes?

   Note that all these codes are polynomial codes. We will see shortly that we can in fact prove a
GM-MDS theorem for all polynomial codes!
   Given the rich set of connections of higher order MDS codes (c.f., Chapters 3 and 4), answering
Question 6.2 or equivalently understanding what codes have the higher order MDS property is a
fundamental question which will likely lead to progress in several areas of coding theory.


6.1.1     Main results
We now discuss the main contributions of this chapter.

Generalized GM-MDS for Polynomial Codes.                      First, we satisfactorily answer Question 6.2
by showing that there is nothing very special about Reed-Solomon, Gabidulin or Linearized Reed-
Solomon codes. We prove that GM-MDS theorem holds for all polynomial codes.

Definition 6.3 (Polynomial codes). Let f = (f1 , f2 , . . . , fk ) ∈ (F[x1 , . . . , xr ])k be a k-dimensional
vector of polynomials such that f1 , . . . , fk are linearly independent over F. Let α1 , α2 , . . . , αk ∈ Fr
   1 It is unclear what GM stands as [DSY14] don’t explain it in the paper where they introduced the conjecture.

Our best guess is that it stands for ‘Generator Matrix’ given that we are looking for MDS codes with constrained
generator matrices.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                    100



be distinct. The polynomial code Cf is defined as the linear code with generator matrix Gk×n where
                                                                                    
                                          f1 (α1 )       f1 (α2 )   ···   f1 (αk )
                                                                                 
                                  f2 (α1 ) f2 (α2 ) · · ·                f2 (αk )

                                                     r
We say that Cf is generic if α1 , . . . , αk ∈ F are chosen to be algebraically independent over F.

Theorem 6.4 ((Informal) Generalized GM-MDS Theorem for Polynomial Codes). Generic polyno-
mial codes are higher order MDS, i.e., is MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ.

Example 6.5. We can recover all the known examples of GM-MDS theorem using Theorem 6.4.

  1. Reed-Solomon codes are polynomial codes with f (x) = (1, x, x2 , . . . , xk−1 ).
                                                                                     2         k−1
  2. Gabidulin codes are polynomial codes with f (x) = (x, xq , xq , . . . , xq                         ) where |F| = q m for
      some m.

  3. Linearized Reed-Solomon or Skew polynomial codes are polynomial codes with

                                                           2              k−1                 k−2
                             f (x, y) = (x, xq y, xq y 1+q , . . . , xq         y 1+q+···+q         )

      where |F| = q m for some m. Note that in general, linearized Reed-Solomon codes can be
      MDS while sharing some of the x, y among various columns. But the GM-MDS theorem
      in [LWWZS23] is only proved when the x, y in each column are different. So the result of
      [LWWZS23] is indeed a special case of our result.

   If a polynomial code f = (f1 , . . . , fk ) is composed of degree at most d polynomials then it is
not hard to show that any particular order ℓ pattern can be achieved by [n, k]-code over a field
of size d(ℓ − 1) + 1 (this does not include the condition that the code is MDS). It simply follows
from showing the individual degree of each variable in the determinant of Lemma 2.9 is at most
d(ℓ−1). As mentioned earlier, for Reed-Solomon codes an MDS code which satisfies a particular zero
pattern can be constructed over a field of size q ≥ n + k − 1 (this is because one could simplify the
determinant in Lemma 2.9 to get better individual degree bounds). In a follow up paper, starting
from a generalization of these theorems we will show how to construct codes which satisfy ‘relaxed’
generic zero patterns over a field of constant size (see end of this section).
   All known examples of codes satisfying GM-MDS theorem such as Reed-Solomon, Gabidulin,
Linearized Reed-Solomon are self-duality property, i.e., their dual codes are also of the same kind. For
example, the dual of a Reed-Solomon code is a again a (generalized) Reed-Solomon code. However,
this is not true for most polynomial codes. And since a code C being LD-MDS(≤ L) (i.e., optimally
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                101



list decodable) is equivalent to the dual code C ⊥ being MDS(L + 1), it is important to know whether
duals of generic polynomials codes are also higher order MDS and hence optimally list-decodable.

Theorem 6.6 ((Informal) Generalized GM-MDS Theorem for Dual Polynomial Codes). Duals of
generic polynomial codes are higher order MDS, i.e., is MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ. As a result, generic
polynomial codes and their duals are MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ and LD-MDS(≤ L) for all L.

Generalized GM-MDS for Irreducible Varieties. We can generalize Theorems 6.4 and 6.6
even further. Instead of specifying an explicit parameterization for f , we instead consider the
columns of our code come from the solutions to an implicit set of polynomial equations (or, more
formally an irreducible variety).
                                                                                                k
Theorem 6.7 ((Informal) Generalized GM-MDS for irreducible varieties). Let X ⊂ F                    be an
irreducible variety which is not contained in any hyperplane. Let v1 , v2 , . . . , vn be generic points
from X. Then the code (n, k)-code C with generator matrix G = [v1 , v2 , . . . , vk ] as well as the dual
code C ⊥ are MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ.
                                                                                                      r
   Theorems 6.4 and 6.6 are a special case of this theorem where the variety is X = {f (x) : x ∈ F }.
Note that any variety which can be parametrized is irreducible and the linear Independence of
f1 , f2 , . . . , fk implies that X is not contained in any hyperplane.
   In a follow up work [BDGZ23], Theorem 6.7 is crucially used to prove that randomly punctured
Algebraic-Geometric codes achieve list decoding capacity over constant field sizes.

Ramsey’s Theorem for Higher Order MDS Codes. What if the MDS code we consider lacks
a specified algebraic structure? Our second main result is a combinatorial application of Theorem 6.4
to general MDS codes. Define a puncturing of a matrix M to be a submatrix formed by some subset
of the columns. In particular, we show that every sufficiently large MDS code has large puncturings
which are MDS(ℓ).

Theorem 6.8 ((Informal) Ramsey’s Theorem for Higher Order MDS Codes). For all n, k, ℓ ≥ 1
there exists N ≥ n such that any (N, k)-MDS code can be punctured to make a (n, k)-MDS(ℓ).

   This result is somewhat surprising when contrasted with the following observation. Consider
an arbitrary matrix M ∈ FN ×k , must M have a large MDS puncturing? Of course not, as every
column of M could lie in the same hyperplane H ⊆ Fk . What Theorem 6.8 shows is that once M
is MDS (i.e., MDS(2)), there are no further “algebraic” barriers for M to have large higher order
MDS puncturings.

The Ultimate GM-MDS Conjecture. A natural extension of Theorem 6.8 would be to show
that a random puncturing of an arbitrarily large MDS code is MDS(ℓ) with high probability.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                               102



Conjecture 6.9 ((Informal) Ultimate GM-MDS Conjecture). For all n, k, ℓ ≥ 1 and ϵ > 0 there ex-
ists N ≥ n such that for any (N, k)-MDS code a random puncturing is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) with probability
at least 1 − ϵ.

   Note that in the case of Reed-Solomon codes (or Gabidulin codes), the analog of Conjecture 6.9
in those situations is already known due to the Schwartz-Zippel lemma [Sch80, Zip79]. However,
proving this result for general MDS codes is much more difficult. In fact, we show that proving
Conjecture 6.9 is equivalent to extending Theorem 6.4 is reducible varieties instead of just irreducible
varieties.

Theorem 6.10 (Informal). Conjecture 6.9 is equivalent to extending Theorem 6.4 to arbitrary (i.e.,
reducible) varieties.

   Precisely stating this conjectured generalization of Theorem 6.4 is rather technical, but the
polynomial analog of the conjecture can be easily stated.

Conjecture 6.11. Let f (1) , . . . , f (n) ∈ (F[x])k be MDS polynomials, then for algebraically indepen-
dent α1 , . . . , αn ∈ F, we have that
                                                                           
                                    (1)       (2)                 (n)
                                   f1 (α1 ) f1 (α2 )      ...    f1 (αn )
                                  f2 (α1 ) f2(2) (α2 )
                                   (1)                           (n)     
                                                         ...    f2 (αn )
                                      ..        ..       ..         ..   
                                  
                                       .         .          .        .   
                                                                          
                                         (1)    (2)               (n)
                                    fk (α1 ) fk (α2 )     ...    fk (αn )

is MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 3.

   We further give some evidence for Conjecture 6.9 by proving it for the cases that ℓ = 3 and
k = ℓ = 4.


6.1.2        Technical Overview
We now outline how each of our main results are proved. First, it is possible to reduce the GM-MDS
theorem for arbitrary irreducible varieties to the case of monomial codes. A monomial code is a
polynomial code as in Definition 6.3 where each fi is a monomial. We first describe an overview of
the proof for monomial codes and then discuss the reduction.

Generalized GM-MDS Theorem for Monomial Codes

We prove Theorem 6.4 by an inductive approach similar to how the original GM-MDS theorem was
proved [Lov18a, YH19b]. We first recall how the GM-MDS theorem is proved.
   The work of [DSY14] showed that the GM-MDS theorem is equivalent to the following fact.
Let A1 , . . . , Ak ⊆ [n] be sets of size k − 1, such that for any J ⊆ [k] nonempty, we have that
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                           103


    T
|       j∈J Aj | ≤ k − |J|.
                        It suffices to show for any sufficiently large field F, (i.e., |F| ≥ n + k − 1),
                                                                             Q
there exists distinct α1 , . . . , αn ∈ F such that the polynomials gi (x) := j∈Ai (x − αj ) are linearly
independent over F.
        The works [Lov18a, YH19b] prove this linear independence by inductively proving a generalized
statement where they allow each polynomial gi to have 0 as a repeated root.2 In particular, for each
i ∈ [k], they keep track of a set Ai ⊆ [n] and σi ≥ 0 such that σi + |Ai | = k − 1. They inductively
show that the polynomials gi (x) := xσi j∈Ai (x − αj ) are linearly dependent over F if and only if
                                         Q

for all J ⊆ [k] nonempty, we have that

                                                      \
                                          min σj +          Aj ≤ k − |J|.                                      (6.4)
                                          j∈J
                                                      j∈J


Outside of simple base cases, the inductive proof splits into three cases (per the convention used by
[YH19b]):

Case 1. There is J ⊆ [k] with 2 ≤ |J| ≤ k − 1 for which (6.4) is tight. Define gJ (x) :=
xminj∈J σj i∈T Aj (x − αi ). The authors use the inductive hypothesis twice: first they argue
          Q
                     j∈J

that span{gi : i ∈ J} = span{xi gJ (x) : i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , |J| − 1}}. They then apply a second use of
the induction hypothesis is to argue that {xi gJ (x) : i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , |J| − 1}} ∪ {gj (x) : j ∈ [k] \ J} is
linearly independent.

Case 2.         There is exactly one i ∈ [ℓ] for which σi = 0. Note that at least one σi = 0 or else
(6.4) fails for J = [k]. Since every gj (x) with j ̸= i is a multiple of x, it suffices to argue that
{gj (x)/x : j ∈ [k] \ i} are linearly independent over F, which follows from the inductive hypothesis.

Case 3.         Any other situation. In this case the authors identify an index j ∈ [n] such that after
assigning αj → 0–that is, if j ∈ Ai , remove j from Ai and increase σi by 1–we have that (6.4) still
holds. After applying the inductive hypothesis, they show algebraically that undoing this operation
preserves the linear independence of the gi .


        For Theorem 6.4, the connection between arbitrarily polynomial codes with the roots of poly-
nomials is less clear. As such we generalize this technique of “repeated roots” within the higher
order MDS framework of [BGM21, Rot21, BGM23]. Theorem 4.11 of Chapter 4 shows that proving
a GM-MDS theorem is equivalent to the following. Let C be our polynomial code with generator
matrix G as in (6.3), we seek to show for any A1 , . . . , Ak ⊆ [n] of size k −1, such that for any J ⊆ [k],
               T
we have that | j∈J Aj | ≤ k − |J|, we have that GA1 ∩ · · · ∩ GAk = 0.
   2 The natural question of allowing each g to have multiple repeated roots is in general open, see [GS19]. One
                                            i
important observation is that the answer to the problem depends on the characteristic of the field, so it cannot have
as simple of an answer as the GM-MDS theorem.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                    104



Higher Order MDS Codes with a Basis.                      The combinatorial structure of the induction is the
same [Lov18a, YH19b], but our key novelty is how we adapt the “repeated root” trick. A first idea
is that when one seeks to substitute αj with 0, we replace each instance of Gj with a fixed vector v.
However, after few substitutions, one quickly runs into a situation in which one wants to make the
same substitution for multiple elements in the same Ai . To get around this, we have ordered set of
vectors u1 , . . . , uk ∈ Fk such that if s vectors in Ai are “set to zero,” then we replace those vectors
with u1 , . . . , us . More formally, we call this a “higher order MDS code with a basis.”

Definition 6.12. Consider n, k, b ≥ 1 with b ≤ k. Let U ∈ Fk×b and V ∈ Fk×n be matrices. We
say that (U, V ) is a (b, n, k)-order-ℓ higher order MDS code with a basis, or (b, n, k)-MDSb(ℓ), if for
all configurations (σ1 , A1 ), . . . , (σℓ , Aℓ ), we have that

                                   ℓ
                                                      !           ℓ
                                                                                       !
                                   \                              \
                            dim        (U[σi ] + VAi )    = dim         (W[σi ] + WA′ i ) ,             (6.5)
                                   i=1                            i=1


where W ∈ Fk×b and W ′ ∈ Fk×n are generic matrices.

    The generalized statement we prove is that that for any code C with a generator matrix like
(6.3), there is a matrix U ∈ Fk×k such that (U, G) is MDSb(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 2.Note by invoking (6.5)
in the case that each σi = 0, we get that G is MDS(ℓ). Once a suitable basis is identified for our
code C, the strategy of [Lov18a, YH19b], can be followed; although it takes some effort to show that
conditions like (6.5) behave analogous to results on repeated roots of polynomials–this groundwork
is laid in Section 6.3.
    In the case of Reed-Solomon codes, we can set U to be the identity matrix; that is, the appropriate
choice of basis is just the standard basis vectors e1 , . . . , ek ∈ Fk . In general for polynomial codes,
the choice of basis depends on the degrees of the nonzero coefficients of f1 , . . . , fk . However, for
“monomial codes”, where each fi (x) = xdi , for some integers d1 < d2 < · · · < dk , we can again use
the standard basis. In Section 6.4 we give all the details of the proof for monomial codes, and in
Section 6.6, we give a reduction from polynomial codes to monomial codes (see also Section 6.1.2).

Generalized GM-MDS Theorem for Duals of Monomial Codes

As explained earlier, except in special cases, a generic polynomial code C does not necessarily have
that the property that its dual code C ⊥ is a generic polynomial code. Therefore, we cannot use
the (exact) same approach to prove that C ⊥ is MDS(ℓ) (and thus C is LD-MDS(≤ ℓ − 1)) for any
generic polynomial code C.
    An approach which one might take is to directly compute a parity check matrix H of C in terms
of the entries of the generator matrix G from (6.3). However, the entries of H depend on the values
of many of the αi ’s and thus make it nearly impossible to directly show that H is MDS(ℓ), even
with a suitable basis.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                           105



    Instead, we show a surprising connection between higher order MDS codes with a basis and dual
codes. In particular, we show that if (GT , In ) is MDSb(ℓ), then C ⊥ is MDS(ℓ) (see Theorem 6.32).
In other words, if we think of the rows of G as a (partial) basis of Fn , it suffices to show that the
criteria of (6.5) hold when the “main” part of the code is the standard basis of Fn .
    Like in the case of Theorem 6.4, we can assume for the proof of Theorem 6.6 that the generator
matrix G of the code C consists of monomials. In this case, we can prove that (GT , In ) is MDSb(ℓ)
using a GM-MDS-style induction (see Theorem 6.30). A crucial detail in the proof is that since the
algebraic complexity of analyzing statements like (6.5) comes from GT , the inductive proof removes
“repeated roots” rather than adding them. In particular, the base case in which each σi = 0 is easy
to analyze due to In being the standard basis. Once the order of induction is properly chosen, the
remainder of the proof of Theorem 6.6 is similar to that of Theorem 6.4.

Extension to Polynomial Codes and Irreducible Varieties

So far, we have only explained the proofs of Theorem 6.4 and Theorem 6.6 for monomial codes, where
each polynomial of (6.3) has a single nonzero coefficient. To extend this result to general polynomials,
we observe that the higher order MDS conditions are equivalent to certain determinants based on
the columns of the generator matrix G being nonzero (see Lemma 2.9). We show that once a suitable
change of basis is performed to G, a leading monomial in the determinant expansion corresponds to
the analogous determinant where each polynomial of G is replaced by its leading monomial. This
only works when the column of the generator matrix comes from the same polynomial family. As
such, the GM-MDS theorems we proved for monomial codes also apply to general polynomial codes.
    We also extend these results to general irreducible varieties. In this case, we use the fact that
any irreducible variety can be (generically) parameterized by a power series, essentially a polynomial
where there are infinitely many terms. These power series can be analyzed essentially like polynomi-
als, in particular they can be reduced to analyzing a suitable monomial code. As such, Theorem 6.7
follows from the proofs of Theorem 6.4 and Theorem 6.6.

Ramsey’s Theorem for Higher Order MDS Codes

We prove Theorem 6.8 by inducting on the structure of the MDS code. Let V be an (N, k)-code we
seek to prove either of the following

 (a) Identify a puncturing of V which is MDS(ℓ).

 (b) Identify an MDS polynomial g : Fk → F such that “many” columns of V are zeroes of g.

    Assume that (a) does not happen. That is, V ′ := V |i1 ,...,in fails to be (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) for all distinct
i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ]. For each n-tuple we can find a witness A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] such that (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) has
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                    106



the dimension-k generic intersection property, but

                                               VA1 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ ̸= 0.

    Note that the number of distinct witness is a function of only the parameters n, k, ℓ. We show
by invoking a particular hypergraph Ramsey theorem (in particular, a generalization of the Korari-
Sos-Turan theorem [KTST54, Zná63]) that if N is arbitrarily large, then there exist arbitrarily large
disjoint sets I1 , . . . , In ⊆ [N ] such that for any i1 ∈ I1 , i2 ∈ I2 , . . . , in ∈ In we have that the tuple
(i1 , . . . , in ) has the same witness (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ). In particular, there exists a polynomial p(x1 , . . . , xn )
such that this polynomial evaluations to 0 for all (x1 , . . . , xN ) such that each xi ∈ V |Ii .
    For each i ∈ [n], let bi = |{j ∈ [ℓ] : i ∈ Aj }|. It is not hard to show that each monomial
of the expanded polynomial p(x1 , . . . , xn ) has “xi degree” of bi . As such, we can define maps
                bi
ϕi : Fk → Fk         which maps (z1 , . . . , zk ) to all k-wise products of terms of zk –this is known as the
                                                                                                b1                  bn
order k Veronese embedding, then there exists a multilinear polynomial q : Fk                        × · · · × Fk        such
that q(ϕ1 (x1 ), . . . , ϕn (xn )) = p(x1 , . . . , xn ) holds as an identity of polynomials.
    For each i ∈ [n], define the variety Yi := {x ∈ Fk : fi (x) ∈ span{fi (v) : v ∈ V |Ii }}. It is not
hard to see that p is identically zero on Y1 × · · · × Yn and V |Ii ⊆ Yi for all I ∈ [n]. Now here’s the
key observation: if Yi = Fk for all i ∈ [n], then we contradict that p is a nonzero polynomial. Thus,
there must be some Yi ̸= Fk . From this, we can find a nontrivial polynomial g : Fk → F such that
g(Yi ) = 0. In particular, g(x) = 0 for all x ∈ V |Ii .
    Once g is identified, we can recurse on V |Ii to either find an MDS(ℓ) puncturing or another
polynomial condition g ′ . Repeating this, we eventually are left with a system of polynomial equations
with (a bounded) number of solutions, which contradicts that N can be made arbitrarily large.

The Ultimate GM-MDS Conjecture

To prove Theorem 6.10, we need to show both that Conjecture 6.9 implies Conjecture 6.11 and vice
versa. The former is quite easy to show, for if we have a counterexample to Conjecture 6.11, we can
make a large number of samples from each polynomial to build a large MDS matrix for which many
puncturings are not MDS(ℓ).
    Conversely, to show that Conjecture 6.11 implies Conjecture 6.9, we adapt the proof of Theo-
rem 6.8. In fact, once we get to the step that there are varieties Y1 × · · · × Yn which always satisfy
the polynomial p, we have (essentially3 ) contradicted Conjecture 6.11. Thus, there could not have
been a witness for any tuple, and in fact there could not have been a witness for 1 − ϵ fraction of
tuples when N is sufficiently large.
    The proofs of special cases of Conjecture 6.11 induct on the size of the witness. In the case of
ℓ = 3, if A1 , A2 , A3 ⊆ [n] are the sets we care about, then there must be i ∈ [n] which is in exactly
   3 More precisely, we contradict the more general statement for varieties.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                            107



one or two of the three sets. If i is in exactly one set, then if we condition on the assignment of every
other vector, we can show that the “bad” choices for i lie in a low-dimensional hyperplane, and so
since our code is MDS only a constant number of columns lie in this subspace. If i is in exactly two
sets, then we can perform a dimension reduction of the code V . This is similar to Case 2 of the
GM-MDS theorem.
    After using the ideas of the k = 3 case, the case of ℓ = 4 and k = 4 reduces top understanding
the configuration where A1 = {1, 2, 3}, A2 = {1, 4, 5}, A3 = {2, 4, 6}, A4 = {3, 5, 6}. In this case,
let v1 , . . . , v6 be the random vectors selected. We show that after we condition on v3 , . . . , v6 , there
are cannot be two choices for v1 and four choices for v2 such that all combinations do not work.
From this, we can use the Korari-Sos-Turan theorem to get a probability bound. We remark this
technique appears quite different from the ideas used to prove the GM-MDS theorem.


6.2       Preliminaries
In this section, we go over some technical background material not explained in the introduction.


6.2.1      Notation
If we have subsets S ⊆ [k] and T ⊆ [n], we let G|S×T denote the submatrix with entries (i, j) ∈ S ×T .
    Given two matrices U and V with the same number of rows, we let [U | V ] denote the matrix
with the columns of U and V concatenated. We let Ik denote the k × k identity matrix. If k is
implicit from context, we write just I (e.g., [I | V ]).
    Given a field F and parameters (n, k), we define a generic (n, k)-matrix as follows. Let Fex be
an extension of F with kn transcendental4 variables {wi,j : (i, j) ∈ [k] × [n]}. Our generic matrix
is W ∈ Fk×n
        ex  where Wi,j = wi,j for all (i, j) ∈ [k] × [n]. Given a polynomial code such as in (6.3),
we say that it is a generic polynomial code if α1 , . . . , αn are transcendental variables in a suitable
extension of F.


6.2.2      Properties of Higher Order MDS Codes
We now state a few properties of higher order MDS codes that did not appear in the introduction.

 (a) In any MDS(ℓ) code V , we have that dim(VA1 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ ) ≤ d.

6.3       Higher Order MDS Codes with a Basis
In this section, we generalize the definition of higher order MDS codes to also allow some vectors to
be chosen in a fixed order from a basis. As mentioned in the technical overview, this will allow us
to substantially generalize the “repeated roots” trick in the proof of the GM-MDS theorem.


6.3.1      Definition
Consider n, k, b ≥ 1 with b ≤ k. Let U ∈ Fk×b and V ∈ Fk×n be matrices. We define an order-ℓ
configuration to be ℓ pairs (σ1 , A1 ), . . . , (σℓ , Aℓ ), where for each i ∈ [ℓ] we have that 0 ≤ σi ≤ b and
Ai ⊆ [n]. For all J ⊆ [ℓ] nonempty, let

                                                   σJ := min σi
                                                         i∈J
                                                         \
                                                   AJ :=     Ai .
                                                            i∈J


Let U≤σ = span{ui : i ∈ [σ]}, and U |≤σ denote the submatrix of the first σ columns of U .

Definition 6.14. Consider n, k, b ≥ 1 with b ≤ k. Let U ∈ Fk×b and V ∈ Fk×n be matrices. We
say that (U, V ) is a (b, n, k)-order-ℓ higher order MDS code with a basis, or (b, n, k)-MDSb(ℓ), if for
all configurations (σ1 , A1 ), . . . , (σℓ , Aℓ ), we have that

                                   ℓ
                                                      !           ℓ
                                                                                      !
                                   \                              \
                           dim         (U≤σi + VAi )      = dim         (W≤σi + WA′ i )   ,              (6.6)
                                  i=1                             i=1


where W ∈ Fk×b and W ′ ∈ Fk×n are generic matrices.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                               109



6.3.2     Comparison to MDS(ℓ) Codes
To help the reader get intuition for Definition 6.12, we compare and contrast with Definition 2.4.
   Like in [BGM21], this definition is monotone in ℓ. That is, if (U, V ) is MDSb(ℓ), then it is
MDSb(ℓ′ ) for all ℓ′ ≤ ℓ. This can be observed by setting some of the pairs (σi , Ai ) in (6.6) equal to
each other (c.f. [BGM21]).
   Another key observation is the following:

Proposition 6.15. If (U, V ) is MDSb(ℓ), then V is MDS(ℓ).

Proof. In (6.6), set σi = 0 for all i ∈ [ℓ], we then derive the condition in Definition 2.4.

   In particular, technique for proving that a code V is MDS(ℓ) is to find a suitable basis U such that
(U, V ) is MDSb(ℓ). This stronger statement can lend itself to an inductive proof, see Section 6.3.4
   However, there are codes for which (U, V ) is MDSb(1) but [U | V ] is not MDS. For instance, let
the ambient field be F2 , have U = I3 , and V be the matrix
                                                         
                                                  1      1
                                                         
                                             V = 1       ,
                                                         1
                                                 
                                                  0      1

then one can verify that (U, V ) is MDSb(1), but [U | V ] is not MDS, as the columns of V and the
third column vector of U are linearly dependent.
   As more nontrivial example, have U = I4 and V ∈ F2×4 be a generic Reed-Solomon code:
                                                            
                                                     1   1
                                                            
                                              α         β
                                           V =              .
                                              α2        β2
                                                            
                                                   α3    β 3



The original proofs of the GM-MDS theorem [Lov18a, YH19b] show that (U, V ) is MDSb(4). How-
ever, [U | V ] is not MDS(4). To see why, let A1 = {1, 2, 5}, A2 = {3, 4, 5}, A3 = {1, 3, 6}, and
A4 = {2, 4, 6}, then one can show

                          [U | V ]A1 ∩ [U | V ]A2 ∩ [U | V ]A3 ∩ [U | V ]A4 ̸= 0,

as can be checked via Lemma 2.6, despite this identity being true for a generic code.
   Although an MDSb(1) code (U, V ) is not necessarily MDS, every subset of the columns of [U | V ]
which shows up in computing (6.6) does behave like an MDS code. This is formalized in the following
proposition.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                         110



Proposition 6.16. Let (U, V ) be a (b, n, k)-MDSb(1) code. For any configuration (σ, A), we have
that
                                          dim(U≤σ + VA ) = min(σ + |A|, k).

Proof. For generic codes, W and W ′ , we must have that dim(U≤σ + VA ) = dim(W≤σ + WA′ ) =
min(σ + |A|, k).

   As a corollary, we have that the ≥ direction of (6.6) follows whenever (U, V ) is MDSb(1) (c.f.,
[BGM21] or Lemma 3.1 of [BGM23]).

6.3.3     Properties of MDSb(ℓ) Codes
In this section, we shall prove various properties about MDSb(ℓ) codes, which shall lead to an
inductive framework for proving that various codes are MDSb(ℓ), see Section 6.3.4.
   5 More formally, the condition that a matrix has rank at least r is equivalent to whether there exists a r × r

submatrix with nonzero determinant. Since the entries of (W, W ′ ) are transcendental, any such determinant in the
block matrix is necessarily nonzero for (W, W ′ ) if it is nonzero for (U, V ).
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                             111



   We say that a configuration (σ1 , A1 ), . . . , (σℓ , Aℓ ) is proper if σi + |Ai | ≤ k for all i ∈ [ℓ]. We
observe that it suffices to check that a code if MDSb(ℓ) by considering only proper configurations.

Proposition 6.18. If (6.6) for all proper configurations, then (6.6) holds for all configurations.

Proof. We prove this by induction on ℓ. The case ℓ = 1 follows from Proposition 6.16 and that
dim(U≤σ + VA ) is monotone in (σ, A). For ℓ ≥ 2, note that if we have a configuration with some
σi + |Ai | > k, then by the induction hypothesis, we have that dim(U≤σi + VAi ) = k, so U≤σi + VAi =
Fk . In particular, we can replace (σi , Ai ) with any (σi′ , A′i ) with σi′ +|Ai | = k and whether (6.6) holds
will not change. We can repeat this procedure until we are left with a proper configuration.

   By adapting the generic intersection dimension formula of [BGM23], we can get an explicit
combinatorial formula for the RHS of (6.6) for proper configurations.

Proposition 6.24. Let X, Y, Y ′ , Y ′′ be linear spaces such that Y ′ ⊆ Y ⊆ Y ′′ and each inclusion has

(Y, Y ′′ )f or(Y ′ , Y )).

these cases follow only from the fact that (U, V ) is MDSb(ℓ − 1).

   We use this inductive template in both Section 6.4 and Section 6.5 to prove various generalizations
of GM-MDS.

6.5     A GM-MDS Theorem for Dual Monomial Codes
The goal of this section is to prove the following result, which serves as an important ingredient for
our more general theorem on LD-MDS(ℓ) codes (Theorem 6.40).

Theorem 6.29. Let e1 < e2 < e3 < · · · < ek be integers. Let α1 , . . . , αn be generic points in an
algebraically closed field F. Then, for all L ≥ 2, the matrix
                                                                               
                                                 α1e1    α2e1   ···      αne1
                                            e2
                                                         α2e2
                                                                              
                                           α1                  ···      αne2 
                                      V :=  .                                                              (6.19)
                                                                             
                                            ..           ..    ..        .. 
                                                          .       .       . 
                                             α1ek        α2ek   ···      αnek



is LD-MDS(≤ L). In other words, the dual matrix V ⊥ is (n, n − k)-MDS(L + 1).

   Note that the special case where each ei = i − 1 corresponds to Reed-Solomon codes. This result
was established in [BGM23] by combining the GM-MDS theorem (Theorem 6.2), Theorem 4.11, and
the fact that the dual of a Reed-Solomon code is a Reed-Solomon code up to column scaling (c.f.,
Proposition 4.4 of [BGM23] or [MS77, Hal]). One would hope that we could use the same proof
strategy, except replacing the GM-MDS theorem with Theorem 6.39. However, the last step that
“the dual of a Reed-Solomon code is a Reed-Solomon code up to column scaling” is false for this
more general family of codes. Hence, we need to find an alternative proof of the GM-MDS theorem
that works with this more general family of dual codes.


6.5.1    Reduction
Our plan for proving Theorem 6.29 is to (non-obviously) reduce the statement to the following fact.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                122



Theorem 6.30. Let V be as in (6.15), then (V T , In ) is MDSb(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 1.

   Rather unintuitively, the basis is the rows of V T and the “normal” part is In . We shall explain
how this structure arises shortly, but first we shall prove the following fact.

Proposition 6.31. Let V be an (n, k)-MDS matrix. Then, V ⊥ is MDS(ℓ) if and only if for any
                                                                          Pℓ
A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] with the dimension n − k null intersection property, i=1 |Ai | = (ℓ − 1)(n − k),

and only if H ⊗ V ⊥ is an (ℓ, n, 1, k)-MR tensor code. More precisely, we have that VA1 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ = 0

Note that every row of (6.21) is a parity-check condition for the code H ⊗ V ⊥ . Further the rows of
(6.21) span the parity check conditions of H ⊗ V ⊥ . Thus, the pattern E is recoverable if and only
if the columns of (6.21) corresponding to E are linearly independent (c.f., [BGM21]). Observe that
(6.21) restrict to the columns indexed by E is precisely (6.20). Thus, (6.20) has rank |E| = n+(ℓ−1)k
if and only if VA1 ∩ · · · ∩ VAℓ = 0, as desired.

submatrix of (6.23) is the transpose of (6.20), we have that V ⊥ is MDS(ℓ) by Proposition 6.31,
where we use that V is MDS.


6.5.2       Proof of Theorem 6.30
In this section, we prove that (V T , In ) is MDSb(ℓ). We proceed by the inductive approach of
Section 6.3.4. However there is a key difference between this proof and the others present so far.
Consider a configuration (σi , Ai , δi ) which occurs in the induction. The GM-MDS theorem (and
Theorem 6.39) seeks to “shrink” the Ai ’s and grow the corresponding σi ’s. In our case, we will
shrink the σi ’s and grow the Ai ’s. In the end, when σi = 0 for all i ∈ [ℓ], we can directly compute
the intersection dimension, as In is just the standard basis and thus is MDS(ℓ).
   Consider a configuration (σ1 , A1 , δ1 ), . . . , (σℓ , Aℓ , δℓ ) satisfying conditions (a*)-(e*) in Section 6.3.4.

expansion of (6.26). If αj ∗ i is achieved, it must be obtained by selecting the αj ∗ i term in the ith

   • For all i ∈ J ∗ replace the column V T |σi in the ith row block, with a k-dimensional indicator
      vector of the j ∗ th row.

By applying a suitable sequences of columns swaps, we can then obtain (6.27). Thus, up to a factor
of ±1, the coefficient of αje∗ in the determinant expansion of (6.26) equals the determinant expansion
of (6.27), as desired.

6.6       Extension to irreducible varieties
6.6.1      Algebraic preliminaries
We first recall some standard facts from algebraic geometry.
    Recall, an ideal I ⊆ F[x1 , . . . , xk ] is said to be radical if f r ∈ I for any r implies f ∈ I.
    For an algebraically closed F, a variety X over Fk is defined as the zero set of a radical ideal
I ⊆ F[x1 , . . . , xk ]. The Zariski topology over X is generated by open sets Uf = {x|f (x) ̸= 0, x ∈ X}
(in other words sets where a polynomial does not vanish) for all f ∈ F[x1 , . . . , xk ].
    A variety X is said to be irreducible if F[x1 , . . . , xn ]/I is an integral domain or equivalently I is
a prime ideal. Topologically, this means that X does not contain two disjoint non-empty open sets.
    Any variety X ⊆ Fk is uniquely a finite union of irreducible components X1 , . . . , Xm . The
uniqueness means that if X can be written as a union of irreducible varieties such that none of them
are contained in the other then they have to be X1 , . . . , Xm . Algebraically speaking there exists
finitely many minimal prime ideals I1 , . . . , Im containing I. Also I1 ∩ . . . ∩ Im = I. Note that the
irreducible components can be of different dimension (for instance take the union of a curve and a
hyperplane not containing the curve).
    A polynomial f is said to be generically non-vanishing on a variety X if the open set Uf =
{x|f (x) ̸= 0, x ∈ X} is dense for the Zariski topology on V . For an irreducible V that simply means
f ̸∈ I. Equivalently, there is at least one point in V on which f does not vanish. For a general X,
it means that f does vanish on each of the irreducible components of X.
    We need a simple fact about the product of two varieties.

Lemma 6.34 (Product of varieties (See 9.4.E in [Vak13])). If X1 ⊆ Fk1 and X2 ⊆ Fk2 are two
varieties then X1 × X2 ⊆ Fk1 +k2 is also a variety. Furthermore, if X1 and X2 are irreducible then
so is X1 × X2 .
    In general if X1 and X2 have irreducible components X1,1 , . . . , X1,m1 and X2,1 , . . . , X2,m2 respec-
tively then X1,i × X2,j , i ∈ [m1 ], j ∈ [m2 ] are the irreducible components of X1 × X2 .

Lemma 6.35. For an algebraically closed F and an irreducible variety X ⊆ Fk , there exists a d
(d will be the dimension of X) such that we can find functions f1 , . . . , fk (depending on X) in the
formal power series ring F[[z1 , . . . , zd ]] such that for any polynomial g ∈ F[x1 , . . . , xk ] over Fk it is
generically non-vanishing over X if and only if g(f1 , . . . , fk ) ∈ F[[z1 , . . . , zd ]] is non-zero.
    We also have that for X n ⊆ Fkn any polynomial g ∈ F[x1,1 , . . . , x1,k , . . . , xn,1 , . . . , xn,k ] is gener-
ically non-vanishing over X n if and only if g(f1 (z1 ), . . . , fk (z1 ), . . . , f1 (zn ), . . . , fk (zn )) is non-zero.

    The above follows from standard facts in algebraic geometry (c.f., [Vak13]). See the appendix of
[BDG23a] for details.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                    129



6.6.2      Algebraic GM-MDS
Definition 6.36 (MDS(ℓ) property for varieties). For an algebraic closed F, we say a variety X ⊆ Fk
satisfies the MDS(ℓ) property if and only if for every k ×n matrix V X with columns of V X initialized
from X, V X is generically a [n, k]-MDS(ℓ) code.
    Concretely, this means for any A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] with |Ai | ≤ k and |A1 | + . . . + |Aℓ | = (ℓ − 1)k,
we have that VA1 ∩ . . . ∩ VAℓ = 0 if and only if
                                                                                      
                                          Ik    V X |A1
                                                                                      
                                      Ik                 V X |A2                      
                                  det  .                                              ,
                                                                                      
                                       ..                          ..
                                                                        .             
                                                                                       
                                        Ik                                   V X |Aℓ

is generically non-vanishing on X (ℓ−1)k , where V is a k × n generic matrix (that is a matrix whose
every entry is an independent formal variable).

    A MDS(2) variety we just call MDS. We also note that being MDS equivalently means that the
determinant of a k × k matrix where each column is a point in Fk is generically non-vanishing over
X k . This allows us to give another simple characterization of MDS varieties.

Theorem 6.37 (MDS varieties are not contained in hyperplanes passing through the origin). A
variety X ⊆ Fk is MDS if and only if each of its irreducible components is not contained in any
hyperplane passing through the origin. In particular, an irreducible variety is MDS if and only if it
is not contained in any hyperplane passing through the origin.

Proof. Let f be the determinant of a k × k matrix M where the ith column is a point (x1,i , . . . , xk,i )
in Fk and X have irreducible components X1 , . . . , Xm . The irreducible components of X k are simply
Xj1 × . . . × Xjk with j1 , . . . , jk ∈ [m].
    Say some irreducible component Xi of X lies in a hyperplane passing through the origin. Then
f vanishes on (Xi )k which means X is not MDS (because M has a kernel corresponding to the
hyperplane).
    Say X is not MDS which means f vanishes on some Y = Xj1 × . . . × Xjk . We now consider
the determinants of the sub-matrices of M . Our goal will be to show some Xji lies in a hyperplane
passing through the origin which would complete the proof.
    If any of the co-ordinate functions (say x1,1 ) (corresponding to size 1 sub-matrices) vanishes on
Y then Xj1 lies in the co-ordinate hyperplane x1,1 = 0. Say the largest-size sub-determinant which
does not vanish on Y has size t > 0. This means the determinant of some t × t sub-matrix M1
of M does not vanish on Y and all the determinants of (t + 1) × (t + 1) sub-matrices of M vanish
on Y . Pick a t + 1 × t + 1 sub-matrix M2 of M containing M1 . Without loss of generality we can
assume the columns of M1 come from Xj1 , . . . , Xjt and the extra column in M2 comes from Xjt+1
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                    130



(the choice of rows is not important). We know that det(M2 ) vanishes on Y . Let R be the index of
rows in M2 . Expanding the determinant of M2 along the t + 1th column will give us
                                                        X
                                          det(M2 ) =          xi,t+1 det(M2′ (i)),
                                                        i∈R


where M2′ (i) is the sub-matrix of M2 obtained by removing the ith row and t + 1the column.
Note, M2′ (t + 1) = M1 which does not vanish on Y . This means it is possible to find points in
y1 ∈ Xj1 , . . . , yt ∈ Xjt such that g = det(M2 )(x1 = y1 , . . . , xt = y2 ) is a non-zero polynomial.
But as det(M2 ) vanishes on Y so does g. But that implies Xjt+1 is contained in g = 0 which is a
hyperplane passing through the origin.

    We also generalize the definition of LD-MDS(≤ L) to the setting of varieties.

Definition 6.38 (LD-MDS(≤ ℓ) property for varieties). For an algebraic closed F, we say a variety
X ⊆ Fk satisfies the LD-MDS(≤ ℓ) property if and only if for ever k × n matrix V X with columns
of V X initialized from X, the dual of V X is generically a [n, n − k]-MDS(ℓ + 1) code.
    Concretely this means that for any A1 , . . . , Aℓ+1 ⊆ [n] with |Ai | ≤ n − k and |A1 | + . . . + |Aℓ+1 | =
ℓ(n − k), we have that VA1 ∩ . . . ∩ VAℓ+1 = 0 if and only if
                                                                                                  
                                   In−k   (V X )⊥ |A1
                                                          (V X )⊥ |A2
                                                                                                  
                              In−k                                                                
                          det  .                                                                  ,
                                                                                                  
                               ..                                        ..
                                                                              .                   
                                                                                                   
                                In−k                                               (V X )⊥ |Aℓ+1

is generically non-vanishing on X ℓ(n−k) where V is a n − k × n generic matrix.

    Our main theorems for varieties are the following.

Theorem 6.39. For an an algebraic closed F if an irreducible X is MDS then it is MDS(ℓ) for all
ℓ > 2.

Theorem 6.40. For an an algebraic closed F if an irreducible X is MDS then it is LD-MDS(≤ ℓ)
for all ℓ > 2.

    The main theorems about power series is the following.

Theorem 6.43. For a tuple F = (f1 , . . . , fk ) ∈ (F[[z1 , . . . , zd ]])k , F is MDS if and only if F is

6.6.4       Reduction of power series to univariate monomials
The goal will be to reduce Theorems 6.39 and 6.40 to equivalent theorems about curves of the form
x → (xd1 , . . . , xdk ), d1 < . . . < dk .

Lemma 6.45. A k-tuple F = (f1 , . . . , fk ) ∈ F[[z1 , . . . , zd ]]k is k-MDS if and only in f1 , . . . , fk are
linearly independent as power series over F.

6.7        Ramsey’s Theorem for Higher Order MDS Codes
As partial evidence of the Ultimate GM-MDS conjecture, we prove that the “Ramsey” version of
the GM-MDS conjecture holds for any sufficiently large MDS code.

Theorem 6.49 (Ramsey’s theorem for higher order MDS codes). For all positive integers n ≥ k ≥
ℓ ≥ 3, there exists a positive integer N ≥ n with the following property. For any (N, k)-MDS code
V there exists a n columns i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ] of V such that V |i1 ,...,in (i.e., the restriction of V onto
columns i1 , . . . , in ) is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ).

Remark 6.50. It is possible to work out an effective bound for N . It appears to be (approximately)
an exponential tower of height 2k.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                           136



6.7.1       Algebraic background
We now state some additional algebraic background.

Definition 6.51. For all k, b ≥ 1, define the (k, b)-Veronese embedding to be the map ϕk,b : Fk →
  k+b−1
F( k ) defined by
                                      ϕk,b (x1 , . . . , xk ){i1 ,...,ib } = xi1 xi2 · · · xib ,

for all multisets {i1 , . . . , ib } ⊆ [k].

   Given a monomial xe11 · · · xenn and a subset I ⊆ [n], we define the total I degree of the monomial
     P
to be i∈I ei .

Proposition 6.52 (folklore). Let p : (Fk )n → F be a multivariate polynomial such that for all
i ∈ [n], every monomial of p has total xi degree of bi . Then, there exists a multilinear polynomial
            k+bi −1
         F( k ) → F such that for all x , . . . , x ∈ Fk , we have that
   Qn
q:    i=1                                             1          n


                                   p(x1 , . . . , xn ) = q(ϕk,b1 (x1 ), . . . , ϕk,bn (xn )).

Proof. Let I1 , . . . , In ⊆ [k] be multisets with |Ii | = bi . Define the coefficient y1,I1 · · · yn,In of
q(y1 , . . . , yn ) to be the coefficient of xI11 · · · xInn in p. The identity then follows from inspection.

Proposition 6.53. Let U1 , . . . , Un be vector spaces over F, and let q : U1 × · · · × Un → F be a
multilinear map. Let S1 ⊆ U1 , . . . , Sn ⊆ Un be sets such that q(S1 , . . . , Sn ) = 0. Then,

                                              q(span S1 , . . . , span Sn ) = 0.

Proof. Consider x1 ∈ span S1 , . . . , xn ∈ span Sn . Each xi can be written as a finite linear combina-
tion of elements of Si . Since q is multilinear, this means that q(x1 , . . . , xn ) can be written as a finite
linear combination of terms of the form q(s1 , . . . , sn ) with (s1 , . . . , sn ) ∈ S1 × · · · × Sn . Since each
q(s1 , . . . , sn ) = 0, we have that q(x1 , . . . , xn ) = 0.

Definition 6.54. Let I ⊆ F[x1 , . . . , xk ] be an ideal generated by polynomials f1 , . . . , fb . Define the
complexity of I to be b + k + maxi∈[b] deg fi , where deg f is the total degree of f . If I has multiple
generating sets, we take the minimum complexity among all such sets. We define the complexity of
a variety to be the minimum complexity of any ideal defining X.

Proposition 6.55. Let X1 , X2 be varieties of complexity at most c1 and c2 , respectively, then
X1 ∩ X2 has complexity at most c1 + c2 .

Proof. Note that the ideal defining X1 ∩ X2 is the sum of the ideals defining X1 and X2 . From this
the result easily follows.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                    137



Theorem 6.56 (Bounding the number of irreducible components of a variety). Let I be an ideal
generated by polynomials f1 , . . . , fr of degree at most d. Then, the variety X defined by the radical
of I will have at most dr many irreducible components.

    The follows inductively using Bezout’s Theorem, Krull’s principal ideal theorem, and a simple
inductive argument. A proof will be presented in the full version of [BDG23c].


6.7.2        Hypergraph Korari-Sos-Turan Theorem
The Korari-Sos-Turan Theorem states that any bipartite graph G which avoids any induced copy of
the complete bipartite graph Ks,t has at most O(n2−1/ min(s,t) ) edges [KTST54, Zná63]. Determining
the optimal exponent for n is a well-studied open question.6 In this section, we prove a suitable
hypergraph generalization of the Korari-Sos-Turan theorem.

Definition 6.57. We say a (directed) hypergraph H is an r-partite r-uniform hypergraph if there
exists a partition S1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Sr of the vertices of H such that every hyperedge (u1 , . . . , ur ) of H has
u1 ∈ S1 , . . . , ur ∈ Sr .

    For notational convenience, we will often identify a hypergraph with its set of hyperedges.

Definition 6.58. Let Htr be the complete r-partite r-uniform hypergraph with vertex set [r] × [t].
The edges of Htr are
                                                   ((1, i1 ), . . . , (r, ir ))

for all i1 , . . . , ir ∈ [t].

Theorem 6.59. For all r, t ≥ 1, there exists c(r, t) = 1/tr−1 and d(r, t) = 2r(t−1) with the following
property. Let H be an r-uniform r-partite hypergraph on vertex set S1 ⊔· · ·⊔Sr with each part having
at most n vertices. If H lacks an induced copy of Htr , then H has at most dnr−c hyperedges.

Proof. We prove this by induction on r. The base case r = 1 is trivial, as H avoiding Ht1 can have
at most t − 1 ‘edges’, so we can set c(1, t) = 1 and d(1, t) = 2(t − 1) ≥ t − 1.
    Pick ϵ = 1/tr−1 = c(r − 1, t)/t. Partition the set Sr into m parts T1 , . . . , Tm each of size at most
nϵ . Note that we can pick m = ⌈n/⌊nϵ ⌋⌉ ≤ 2n1−ϵ . For each I ⊆ [n], let

6.7.3    Proof of Theorem 6.49
We shall prove Theorem 6.49 by induction on a stronger statement.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                          139



Lemma 6.61. For all positive integers n ≥ k ≥ ℓ ≥ 3, r ∈ {0, 1, . . . , k} and c ≥ 1, there exists a
positive integer N (n, k, ℓ, r, c) with the following property. Consider any irreducible variety X ⊆ Fk
of complexity at most c and dimension at most r and any (N, k)-MDS code V such that each column
of V lies in X. Then, there exists a n columns i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ] of V such that V |i1 ,...,in is (n, k)-
MDS(ℓ).

    Note that Theorem 6.49 is immediately implied by taking r = k and X = Fk (and c the
complexity of X). We proceed to prove Lemma 6.61 by induction on r. The base case r = 0 is
vacuously true, as the only irreducible zero-dimensional varieties are single points. Thus, if we select
N = 2, our MDS code cannot be entirely contained in X. This contradicts the assumptions, so the
conclusion vacuously follows.
    Now assume r ≥ 1 and also assume parameters N ≫ N ′ be sufficiently large; we also assume N
is divisible by n. Fix our variety X ⊆ Fk of dimension r with complexity c and our code V . Note
that since N ≫ k, we have that X is MDS by Theorem 6.37 and thus MDS(ℓ) by Theorem 6.7.
Assume for sake of contradiction that all n-puncturings of V are not (n, k)-MDS(ℓ). Partition [N ]
                                                                                         n
into n sets S1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Sn each of size N/n. We can then identify S1 × · · · × Sn with HN/n .
                               n

such that |A1 | + · · · + |Aℓ | = (ℓ − 1)k and (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) has the dimension-k null intersection property.
Note that |Cn,k,ℓ | ≤ 2nℓ . Since each choice of i1 ∈ S1 , . . . , in ∈ Sn has that V |i1 ,...,in in not MDS(ℓ),
we have that there exists a witness (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∈ Cn,k,ℓ for which

variables. Thus, Yi has complexity at most k + k + ℓ. By Proposition 6.55, we have that X ∩ Yi
has complexity at most c′ := k+ℓ
                                   
                                 k   + k + ℓ + c.
   We now have the following two facts

   • p(X ∩ Y1 , . . . , X ∩ Yn ) = 0, and

   • Si′ ⊆ X ∩ Yi for all i ∈ [n].

   Now we break the proof into cases. First, if X ∩ Yi = X for all i ∈ [n], then we have that
p(X, . . . , X) = 0. However, this contradicts the fact that X is an MDS(ℓ) variety. Thus, there
exists i ∈ [n] for which X ∩ Yi ⊊ X. Thus, dim(X ∩ Yi ) ≤ r − 1. Since the complexity of X ∩ Yi
is at most c′ by Theorem 6.56 there exist c′′ (c′ ) and M (c′ ) such that there are at most m ≤ M
irreducible varieties Z1 , . . . , Zm of complexity at most c′′ whose union is X ∩ Yi . Since Si′ ⊆ X ∩ Yi ,
and |Si′ | = N ′ , there exists j ∈ [m] and S ′′ ⊆ Si′ of size at least N ′ /m ≥ N ′ /M such that S ′′ ⊆ Zj .
   By the induction hypothesis, if N ′ /M ≥ N (n, k, ℓ, r − 1, c′′ ), then there exists n elements
i1 , . . . , in ∈ S ′′ such that V |i1 ,...,in is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ). This completes the proof.


6.8      Ultimate GM-MDS Conjecture
We now discuss a couple of generalizations of Theorem 6.4 and Theorem 6.7. In particular, we
consider the following algebraic and combinatorial statements.

Conjecture 6.62 (Generalized GM-MDS for Varieties). Let X be an algebraic variety, then X is
MDS if and only if it is MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ ≥ 3.

Conjecture 6.63 (Generalized Probabilistic GM-MDS). For all positive integers n ≥ k ≥ ℓ ≥ 2
and ϵ > 0, there exists a positive integer N ≥ n with the following property. For any (N, k)-MDS(2)
code V , pick n distinct columns i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ] uniformly at random (without replacement). Then,
V |i1 ,...,in is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) with probability at least 1 − ϵ.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                    141



Remark 6.64. We further conjecture that ϵ = Ok,ℓ (N −1 ) suffices. Via the Schwartz-Zippel lemma,
this is known to hold for algebraic codes.

   We shall show these two conjectures are equivalent.


6.8.1     Equivalence of Conjectures 6.62 and 6.63
Since any variety can be decomposed into a finite union of irreducible varieties, we can rewrite
Conjecture 6.62 as follows.

Definition 6.65. Let X1 , . . . , Xn ⊆ Fk be irreducible algebraic varieties, we say that the list of
varieties (X1 , . . . , Xn ) is M DS(ℓ) if for generically chosen x1 ∈ X1 , . . . , xn ∈ Xn , the matrix with
columns (x1 , . . . , xn ) is (n, k)-MDS(ℓ).

Conjecture 6.66. Let X1 , . . . , Xn ⊆ Fk be irreducible algebraic varieties. We have that (X1 , . . . , Xn )
is MDS if and only if it is MDS(ℓ).

   Note that Conjecture 6.66 is equivalent to Conjecture 6.62 via Theorem 6.56 that any variety
can be decomposed into an (effective) number of irreducible varieties.
   As such, it suffices to prove that Conjecture 6.66 and Conjecture 6.63 are equivalent. We shall
do this by proving two implications. One of these implications is relatively simple to prove.

Lemma 6.67. Conjecture 6.63 implies Conjecture 6.66.

Proof. Fix X1 , . . . , Xn ⊆ Fk . We seek to show that for generic x1 ∈ X1 , . . . , xn ∈ Xn , the matrix
with columns (x1 , . . . , xn ) is MDS(ℓ).
   To do this, we shall invoke Conjecture 6.63 with ϵ = 2n1n . For each i ∈ [n], pick N generic
points vi,1 , . . . , vi,N ∈ Xi . Let V be the matrix with N n columns consisting of all the vi,j ’s. Since
X1 ∪ · · · ∪ Xn is MDS by Theorem 6.37, we have that this matrix V is MDS. By Conjecture 6.63,
a random sample of n columns is MDS(ℓ) with probability 1 − ϵ. Let w1 , . . . , wn be these randomly
chosen columns, and let E1 be the event that these columns form an MDS(ℓ) code. Let E2 be the
event that wi ∈ {vi,1 , . . . , vi,N } for all i ∈ N . Note that Pr[E2 ] ≥ 1/nn . We can then compute that

                                                                       1
                             Pr[E1 ∧ E2 ] ≥ Pr[E1 ] + Pr[E2 ] − 1 ≥       > 0.
                                                                      2nn

Since this probability is nonzero, we must have that for generic x1 ∈ X1 , . . . , xn ∈ Xn , the matrix
with columns (x1 , . . . , xn ) is MDS(ℓ).

   We now show the other direction. This proof is similar to the proof of Theorem 6.49.

Lemma 6.68. Conjecture 6.66 implies Conjecture 6.63.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                       142



Proof. Assume for sake of contradiction that for some (n, k, ℓ, ϵ) we have that for arbitrarily large
N there exists an MDS code V such that if we pick a random n-puncturing of V , the resulting code
is MDS(ℓ) with probability less than 1 − ϵ. In particular, with probability at least ϵ, the code is not
MDS(ℓ). Also designate an arbitrarily large parameter N ′ ≪ N to be determined later; also assume
N is divisible by n.
   Let H be the directed hypergraph on vertex set [N ] for which (i1 , . . . , in ) is a hyperedge if
V |i1 ,...,in is not MDS(ℓ). To each hyperedge we can assign a witness (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∈ Cn,k,ℓ certifying
that V |i1 ,...,in is not MDS(ℓ). Since |Cn,k,ℓ | ≤ 2nℓ , we have that there exists a subgraph H ′ ⊆ H all
of which have a common witness and

                                                    |H|       ϵ  N!
                                       |H ′ | ≥             ≥ nℓ        .
                                                  |Cn,k,ℓ |  2 (N − n)!

   We would now like to invoke Theorem 6.59, but currently H ′ is not n-partite. but H ′ is not
currently bipartite. To fix this, randomly partition [N ] into n groups S1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Sn = [N ], where
each group has size N/n. Let H ′′ = H ′ ∩ (S1 × · · · × Sn ). Note that any edge e ∈ H ′ is included in
H ′′ with probability at least 1/nn . Thus, there exists a choice S1 , . . . , Sn for which

                                                         ϵ           N!
                                           |H ′′ | ≥                        .
                                                       2nℓ nn (N − n)!

By Theorem 6.59, H ′′ has an induced copy of HN
                                              n
                                                ′ , where N
                                                            ′
                                                              is the greatest positive integer for
which
                                                             ′ n−1         ϵ      N!
                              2n(N ′ − 1)(N/n)n−1/(N )               <                   .

Note that N ′ can be made sufficiently large. Let S1′ ⊆ S1 , . . . Sn′ ⊆ Sn be the vertices of the n parts
                 n
of this copy of HN ′ . By the same logic as the proof of theorem 6.49, we can construct varieties

Y1 , . . . , Yn ⊆ Fk such that V |Si′ ⊆ Yi for all i ∈ [n] and (Y1 , . . . , Yn ) fails to be MDS(ℓ).
   Furthermore, these varieties are of bounded complexity, so there exists a constant M and irre-
ducible varieties Zi ⊆ Yi such that at least N ′ /M of the elements of V |Si′ are contained in Zi , but
(Z1 , . . . , Zn ) fails to be MDS(ℓ). As long as N ′ /M ≥ k, by Theorem 6.37, we have that each of
Z1 , . . . , Zn are MDS but Z1 × · · · × Zn are MDS(ℓ).


6.8.2     Proving Special Cases
By applying a suitable union bound, one can show that Conjecture 6.63 is equivalent to the following.
Let Pn,k,ℓ be all ℓ-tuples A1 , . . . , Aℓ ⊆ [n] with each set of size at most k. We let gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ )
denote the dimension-k generic intersection dimension.

Conjecture 6.69. For all positive integers n ≥ k ≥ ℓ ≥ 2 and all (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∈ Pn,k,ℓ , there exists
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                              143



a function ϵ(N ) with limN →∞ ϵ = 0 such that for any (N, k)-MDS code V , we have that

we have that Conjecture 6.69 is true for ℓ = 2 with the function ϵ = 0. This establishes a base case.
    Let Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) be the logical statement that Conjecture 6.69 is true for a particular choice
of (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∈ Pn,k,ℓ . We say that (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) is a reducible pattern if there exists a (finite)

In particular, the statement Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) means that for any (N, k)-MDS code V , the assertion
Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ; V |i1 ,...,in ) holds for most i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ], with probability approaching 1 as N
tends to infinity.

Some Reducible Patterns

Recall that it suffices to assume that A1 , . . . , Aℓ have the k-dimensional null intersection property.
Observe that if there is some i ∈ [n] for which i ̸∈ A1 ∪ · · · ∪ Aℓ , then by suitably permuting the
indices of the Ai ’s we can reduce to some pattern in Pn−1,k,ℓ . Thus, we can always assume that
n = |A1 ∪ · · · ∪ Aℓ |. Further, by definition of Cn,k,ℓ we cannot have any i ∈ [n] appear in every
Aj . Thus, each i ∈ [n] appears in at least 1 but at most ℓ − 1 Aj ’s. We now seek to that these
two extremes are reducible patterns. However, we first observe that we any pattern with generic
intersection dimension greater than 0 is reducible.
Proposition 6.70. Let (A1 , · · · , Aℓ ) ∈ Pn,k,ℓ be a pattern for which gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ≥ 1. Then,
(A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) is a reducible pattern.
Proof. Let d = gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ). From Lemma 2.12, there exists A′1 ⊆ A1 , . . . , A′ℓ ⊆ Aℓ such that
|A1 \ A′1 | + · · · + |Aℓ \ A′ℓ | = d and gidk (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ) = 0. Now assume Qn,k (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ) with a
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                          144



corresponding ϵ(N ). Since, |A1 \ A′1 | + · · · + |Aℓ \ A′ℓ | = d, we have that
                                                                                                    
                                             ℓ                                                     
                                             \                                                  [N ] 
                                  Pr dim        (V |i1 ,...,in )Aj > d i1 , . . . , in ∈
                                             j=1
                                                                                                 n
                                                                                                         
                                                 ℓ                                                      
                                                 \                                                   [N ] 
                                  ≤ Pr dim          (V |i1 ,...,in )A′j > 0 i1 , . . . , in ∈
                                               j=1

                                  ≤ ϵ.

This proves Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ).

Lemma 6.71. Let (A1 , · · · , Aℓ ) ∈ Pn,k,ℓ be a pattern for which for some i ∈ [n], there is exactly
one j ∈ [ℓ] for which i ∈ Aj . Then, (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) is a reducible pattern.

Proof. Assume without loss of generality that 1 ∈ A1 but 1 ̸∈ A2 ∪ · · · ∪ Aℓ . By Proposition 6.70 we
may also assume that gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) = 0.
    Let A′1 = A1 \ {1}. We shall show that

                      Qn−1,k (A′1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∧ Qn−1,k (A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) =⇒ Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ )

    For fixed distinct i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ], let U := V |i1 ,...,in . Also, let W = UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ . Then, note
that

         dim(UA1 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ ) = dim(UA1 ∩ W )
                                         = dim(UA1 ) + dim(W ) − dim(UA1 + W )
                                         = 1 + dim(UA′1 ) + dim(W ) − dim(UA′1 + W ) − 1[U1 ̸∈ UA′1 + W ]
                                         = 1[U1 ∈ UA′1 + W ] + dim(UA′ 1 ∩ W )
                                         = 1[Vi1 ∈ UA′1 + W ] + dim(UA′1 ∩ UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ ).

Note that the intersection dimension condition is 0 for most choices of i2 , . . . , in by assumption of
Qn−1,k (A′1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ ). Thus, it suffices to analyze when Vi1 ∈ UA′1 + W .
    If we know that UA′1 + W ̸= Fk , then since V is MDS, there are at most k − 1 columns of V such
that are contained in UA′1 + W . Thus, in this case 1[Vi1 ∈ UA′1 + W ] = 0 for nearly all choices of
i1 ∈ [N ] \ {i2 , . . . , in }.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                                        145



    However, if UA′1 + W = Fk , then 1[Vi1 ∈ UA′1 + W ] = 1 for all i1 ∈ [N ]. Thus,

                             k = dim(UA′1 + W )
                               = dim(UA′1 ) + dim(W ) − dim(UA′1 ∩ W )
                               = |A′1 | + dim(UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ ) − dim(UA′1 ∩ UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ )

By the assumption Qn−1,k (A′1 , A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) ∧ Qn−1,k (A2 , . . . , Aℓ ), for most choices of i2 , . . . , in ∈ [N ],
we have that the two intersection dimensions above are equal to their generic intersection dimensions.
Thus,
                                     0 = gidk (A′1 , . . . , Aℓ ) = |A′1 | + gidk (A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) − k

However, this means that

                             gid(A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) ≥ |A1 | + gidk (A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) − k = |A1 \ A′1 | = 1,

a contradiction. Thus, we have proved the following fact.

    • UA′1 + W = Fk only if dim(UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ ) ̸= gid(A2 , . . . , Aℓ ) or dim(UA′1 ∩ UA2 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ ) ̸=
        gid(A′1 , . . . , Aℓ ).

    Therefore, we have proved that

      Pr            [¬Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ; V |i1 ,...,in )]

    By our assumptions, we have that this last expression tends to 0 as N → ∞, as desired.

Lemma 6.72. Let (A1 , · · · , Aℓ ) ∈ Pn,k,ℓ be a pattern for which for some i ∈ [n], there is exactly
one j ∈ [ℓ] for which i ̸∈ Aj . Then, (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) is a reducible pattern.

Proof. Note that we may assume that gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) = 0. Further, we can assume each |Ai | ≤
k − 1. Assume without loss of generality that 1 ̸∈ A1 but 1 ∈ A2 ∩ · · · ∩ Aℓ . Let A′i = Ai \ {1} for
all i ∈ [n]. We seek to show that

                                            Qn−1,k−1 (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ) =⇒ Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ).

First, we make the following observation.
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                               146



Proposition 6.73. gidk−1 (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ) = gidk (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ) = 0.

Proof. Consider any partition P1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ Ps = [ℓ]. We have that
                                                                                                              
                                  s
                                  X \                                          s
                                                                               X         \
            −(k − 1)(s − 1) +                A′j = −(k − 1)(s − 1) +                           Aj − 1[1 ̸∈ Pi ]
                                  i=1 j∈Pi                                     i=1       j∈Pi

                                                                       s

so the two generic intersection dimensions are equal by the generic intersection dimension formula
(Theorem 4.14).

    Given a vector v ∈ Fk , let Πv : Fk → Fk−1 be an arbitrarily chosen linear map with ker Πv =
span v (and so by the rank-nullity theorem, im Πv = Fk−1 ). Note that if U is a (n, k)-MDS code
with columns u1 , . . . , un , then Πu1 (u2 ), . . . , Πu1 (un ) form the columns of a (n − 1, k − 1)-MDS code.
We shall denote this code by Π1 (U ).

Proposition 6.74. For any (n, k)-MDS code U , we have that

                          Qn−1,k−1 (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ; Π1 (U )) =⇒ Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ; U ).

Proof. Let W = Π1 (U ). We shall prove the contrapositive. Assume ¬Qn,k (A1 , . . . , Aℓ ; U ). Thus,
there exists a nonzero vector w ∈ UA1 ∩ · · · ∩ UAℓ . Note that since w ∈ UA1 , 1 ̸∈ A1 and |A1 | ≤ k − 1,
we have that u1 ̸∈ UA1 , so Πu1 (w) ̸= 0. Thus, it suffices to show Πu1 (w) ∈ WA′1 ∩ · · · ∩ WA′ℓ . Fix
i ∈ [ℓ], and note that since w ∈ UAi , there exists a function λ : Ai → F such that
                                                         X

                                                        j∈Ai


Since Πu1 is a linear map, we have that
                                            X                          X
                               Πu1 (w) =           λ(j)Πu1 (uj ) =            λ(j)Πu1 (uj ).
                                            j∈Ai                      j∈A′i


Thus, Πu1 (w) ∈ WA′j for all j ∈ [ℓ]. By Proposition 6.73, this proves, ¬Qn−1,k−1 (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ; Π1 (U )).


    To finish, consider distinct i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ]. By Proposition 6.74, we have that

                            ℓ

                                (V |i1 ,...,in )Ai = 0 ⇐⇒          (Πi1 (V )|i2 ,...,in )A′i = 0
