CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                               147



Applying Qn−1,k−1 (A′1 , . . . , A′ℓ ) to the (N −1, k −1)-MDS code Πi1 (V ), we have that the RHS occurs
with probability at least 1−ϵ for a random choice of distinct i2 , . . . , in ∈ [N ]\{i1 }. Thus, for random
choice of distinct i1 , . . . , in ∈ [N ], the LHS holds with probability at least 1 − ϵ, as desired.

    From Lemma 6.71 and Lemma 6.72, we can immediately deduce that Conjecture 6.63 is true
when ℓ = 3.

Theorem 6.75. Conjecture 6.63 is true for ℓ = 3.

Proof. Every configuration (A1 , A2 , A3 ) ∈ Pn,k,3 is reducible via either Lemma 6.71 and Lemma 6.72.



Observations about the ℓ = 4 Case

We now move on to describing the challenges in understanding the ℓ = 4 case. Based on Lemma 6.71
and Lemma 6.72, it suffices to look consider tuples (A1 , A2 , A3 , A4 ) such that each i ∈ [n] appears
in exactly two sets. In particular, we can partition [n] into six sets Bij = Bji for 1 ≤ i < j ≤ 4 such
that
                                                           [
                                                  Ai =               Bij .
                                                         j∈[4]\{i}

We leave resolving such configurations in general as an open question, but we do consider the special
case in which k = ℓ = 4 (and so n = 6). This splits into two cases, either |Bij | ≥ 2 for some i < j or
|Bij | = 1 for all i and j. In the former case, since each Ai has cardinality at most 3, we have that
VAi ∩ VAj = VBij for any MDS code V . This implies that any such case is reducible. This logic is
similar to Case 1 of the proof of the GM-MDS theorem.
    The latter case is much more difficult–we prove it in Theorem 6.76. We remark that this case
does not seem to be an adaptation of the techniques which prove the GM-MDS theorem.

Theorem 6.76. Let n = 6, k = 4, ℓ = 4, A1 = {1, 2, 3}, A2 = {1, 4, 5}, A3 = {2, 4, 6}, and A4 =
{3, 5, 6}. Then, Q6,4 (A1 , . . . , A4 ) holds.

Remark 6.77. One can visualize this configuration in projective 3-space. Think of points p1 , . . . , p6
defining the vertices of an octahedron, with pi and pj antipodal if i + j = 7. This octahedron has 8
2-dimensional faces. Two-color these faces such that no two faces of the same color share an edge.
MDS(4) condition then says there does not exist a point lying on the (extended) planes through the
four faces of one color.

Proof. Recall that we seek to show there exists a function ϵ(N ) tending to 0 as N tends to infinity
such that for any (N, 4)-MDS matrix V and for random distinct i1 , . . . , i6 ∈ [N ], we have that
Q6,4 (A1 , . . . , A4 ; V |i1 ,...,i6 ) holds with probability at least 1 − ϵ. In our proof, we fix the values of
i3 , . . . , i6 . Since the property Q6,4 (A1 , . . . , A4 ; V |i1 ,...,i6 ) is invariant under multiplying V on the left
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                                                          148



by an invertible matrix in F4×4 , we may assume without loss of generality that Vi3 = e1 , . . . , Vi6 = e4 ,
where e1 , . . . , e4 are the standard basis of F4 . Thus, we seek to verify for random distinct i1 , i2 ∈
[N ] \ {i3 , . . . , i6 } that with probability at most ϵ, we have that

           span{Vi1 , Vi2 , e1 } ∩ span{Vi1 , e2 , e3 } ∩ span{Vi2 , e2 , e4 } ∩ span{e1 , e3 , e4 } ̸= 0.                  (6.33)

For now, we also fix i1 and i2 , and write Vi1 = (x1 , x2 , x3 , x4 ) and Vi2 = (y1 , y2 , y3 , y4 ). Evaluating
the determinant condition equivalent to (6.33), we get the polynomial expression:

                                                                                                    ?
                          p(x, y) := x1 x2 y3 y4 − x1 y2 y3 x4 − y1 x2 y3 x4 + y1 y2 x3 x4 = 0.

Thus, it suffices to show for randomly chosen distinct i1 , i2 ∈ [N ] \ {i3 , . . . , i6 } we have that
p(Vi1 , Vi2 ) = 0 with probability at most ϵ. The following lemma will do most of the job.

Lemma 6.78. Let x(1) , x(2) , y (1) , . . . , y (4) ∈ F4 be vectors such that (e1 , . . . , e4 , x(1) , x(2) , y (1) , . . . , y (4) )
is a (10, 4)-MDS code. Then, there exist i ∈ [2] and j ∈ [4] such that p(x(i) , y (j) ) = 0.

Proof. Assume for sake of contradiction that such vectors exist. Consider the polynomial

                                       q(x, x′ , y) = x′3 x′4 p(x, y) − x3 x4 p(x′ , y)
                                                    = y3 [(x′2 x3 x4 x′4 − x2 x′3 x4 x′4 )y1
                                                       + (x′1 x3 x4 x′4 − x1 x′3 x4 x′4 )y2
                                                       + (x1 x2 x′3 x′4 − x′1 x′2 x3 x4 )y4 ]

Note that q(x(1) , x(2) , y (j) ) = 0 for all j ∈ [4]. Further, since the vectors y (j) , e1 , e2 , e4 are linearly
                                     (j)
independent, we have that y3 ̸= 0 for all j ∈ [4]. Thus, we have that for all j ∈ [4], we have that

                                             (2) (1) (1) (2)        (1) (2) (1) (2)       (j)
                                    0 = (x2 x3 x4 x4 − x2 x3 x4 x4 )y1
                                               (2) (1) (1) (2)          (1) (2) (1) (2)       (j)
                                           + (x1 x3 x4 x4 − x1 x3 x4 x4 )y2
                                               (1) (1) (2) (2)          (2) (2) (1) (1)       (j)
                                           + (x1 x2 x3 x4 − x1 x2 x3 x4 )y4

This equation is linear in y (j) , so since the y (j) ’s are linearly independent, we must have each of the
coefficients is equal to 0. In particular, from the first coefficient we derive the identity

                                                (2) (1)       (1) (2)    (1) (2)
                                             (x1 x3 − x1 x3 )x4 x4 = 0

Each of these factors is nonzero because each of the four-tuples (x(1) , x(2) , e2 , e4 ), (x(1) , e1 , e2 , e3 ),
(x(2) , e1 , e2 , e3 ) are linearly independent. Thus, we have found a contradiction.

    To finish, consider the following sampling algorithm for i1 and i2 ,
CHAPTER 6. GENERALIZED GM-MDS THEOREM                                                               149



   • Randomly partition S1 ⊔ S2 = [N ] \ {i3 , . . . , i6 } into two sets of size ≈ (N − 4)/2.

   • Randomly pick i1 ∈ S1 and i2 ∈ S2 .

By inspection this algorithm picks a uniformly random sample. It suffices to show that p(Vi1 , Vi2 ) = 0
occurs with probability at most ϵ when i1 = i2 .
   Let E be the bipartite graph on S1 ⊔ S2 where (i, j) is an edge iff p(Vi , Vj ) = 0. By Lemma 6.78,
this graph E has no K2,4 subgraph. Thus, by the Korari-Sos-Turan theorem, we have that E has at
most O(N 3/2 ) edges. Thus, averaging over the random choice of S1 ⊔ S2 , we can pick ϵ = O(N −1/2 ),
which tends to 0 as N → ∞.

Remark 6.79. We leave as an open question whether ϵ = O(N −1 ) can be chosen. If not, this
shows a fundamental departure from the probabilistic interpretation of the GM-MDS theorem, where
all failure probabilities are O(N −1 ) due to the Schwartz-Zippel lemma.

Remark 6.80. The choice of fixing the indices i3 , . . . , i6 was important. If instead we would have
set Vi2 = e1 , . . . , Vi5 = e4 , then the polynomial condition becomes

                                     p(x, y) = x1 y2 y3 x4 − y1 x2 x3 y4

There do exist arbitrarily large MDS codes V for which p(x, y) = 0 for all distinct pairs of columns
of V . This include the Reed-Solomon code with columns (1, α, α2 , α3 ). Thus, even generalizing
Theorem 6.76 to the full ℓ = 4 case may require a careful induction.

   • Future directions for research, including a number of open questions.

Sources:    The material is adapted from the open problem and subsequent work sections of [BGM21,
BGM22, BDG23b, BDG23a].


   In this thesis, we have explored the theory of higher order MDS codes, and the impact that they
already have had on coding theory. By generalizing a geometric definition of the MDS codes in
the appropriate manner, higher order MDS codes serve as a hierarchy of further properties beyond
MDS. In particular, higher order MDS codes capture an important special cases of the larger family
of MR Tensor Codes. They also serve to give a unified framework for understanding theories such
as the GM-MDS theorem and the study of capacity-achieving codes for list-decoding. Further,
understanding the optimal field size of higher order MDS is an important topic of inquiry which
could have real-world implications. With all of these rich connections, there are a number of exciting
avenues which warrant further exploration. We list a few of these directions.

The limits of the GM-MDS theorem. As discussed in Chapters 4 and 6, the GM-MDS the-
orem and its generalizations are a powerful tool for describing which codes are higher order MDS
codes. The vaster these generalizations become, it appears that being a higher order MDS code is
a “common” property. In particular, one could expect the following is true:

Conjecture 7.1 (Ultimate GM-MDS conjecture, restating Conjecture 6.63). Let C0 be any (N, k)-
code which is MDS. Let C be an (n, k)-code obtained by a random puncturing of C0 (i.e., its generator



                                                 150
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS                                                                             151



is formed by randomly selecting n columns from the generator of C0 ). Then as with probability
1 − oN (1), C is MDS(ℓ) for all ℓ.

   As proved in Chapter 6, this is equivalent to generalizing Theorems 6.4 and 6.6 to the setting
where each column of our code is generic evaluations of different polynomial maps.
   That said having each column of the generator matrix be “independent” of the other columns
greatly restricts the space of matrices. Do there exist generalized GM-MDS theorems where the
polynomials defining the code share variables across rows and columns (c.f., Example 6.5)? One
hint that such a generalization should exist is that our generalized GM-MDS theorems apply to
both polynomial codes and their duals. Naively, the generator matrix of the dual of a polynomial
code consists of many entries which share input variables.
   One may also ask what implications these generalized GM-MDS theorems have in the broader
field of algebraic geometry. For instance, in a recent work [BELL23], we resolved an open question of
Silversmith [Sil22] on the intersection theory of moduli spaces (i.e., points on a Euclidean sphere up
to a Möbius transform) by reducing the question to the GM-MDS theorem. See a more recent work
of Silversmith [Sil23] for a broader discussion of this area. Conversely, perhaps some of the standard
tools used to study these questions could yield interesting new theorems about higher order MDS
codes.

Even better field size bounds for higher order MDS codes.                Although progress on under-
standing generic constructions of higher order MDS codes has greatly improved, there is much to be
done to understand for which finite fields higher order MDS codes can be constructed.
   An improved upper bound on the field size of (n, k)-MDS(ℓ) codes was proved by [ACKL23] for
some choices of n, k, ℓ (in particular when k ≪ℓ n). However, in the regime where k = Rn, where
R ∈ (0, 1) is a constant. The currently best lower bound on the field size is still approximately
exp(Ω(n)), whereas the best upper bound is approximately exp(O(ℓn)). It is currently unclear
which bound (if either) is the correct answer, and finding the asymptotically optimal field size may
require resolving some nontrivial questions in additive combinatorics.
   As we shall see in a later section, this gap of ℓ in the exponent appears to correspond to a
long-standing open question in coding theory (the optimal field size of codes achieving list-decoding
capacity).

Constructions of higher order MDS codes.             Despite knowing that many generic polynomial
codes are higher order MDS, we do not know of any explicit constructions of such higher order MDS
codes which have better than doubly exponential field size.
   As previously mentioned, Theorem 4.5 implies that MDS(ℓ) codes exist over fields of size at most
Ok,ℓ (nmin{k,n−k}ℓ ). Conversely, MDS(ℓ) codes (with ℓ ≥ 3) require a field of size Ωk (nmin(k,n−k)−1 )
(see Theorem 5.8). The simplest non-trivial case is when k = 3, ℓ = 3. The lower bound implies
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS                                                                              152



that, we need field size at least q = Ω(n2 ) in this case.

Question 7.2. Do there exist explicit MDS(ℓ) codes of length n over fields of size at most nOℓ (min(k,n−k)) ?
More concretely, can we construct an explicit MDS(3) (n, 3)-code over a field of size O(n2 )?

   Constructing such MDS(ℓ) codes would yield the first “truly” explicit family of linear codes
achieving list decoding capacity. See Chapter 5 for the current state-of-the-art of constructions of
higher order MDS codes, including an O(n3 ) construction for (n, 3)-MDS(3).

Maximally recoverable tensor codes when a, b ≥ 2. We saw that MDS(ℓ) codes arise naturally
from studying (m, n, a, b)-MR tensor codes when a = 1. It would be interesting to study, what
properties of the row and column codes would be needed to construct MR tensor codes for a, b ≥ 2.
   As previously mentioned, the work of [GHK+ 17a] fully characterized the correctable erasure
patterns for a (m, n, a, b) tensor code when a = 1. Theorem 4.14, when combined with the results
of [BGM21], fully characterizes the linearly independent patterns when a = 1. We hope that these
results lead to insights which resolve question of characterizing correctable erasure patterns in the
general case. More precisely,

Question 7.3. Given generic vectors u1 , . . . , um ∈ Fm−a and v1 , . . . , vn ∈ Fn−b , for which E ⊆
[m] × [n] is {ui ⊗ vj : (i, j) ∈ E} of full rank? For which E are they linearly independent?

   We further hope that this characterization can be made tractable.

Question 7.4. Is there a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for detecting which erasure pat-
terns of an (m, n, a, b)-MR tensor code are correctable when a, b > 1?

   Currently, there is no “simple” condition like that of regularity and no efficient deterministic
algorithm which is known for testing correctability when a, b > 1. See Section 3.4 for a conjecture
which could lead to progress for the a = 2 case. A concrete stepping stone toward these questions
would be resolve the “disjoint rectangle” case.

Question 7.5. Let X1 , . . . , Xk ⊆ Fm−a and Y1 , . . . , Yk ⊆ Fn−b be linear subspaces in general posi-
tion. What is a combinatorial formula for

                                                    k
                                                                    !
                                                    X
                                             dim          Xi ⊗ Yi
                                                    i=1


in terms of m − a, n − b, dim(X1 ), . . . , dim(Xk ), dim(Y1 ), . . . , dim(Yk )?

   Another natural question to ask is whether the tensor product of two generic Reed-Solomon
codes is also MR. However, given how the results like the GM-MDS theorem are proved, it is quite
unlikely that such a question can be answered without first adequately resolving Question 7.3.
CHAPTER 7. CONCLUSIONS                                                                              153



Efficient list-decoding of LD-MDS(≤ L) codes. As previously mentioned in Chapter 4, the
result of Guruswami-Sudan [GS98] shows that any (n, k)-Reed-Solomon code of rate R can be
                                             √
efficiently list-decoded up to radius ρ = 1 − R. A hardness result by Cheng and Wan [CW07]
states that it is discrete-logarithm-hard to decode up to radius ρ̂ := 1 − ĝ/n, where
                                                           
                                                 n
                                    ĝ = min g :   |F|k−g ≤ 1
                                                 g

                                                                                         n−2
                                                                                             
However, this result only applies for small field sizes. Since we now know that |F| ≥    k−1 , we have
that ĝ = k + Θ(1), which is essentially the list-decoding capacity bound of radius 1 − R. On the
other hand, LD-MDS(≤ L) are list decodable only up to list-decoding radius ρ = 1 − R − 1−R
                                                                                       L+1 with
list size L. Thus, we believe the following is open in general.

Question 7.6. Assume C is a (n, k)-Reed-Solomon code which is LD-MDS(≤ L). Given y ∈ Fn ,
                                                                 L
can one efficiently list all c ∈ C with distance from y at most L+1 (n − k)?

Relaxations of higher order MDS Codes              Recall that even though higher order MDS codes
have optimal (average-radius) list-decoding guarantees, the property of achieving list-decoding ca-
pacity is a far weaker condition. That said, a number of connections between higher order MDS
codes and suitable “relaxations” of these codes have been recently discovered.
   Theorems 5.8 and 5.10 were generalized by [AGL23a] to show that any code, linear or non-linear,
which is ϵ-close to achieving list-decoding capacity requires a field size of at least exp(Ω(1/ϵ)). They
prove this result by extending the techniques of this paper. Note that this lower bound is tight for
non-linear codes. However, for linear codes the currently best constructions [AGL23b, BDGZ23]
require a field size of exp(O(1/ϵ2 )). This discrepancy is precisely because of the exp(Ω(n)) versus
exp(O(ℓn)) gap for (normal) higher order MDS codes.
   In another line of work, Theorem 5.10 has been circumvented entirely [GZ23, AGL23b, BDGZ23]
by considering suitable relaxations of higher order MDS codes in order to construct linear codes which
achieve (average-radius) list-decoding capacity over polynomial-sized or constant-sized fields. Each
of these constructions takes a suitable (generic) higher order MDS code, but plugs in random values
over a small field. The analysis uses the fact that because the generic objects are higher order MDS
codes these random matrices look “close enough” to higher order MDS codes to still achieve capacity
(or in the case of [BDGZ23], “relaxed MR”).
   Perhaps the techniques developed in these papers could be used to construct explicit linear codes
over constant-sized fields which asymptotically achieve list-decoding capacity.
